6 The World Ahead 2027
M
ODERN LIFE can feel like
being in a sci-ﬁ story. A
rocket mogul who wants to put
computers in space became the
world’s ﬁrst trillionaire. Drones
have redeﬁned warfare. Taxis
drive themselves. Artiﬁcial-
intelligence models are going
rogue. Humanoid robots are on
the march. There are injections
that make you thinner and
healthier—if you can afford
them. All that is missing is the
aliens. They probably won’t
appear in 2027. But here are ten

other things to watch out for.
1. The post-Trump era.
As the dust settles from the
midterms, the race to succeed
Donald Trump will begin. But
he won’t be a lame duck—more
like a destructive Angry Bird.
Expect more executive orders,
legal spats, sudden renamings,
grandiose building projects
and military adventures as he
furiously tries to retain his
inﬂuence and shape his legacy.
Meanwhile, the world will start
to wonder what a post-Trump
America might look like.

2. Aftershocks from Iran.
A clean-cut resolution to the
conﬂict seems unlikely, so
uncertainty will persist. But Mr
Trump’s ill-advised war has
reduced America’s stature; its
allies no longer trust it and its
enemies no longer fear it. The
war’s consequences will ripple
throughout 2027, reshaping
regional politics, reconﬁguring
energy ﬂows and threatening
food security, with fertiliser in
short supply. In Iran itself, the
regime will tighten its grip.

3. Hottest year ever, again.
2027 is expected to be the
hottest year on record. A “super
El Niño” will offer a preview of
the warmer, more volatile
future that awaits, with more
droughts, heatwaves, wildﬁres
and extreme weather, unless
emissions are cut quickly. But
Mr Trump has unwittingly
become the world’s greatest
salesman for renewable energy.
It’s the best way not just to
address climate change, but to
escape from the ups and downs
of global energy markets.

4. The AI backlash grows.
The risks of AI are under
scrutiny. The danger of a crash
will rise as AI ﬁrms take on
more debt in complex, opaque
deals. Whatever happens in
markets, more capable models
will power a spate of cyber-
attacks. Companies and
governments will worry about
political risks to AI supply
chains. But concerns about job
losses, or the energy and water
use of data centres, are not
backed by data. The backlash
reﬂects fears over imagined

impacts, not real ones.
5. Europe’s populist right.
Right-wing populism has been
on the rise around the world for
some time. But in 2027 all eyes
will be on France, where
Marine Le Pen of the populist-
right National Rally has a good
chance of winning the
presidency. That would throw a
spanner into the Franco-
German engine that powers
the European Union—and
heighten concerns about
debt-laden France’s precarious
public ﬁnances.
6. Bond-market pressure.

But it’s not just France that has
the bond markets on edge.
Fiscal proﬂigacy, the AI
construction boom and
renewed inﬂation (from higher
energy prices) are raising
governments’ borrowing costs
across the rich world. Higher
rates are squeezing companies
and households, too. This can’t
go on for ever. Something,
somewhere, has to break.
7. Taiwan’s deadline.
Technology has changed the
nature of war, making it easier
for smaller powers to fend off
larger ones, as seen in Ukraine

and Iran. What does that mean
for China’s designs on Taiwan?
Xi Jinping is said to have set a
deadline of 2027 for China’s
armed forces to be ready to
capture the island. America is
distracted. Will China make a
move, or await the outcome of
Taiwan’s elections in 2028? The
next two years will be perilous.
8. The Ozempic effect.
As well as helping people lose
weight, slimming drugs are
changing consumer behaviour,
as the newly slender, granted a
new lease of life, engage in

“revenge shopping”, splurging
on clothes, cosmetics and
ﬁtness. The drugs’ success has
also rejuvenated interest
among pharmaceutical
companies in therapies to
boost longevity.
9. Cheaper space launch.
SpaceX’s huge Starship will
start making regular satellite
launches. Chinese rocketry
ﬁrms will demonstrate reuse of
boosters. Cheaper launch will
pave the way for private space
stations and, yes, building data
centres in orbit, which may not
be as crazy as it seems.

10. Rebooting Hollywood.
“Star Wars” is re-released for its
50th anniversary, and a new
ﬁlm aims to reboot the
franchise. The Avengers return,
again, and a new James Bond is
on the way. Given the success
of low-budget movies made by
YouTubers, though, do
audiences want something
new? Perhaps. Yet with its
depiction of a military
superpower humbled by small,
cheap craft, misbehaving
droids with their own agendas,
and its warning of the conse-
quences of lax security and

stolen data, “Star Wars” may be
more relevant than ever in 2027.
It seems reality has caught
up with sci-ﬁ. We hope you will
ﬁnd The World Ahead 2027 to
be a helpful and entertaining
guide to this fast-moving,
uncertain future. ●
From the editor
It may be 50 years
old, but “Star Wars”
seems more relevant
than ever in 2027
The World Ahead 2027
Tom Standage
Editor, The World Ahead 2027

### Page 9

9The World Ahead 2027
Leaders
W
ITHIN WEEKS of each other, two events in late
2026 shone a spotlight on the forces that will
shape 2027. One, the stunning scale of President
Donald Trump’s repudiation in America’s midterms
on November 3rd, was headline news around the
world. The other—an announcement that OpenAI’s
most powerful (and unreleased) model had cracked
more than 370 hitherto unsolved maths problems—
blindsided geeks but was little noticed elsewhere.

The midterms were the biggest wave-vote in half a
century and the beginning of the end of Trumpism.
The world’s most important democracy, long enfee-
bled by polarisation and presidential bullying, proved
vital and resilient. Furious about an unpopular war and
costly fuel, voters pushed back against corruption and
demanded change. When Democrats win in Texas
and Kansas for the ﬁrst time in generations, the prism
of the possible in American politics has widened.

Yet the “mathpocalypse”, and the power of AI it ex-
empliﬁes, may say more about what lies ahead. Big po-
litical change is feasible in the coming year; but AI-led
upheaval is certain, and on a scale that few outside Sil-
icon Valley have yet got their head around. The theo-
rem-busting breakthrough shows what happens when
improvements in AI models reach the steepening part
of an exponential curve. The unimaginable suddenly
becomes the ubiquitous. In 2027 that will happen far

beyond maths and coding. Expect striking scientiﬁc
breakthroughs: diseases better understood and new
materials discovered, all at a jaw-dropping pace.
People will begin to see the impact of AI more tan-
gibly as personal AI agents become the norm, driver-
less cars appear in more cities and humanoid robots
make their debut. There won’t be a sudden wipe-out of
white-collar work, as the doomiest seers still predict,
but there will be greater disruption, as more ﬁrms in

more industries reinvent the way they are organised.
AI will become scarier, too. Swarms of autonomous
drones will be used on Ukraine’s battleﬁelds.
Cyber-attacks, already surging, will become more fre-
quent and more damaging. Expect an AI incident that
costs lives somewhere in the coming year.
The big question for 2027 is how these two forces,
AI and the decline of Trumpism, will interact. There
could be a costly clash. Americans are fearful and

deeply suspicious of AI. Many Democrats put opposi-
tion to data centres and tech oligarchs at the centre of
their campaigns. The midterm elections included 45
state and local ballots on restricting or banning data
centres; 44 of them passed. Gina Hinojosa, the new
governor of Texas (home to a third of America’s
planned data centres) has promised a moratorium.
More likely is a new bargain: states and local govern-
ments will demand more money and local beneﬁts in

return for hosting data centres.
At the federal level, more regulation of AI is now in-
evitable. The new Democratic-controlled Congress
will push hard for rules and oversight, especially
around safety, something several AI bosses have long
called for and which even some officials within the ad-
ministration quietly applaud. Numerous blueprints
exist and something will become law soon. 2027 will
mark the end of America’s “let it rip” attitude to AI.

This shift will not dramatically change AI’s trajec-
tory. Stopping the technology is impossible, even if it
were a good idea. But thoughtful regulation will, with
luck, ensure that the emergence of super-powerful in-
telligence is slightly slower and somewhat safer. That
will puncture the ﬁnancial bubble built around pell-
mell growth in the riskiest structures. It could also
tweak the business model for leading AI labs. Rather
than releasing their most powerful models, they might

keep them in house, building science labs or trading
ﬂoors to reap the rewards of scientiﬁc breakthroughs
or ﬁnancial insights themselves, without liability risks.
Ideally, 2027 would also bring some basic co-oper-
ation on safety with China. Chinese “open-weight”
models lag America’s, but not by much. And 2027 will
see remarkable capability gains there, too. In a saner
world the two countries would set some minimal rules,
as geopolitical adversaries have done before. Alas, that

won’t happen. Although China cares more, and is do-
ing more, about AI safety than many Americans real-
ise, the mutual suspicions of two superpowers, which
Hold on tight

the rise of AI
and the decline

▸ both (rightly) see the technology as the key to
21st-century success, run deep. The midterm political
revolution will not extend to rethinking AI with China.
AI safety may get mentioned when Presidents Trump
and Xi meet, but to little effect.
Elsewhere, though, the election results could affect
geopolitics more than midterms usually do. Foreign
policy is largely the purview of America’s president and
is thus where lame-duck presidents generally focus,

often to dramatic effect. George W. Bush ordered the
Iraq surge after his midterm defeat in 2006; Barack
Obama negotiated the Iran nuclear deal after his loss in
2014. Mr Trump could respond to his domestic
drubbing with an even more aggressive foreign agenda:
more bullying, more military adventurism, more tariffs.
But the size of the Democratic majorities suggests
Congress won’t be entirely supine. Mr Trump could
order new attacks on Iran, but Congress will be loth to

fund a prolonged military campaign. It could also balk
at any agreement that sells out Ukraine, especially if
business side-deals with Russia are involved. And if Mr
Trump goes tariff-crazy, Congress might even vote to
take back control there.
The more likely outcome is, therefore, that Mr
Trump’s foreign policy is superﬁcially unchanged, but
less consequential as the rest of the world starts to look
beyond his presidency. Meetings with Presidents Xi

and Putin will be more photo-op than a modern
Potsdam. Expect ﬂashy trips (to North Korea, perhaps),
but no ground-breaking deals. Some will lose from this
shift (notably Israel). Most countries will be relieved.
The big outlier in 2027 will be Europe. Ironically, just
as Trumpism has been repudiated in America, several
European countries may embrace the populist right.
Marine Le Pen could become France’s president in May
with dramatic consequences for Ukraine, ﬁnancial

markets and the European Union itself.
European money is keeping Ukraine aﬂoat. If those
funds are cut off, as Ms Le Pen and others on Europe’s
populist right propose, it will collapse. With luck a
President Le Pen would quickly realise that a failed
state on Europe’s eastern ﬂank is not a good idea. And
there is a safety-valve. Rather than taxpayer funds,
expect European countries to ﬁnally agree to seize
frozen Russian assets to pay Ukraine’s bills in 2027.

Europe will be the focus of bond-market jitters.
France could face a full-blown crisis as the election
nears: its public ﬁnances are already unsustainable.
And the policies that European right-wing populists
promise will not just cause economic harm, but could
spell the end of the EU. But this being Europe, the
reality, even if Ms Le Pen wins, is likely to be less
dramatic. Crisis and muddle-through is more likely
than EU collapse. But the opportunity cost will be high.

Europe is a striking laggard in the era of AI. It has no
frontier models and only 5% of global AI computing
capacity. That won’t save it from AI’s risks, but it does
mean Europe will fail to reap the beneﬁts. In a year
when American politics could shape the AI revolution
for the better, Europe’s potential political earthquake
means it risks being permanently left behind. ●
Zanny Minton Beddoes Editor-in-chief, The Economist
Drawing on 2027
C002

### Page 11

11The World Ahead 2027 Leaders
W
ORLD LEADERS know that the old global order is
dying. In 2027 they will, for the ﬁrst time, grasp
the weirdness of the new reality that is replacing it. As
surely as Einstein’s general relativity warped the New-
tonian concepts of time, space and gravity, so govern-
ments will begin to see that an unfamiliar geometry of
international relations is forming around them. Some
will sense opportunities; many more will realise how

unprepared they are, and that they are vulnerable to
strange forces they do not really understand.
The reason for this is the onrush of artiﬁcial intelli-
gence. Even without it, the post-1945 order was being
pulled down by the rise of China and America’s impa-
tience with the military and ﬁnancial costs of acting as
the world’s sole hegemon. With AI as an accelerant,
the collapsing order will burn much faster, because the
technology will impinge directly on many of the vital

components of national power.
Start with time. AI time does not just pass faster
than government time, it is also accelerating exponen-
tially, as computing power grows and frontier models
become more capable. The ﬁrst atomic bomb was
used in war in 1945; the Nuclear Non-Proliferation
Treaty, which governs the spread of nuclear weapons,
took 25 years to come into force. Today the frontier
labs are releasing new AI models at the rate of over ten
a month. Within the next year or two, the models

could be improving themselves.
As covid-19 showed, human beings struggle to
comprehend exponential growth. They are continually
surprised by the magnitude of the latest doubling,
whether it is the number of new infections or the new
powers of the latest AI model. Government institu-
tions struggle even more to cope with such a rhythm
of change. They need to be receptive to lobbying from
different interests—which is why they build in delays
with consultation periods, hearings and rights of ap-

peal. And political leaders will naturally tend to focus
on AI’s domestic impacts—until a crisis erupts abroad.
After time comes space. Normal space is governed
by physical proximity. Britain does 2.5 times more
trade with Ireland than with Japan, even though Ja-
pan’s economy is six times bigger. People make more
than 12m round trips across the Irish Sea each year, but
only about 700,000 between Britain and Japan—which
has 17 times as many people as the island of Ireland.

By contrast, AI space is global. The supply chains
that furnish data centres are wrapped around the plan-
et. America designs chips, Japan supplies chipmaking
chemicals, the Netherlands builds lithography equip-
ment, South Korea makes memory, Taiwan produces
processing chips, and so on. Even China depends on
some of these, despite efforts to develop its own sup-
ply chain. Each is a potential source of leverage.
The models defy distance, too. They are, for the

most part, American or Chinese—and users are under
pressure to bind themselves to one stack or the other,
from chip to chatbot. Models are optimised to run on,
say, Nvidia or Huawei processors. National rules cover
export controls, data privacy and security. Countries,
naturally, want to avoid being captive to a single sup-
plier, but America and China will seek to corral them.
America’s Pax Silica regime promises access to its
technology, so long as Chinese kit is excluded. China’s

Digital Silk Road promotes cheap, open-weight mod-
els as a counterweight to American “neo-imperialism”.
Last is AI’s dangerous shift in gravity, which risks
dragging the world into conﬂict. The late Henry Kis-
singer observed that stable world orders strike a bal-
ance between legitimacy and power. If they are to en-
dure, they must work by broadly accepted rules that
everybody knows can, if necessary, be successfully de-
fended by force. AI will disrupt that equilibrium in all

the dimensions of state capacity.
Warfare is being recast in Ukraine, by swarms of
AI-enabled drones and autonomous weapons, and
over the skies of Iran, where AI-enhanced targeting
accelerated the pace of American and Israeli bombing.
Economies are being shaken up by a torrent of invest-
ment in AI infrastructure that heralds dramatic com-
mercial change—even if a stock-market crash gets in
the way. And, as the data-centre ﬂap in America’s mid-
term elections showed, AI will test how well political

systems can adapt to creative destruction.
One thing is certain. AI will create winners and los-
ers. Some ambitious national leaders will tell them-
selves that the world has become unfair, because it no
longer reﬂects their enhanced military, economic and
political status. Feeling their own power, a few will
become impatient—and impatience can lead to war.
In many areas AI can help humans overcome their
limitations. It has an unmatched ability to solve math-

ematical problems, model the structure of proteins,
write code and analyse data. Geopolitics, by contrast,
will still depend on the wisdom of people. Can the po-
werful countries that emerged from the second world
war accommodate the powerful countries that thrive
in the era of AI? And can the countries that thrive resist
the ancient urge for score-settling and conquest? ●
When AI collides with geopolitics
Artiﬁcial intelligence’s implications for global affairs will ﬁnally become apparent, says Edward Carr

Artiﬁcial
intelligence will
test how well
political systems
can adapt
to creative
destruction Edward Carr Deputy editor, The Economist

### Page 12

12 Leaders The World Ahead 2027
D
ONALD TRUMP’S critics have been predicting his
demise for years. After the attack on the Capitol
on January 6th 2021, commentators on Fox News pre-
dicted that Mr Trump was “done, politically” and that
Republicans would not support him in another bid for
the White House. Democrats said he should never be
allowed to run for office again. Yet Mr Trump was re-
elected president in 2024 and has since governed as if
voters’ love for him is unconditional. It isn’t. On

November 3rd they repudiated him by giving the
Democrats the biggest congressional landslide in 50
years. “You could sort of mark it on your calendar,”
said Tucker Carlson, a podcaster. “Trumpism gone.”
That is, again, premature. Trumpism cannot be
gone while Mr Trump remains president. But the
Trump saga is indeed entering its ﬁnal chapter. Voters,
it seems, do not like it if you promise to end forever
wars and reduce prices, then launch an open-ended

conﬂict that raises them. Nor will they vote for repel-
lent candidates just because Mr Trump told them to.
In 2027 the embattled president will be less a lame
duck than an Angry Bird, facing threats from all sides.
Divided government makes it harder for a presi-
dent to pass legislation, but Mr Trump prefers to push
his agenda through executive orders anyway—he has
issued more executive orders in less than two years
than Barack Obama did in eight. Those who hoped Mr

Trump might spend the rest of his term adorning
Washington’s metro stations with chandeliers should
be chastened by the announcement, on November 6th,
that he would make Stephen Miller his chief of staff.
Mr Miller is a pugnacious nationalist. Rarely has an ap-
pointment been so plainly a battle cry.
Even with Democratic control of Congress, Mr
Trump maintains wide latitude in leading America’s
foreign policy. He will be keen to make his mark, be

that through sound deals or ill-conceived ones, in par-
ticular over Ukraine and Iran. Erstwhile allies will not
want to provoke Mr Trump, but will face their own po-
litical consequences if they are supine to him, partic-
ularly as his power wanes. America’s adversaries will
be increasingly bold, and may precipitate a crisis for
which Mr Trump and his dwindling staff are poorly
prepared. Progress is possible. Volatility is assured.
Domestically, after a period in which the Capitol’s

occupants displayed the vigour of tinned sardines,
Congress will spring to life. Democrats will pass mea-
sures to reduce costs, for instance on health care. Mr
Trump may surprise Democrats (and perhaps even
disappoint some) by deciding to work with them.
More likely, he will veto Democrats’ bills, and Demo-
crats will then use their budget-making authority to
try to extract concessions. Bruising budget battles and
government shutdowns will follow.

In the House, Democrats will advance investiga-
tions, including of the Trump family’s business inter-
ests and the handling of ﬁles on Jeffrey Epstein, a con-
victed paedophile. The White House and its allies will
ignore subpoenas from Congress; that will do little to
dim the spotlight Democrats will shine on their deal-
ings. And though Mr Trump will not be impeached—if
Democrats didn’t have the votes to impeach him after
January 6th, they won’t now—members of his cabinet

will be. Mr Trump values loyalty among his subordi-
nates, but 2027 will reveal that loyalty to be unrecipro-
cated. The president will do little to defend Robert F.
Kennedy junior, for instance, the health secretary who
has overseen the worst measles outbreak in 35 years.
Amid these ﬁghts, Mr Trump can expect ebbing
support from former allies. Clever Republicans will
work with Democrats on issues such as regulating ar-
tiﬁcial intelligence, to limit its most severe risks, and

seek to moderate Democrats’ leftist impulses. Some
Republicans will support House Democrats’ investi-
gations of Mr Trump’s self-dealing: 90% of Americans
say they think government corruption is widespread.
Mr Trump has grown accustomed to a Congress that
willingly gave up its authority. That era is over.
The president faces a different but no less fero-
cious ﬁght over the MAGA movement, as his loyalists
battle MAGA purists over what the election means for

their future. The purists will argue that MAGA has not
failed, but that Mr Trump failed MAGA—in particular
by being too closely aligned with Israel, waging war on
Iran and raising prices. The answer to Republicans’
midterms loss, MAGA purists will contend, is not to re-
ject nationalism or populism, but to double down on
them. That will mean calling for even more immigra-
tion restrictions, as well as a quick end to the Iran war.
Presidential hopefuls such as J.D. Vance, the vice-

president, and Ron DeSantis, Florida’s departing go-
vernor, will aim to position themselves as MAGA’s true
heirs. Right after the midterms, Mr Vance offered the
wispiest of veils to a condemnation of his boss’s poli-
cies. The constitution bars Mr Trump from sacking his
vice-president. But there is no constitutional prohibi-
tion of press conferences in which a president lacer-
ates his number two as he perspires nearby. Mr Trump
may be on the way out, but he won’t go quietly. ●

14 Leaders The World Ahead 2027
P
OPULISM TOOK a beating in 2026. Voters in Hun-
gary tossed out the Muslim-bashing, institution-
subverting and nakedly corrupt government of Viktor
Orban. Serbians sacked a similarly ﬁlthy, Putin-hug-
ging regime. Brazilians unexpectedly rejected Flávio
Bolsonaro, the ultra-nationalist son of a coup-plotter,
and stuck with their spendthrift left-wing president.
The world’s most powerful populist, President Donald

Trump, suffered a walloping at midterm elections and
will have to deal with a hostile, probing Congress for
his last two years in office. And, amid revelations of
questionable funding from overseas, Britain’s main
populist-nationalist party, Reform UK, has fallen
behind Labour and the Conservatives in the polls.
Yet it is too soon to say that populism has peaked.
The populist formula—a charismatic leader who
claims to represent the authentic people against the

venal elite, and who promises to protect a nation, cul-
ture or faith from supposedly existential (but often
wildly exaggerated) threats—has plenty of mileage in
it yet. In 2027 expect this formula to make advances in
new places, most notably in France.
Marine Le Pen, the leader of the anti-immigrant,
France-ﬁrst National Rally, is the odds-on favourite to
become French president in May. That would send
shockwaves through the European Union, whose pow-

ers she wants to curb. In Germany the populist-right
party of Alice Weidel, Alternative for Germany, will
consolidate its lead in national polls. Down under, the
One Nation party of Pauline Hanson, who calls for a
“monocultural” Australia, has soared to become joint
front-runner in the polls, as an election looms in 2028.
Four factors are boosting populists. One is immi-
gration, an explosive topic across the rich world. Many
Spaniards, for example, blame a large inﬂux of Latin

Americans for the high cost of housing. Many more
were unnerved by the sight of thousands of would-be
migrants surging into Ceuta, a Spanish exclave on the
north African coast, in July. Spanish voters are poised
to deliver such strong support for Vox, a populist-
nationalist party, that it expects to form part of the
next ruling coalition.
A second factor is that populists offer simple-
sounding ﬁxes for economic gloom. France is a prime
example. Over 80% of French people say they are pes-

simistic about the future of their country and its econ-
omy. Taxes and deﬁcits are high, growth is slow and
governments ﬁnd it almost impossible to pass a bud-
get. After France was rocked by huge protests (and a
fair bit of rioting), voters are worried about law and
order, too. Ms Le Pen promises not only to restore
calm but also to cut taxes, safeguard beneﬁts and
reduce the (already low) retirement age. It is an inco-
herent pitch, but voters are expected to let her try.

War is a third factor. As conﬂicts in the Middle East
and Ukraine grind on, energy prices will remain high.
That will squeeze consumers and public budgets, add-
ing to popular frustration. That many countries are
trying to boost defence spending will not help. In Italy
the mildly populist prime minister, Giorgia Meloni,
risks being outﬂanked by the more right-wing Roberto
Vannacci. An ex-army general, he is soft on Russia and
wants to halt aid for Ukraine. Ms Meloni must call a

general election before the end of 2027—and will hope
that peace breaks out before then.
Fourth is a general sense of insecurity. Chinese
exports of manufactured goods will continue to men-
ace other countries’ industrial bases. Artiﬁcial intelli-
gence threatens to upend labour markets in unpredict-
able ways. Populists often win support by promising to
put up walls against the outside world and to turn
back the clock, and this message will resonate in 2027.

Fears of deindustrialisation are especially acute in
Germany, where consumers are abandoning domestic
petrol-car brands for Chinese electric vehicles.
Friedrich Merz, the centre-right chancellor, may well
be forced out in 2027.
What can hold populism back? Or, to put it another
way, what is mightier than Le Pen? The challenges are
many, but the outlook is not all gloomy. Populists of-
ten lose their charm once they are in charge. Their sim-
ple-sounding ﬁxes do not work—think of Mr Trump’s

tariffs. Their scorn for institutions and rules spooks in-
vestors, as Prabowo Subianto, Indonesia’s erratic pres-
ident, is discovering. They overreach, as Mr Trump did
with his brutal immigration raids. And if they are cor-
rupt, voters eventually notice, as they did in America,
Hungary and Serbia.
The task for conventional parties in 2027 is to pick
likeable, sensible, centrist candidates who can expose
the ﬂaws in the populist approach in language voters

understand. Senator Jon Ossoff, a Democrat from
Georgia, offers a masterclass in this. Britain’s prime
minister, Andy Burnham, shows promise. And markets
will play an important role in curbing the excesses of
populist governments: bond investors cannot be
blinded by appeals to patriotism. So populists will
continue to sow strife and disrupt institutions in 2027.
But they will not have everything their own way. ●
Has populism peaked?
Four factors are boosting the inﬂuence of populists, in Europe and beyond, says Adam Roberts

yet, and will
make advances
in new places Adam Roberts Foreign editor, The Economist

16 Leaders The World Ahead 2027
C
ONFRONTATIONS WITH Iran have a history of
shaping far-distant politics. Nearly half a century
ago, the hostage crisis in Tehran contributed to Jimmy
Carter’s defeat by Ronald Reagan in America’s presi-
dential election of 1980. Now Iran’s hardliners would
like to claim another victory. The Republicans were
trounced in America’s midterm elections in part be-
cause of a backlash against Donald Trump’s war with
Iran. The president’s domestic political troubles are

one reason to hope for less ﬁghting in the Middle East
in 2027. A new government in Israel may be another.
But an absence of all-out war will not herald peace.
The past year has highlighted both the extent of
American and Israeli military might and, like many of
their wars before, its limitations. Supremacy on the
battleﬁeld has not translated into political settle-
ments. In 2027 the war with Iran will continue to un-
dermine many long-held assumptions about the bal-

ance of power and security in the region. Mr Trump
will remain the wild card in all these calculations.
Assume the president is chastened by defeat at
home, and sets aside his plans to batter Iran again with
air strikes. Iran, its economy in tatters, and desperate
for sanctions relief, makes concessions. It opens the
Strait of Hormuz and agrees to talks about talks on its
nuclear programme. Mr Trump rushes towards a deal.
It is scrappy and full of holes. No one expects it to last

or to deﬁnitively constrain Iran’s nuclear plans. But it
lets the president declare victory—and it is not war.
That could well happen. But the president could
just as plausibly swing the other way. In the pick-your-
own-adventure of his Middle East policy, a bruised
and angry Mr Trump decides that a dramatic military
victory is the best way to salvage his reputation. He
once again unleashes America’s armed forces, whack-
ing military and civilian targets. Iran responds by

attacking its Gulf neighbours, taking out energy assets
and damaging essential civilian infrastructure.
Coming under renewed attack gives Iran’s hated
regime a boost. Its rulers are in no mood for compro-
mise with a weakened Mr Trump, nor to do much to
improve the lot of the Iranian people. Control of the
Strait of Hormuz is a wasting asset, but one Iran tries
to exploit for as long as it can, attacking tankers and
disrupting global energy markets.

Any new conﬂagration pleases Binyamin Netanya-
hu, no longer Israel’s prime minister but still lurking.
He continues to beat his war drum in an effort to keep
security at the heart of the political debate and ensure
his own political survival. A new minority government
proves incapable of booting him off the political stage.
Israel is gripped by political paralysis. The reconstruc-
tion of Gaza continues to go nowhere. A government
led by Gadi Eisenkot tries to tamp down settler vio-

lence in the West Bank, but has no interest in seriously
grappling with Israel’s relations with the Palestinians.
His government is more worried about worsening
relations with America, but is unable to ﬁx them.
That is also plausible. But whether the war resumes
or not, the Gulf states will look on with dismay. Amer-
ica’s continued retreat from its role as the regional
hegemon will leave them exposed. They will try to dis-
suade Mr Trump from restarting his war with Iran but

will have to recognise the limits of their inﬂuence.
They will respond by looking elsewhere for security
guarantees (though not by expanding the Abraham
accords with Israel). But the rivalry between Saudi
Arabia and the United Arab Emirates will complicate
such efforts. Regional differences will sharpen as
Oman and Qatar cut side-deals with Iran.
Muhammad bin Salman, Saudi Arabia’s crown
prince, will ﬁnd himself in a particularly awkward po-
sition. The Saudis will make a costly deal to buy off the

Houthis, the Iran-backed rebels who have been attack-
ing their airports and energy infrastructure. But it is
likely to unravel. The prince’s plan to transform his
kingdom relies on attracting investment, companies
and visitors to a country that promises stability and
prosperity. Persistent, unpredictable dangers threaten
that vision. Questions about his judgment will grow,
though no direct challenge to his rule will emerge.
Governments and businesses in the Gulf will ﬁnd

more and better workarounds for Hormuz, investing in
pipelines, alternative trade routes and other ways to
bypass the strait. That will make their economies more
resilient, but will also make the underlying regional
instability more durable by easing the immediate pres-
sure to ﬁnd a lasting resolution.
The coming year will see fewer battles. That will be
welcome. But piecemeal ceaseﬁres, armed stand-offs
and improvised economic workarounds will not bring

lasting peace. What was once unthinkable—American
and Israeli strikes on Iran; Iranian attacks on oil tank-
ers and infrastructure—will feel familiar. The Middle
East will have to live with uncertainty. Those who
could bring a meaningful end to the ﬁghting will not
consider it in their interests to do so. Instead, a sullen
stand-off will prevail, punctuated by threats, ﬂare-ups
and ﬁtful attempts at diplomacy. ●

18 Leaders The World Ahead 2027
N
EARLY ALWAYS, investors are wisest simply to ig-
nore governments’ mounting debt. Debt-to-GDP
ratios across the rich world have, after all, risen steeply
since the turn of the millennium. Only occasionally—
around the euro crisis, for example, or in Britain when
Liz Truss was brieﬂy prime minister—have ﬁscal woes
been a top-tier driver of bonds, stocks and currencies.
Even 2026, mostly, wasn’t another of those mo-

ments. Investors chattered more about high govern-
ment debt, but markets largely behaved normally.
Term premiums, a measure of the risk-compensation
investors demand for holding long-term bonds, were
high but did not rise by much in America and most
other rich countries. Bond yields marched upwards
around the world, but mostly in response to strong
growth, and high oil prices due to the Iran war.
The one exception, and it was a stark one, was
France, where a budgetary mess and looming election

pushed yields sharply higher. The spread of French
bonds over German ones approached the highs of the
euro crisis a decade and a half ago.
So when will talk become action? When will inves-
tors begin to materially reprice government debt to re-
ﬂect concerns about whether it really can be paid back
in an orderly fashion? The fundamentals, after all, are
troubling. The latest IMF ﬁgures put America’s budget
deﬁcit for 2026 at 8.5% of GDP, France’s at 5.4%, Brit-

ain’s at 3.8% and even once-frugal Germany’s at 4.3%.
Whether governments can credibly pay back their
debts depends also, though, on how quickly their
economies are growing and how much markets are
charging in debt interest. The Economist’s own calcula-
tions, which pull those factors together, suggest that
governments will need some eye-popping tax rises or
spending cuts to stabilise debt. America would need a
ﬁscal adjustment of more than 5% of GDP; France

would need one worth over 4%, but with far higher tax
rates as a starting-point. Britain, Germany and Canada
would all need adjustments of 2.5-3.5%. Each of those
has deteriorated substantially since 2025.
In 2027 governments may have room to delay. If the
Strait of Hormuz opens up and oil prices fall, that
could help pull down inﬂation, relieve pressure on
central banks to raise interest rates, and ultimately
help reduce bond yields. That would give politicians a

little more breathing room. Paradoxically, an AI-relat-
ed debt blow-up might do the same. As havens, gov-
ernment bonds tended to rally in 2026 (and yields thus
fell) when markets were most alive to those risks (see
Finance and economics section).
But it is more likely that ﬁscal worries will only
worsen. The presidential election in France, and the
start of serious campaigning for the 2028 election in
America, offer clear catalysts. France has already

struggled to make serious progress on its budgetary
woes during the presidency of Emmanuel Macron.
Betting markets have Marine Le Pen of the populist-
right National Rally as favourite.
In America, contenders for both parties will be in
giveaway mode with voters, and keeping studiously si-
lent on how their spending splurges and tax cuts will
be funded, let alone how they plan to tackle America’s
already vast deﬁcits. Whoever is elected in 2028 may

well also have to deal with the Social Security trust
fund running out towards the end of their presidency.
Covering that would require further tax rises.
Eventually, market pressure may build to the point
that politicians are forced to respond, as happened to
Ms Truss in Britain in 2022. But there will be plenty of
ineffectual efforts to delay the inevitable before then.
One recently came from America’s treasury secre-
tary, Scott Bessent. He had pledged to return deﬁcits

to 3% of GDP, as part of a “3-3-3” plan that would also
raise growth and oil production. Having failed to do
so, in August he announced plans to buy long-term
Treasuries, paid for by more short-term borrowing.
That only brieﬂy held yields down, before they surged
again. A hostile response from investors, including
Stanley Druckenmiller, his own former boss, seems to
have deterred Mr Bessent from further efforts.
But as yields rise, the temptation for similar ﬁddles

will increase. So will the desire to push debt problems
onto central banks, by pressing them to lower interest
rates. The European Central Bank is racing to bring in
new leadership before Ms Le Pen has a chance to im-
pose her will on the transition.
As long as fundamentals worsen, though, and poli-
ticians fail to make serious efforts to change them,
markets cannot ignore debt altogether. These issues
tend to show up ﬁrst, and most acutely, at points of

market stress. On “Liberation Day” in 2025 and, to a
lesser extent after Mr Bessent’s buybacks in 2026, the
dollar sold off sharply, even as bond yields rose—an
unusual and unwelcome combination. Investors will
be watching French bond yields closely in the run-up
to the election. Eventually, politicians will discover
that the only way markets can deal with a deteriorat-
ing ﬁscal position is to price it in, by demanding even
higher bond yields. They will not like that at all. ●

20 Leaders The World Ahead 2027
F
OR YEARS, people have made strange, crazy-sound-
ing predictions about artiﬁcial intelligence. It will
solve climate change, cure all disease and make people
immortal! It will turn the whole world into paper clips!
It will be so advanced that it will treat humans like
pets! It was easy to dismiss such speculation with a
shrug or an eye-roll. But in 2026 AI actually started to
do strange, crazy-sounding things.
In several separate incidents, swarms of AI agents

went rogue, breaking out of AI labs and breaching ex-
ternal systems. Crypto wallets were drained, and
supermarket fridges across Europe set to defrost, in
AI-assisted cyber-attacks. AI models produced sol-
utions to long-standing problems in maths, computer
science and ﬂuid dynamics, causing much soul-
searching in academia. AI-written stories won literary
prizes. AI-generated music topped the charts.
Suddenly, the risk that progress in AI could lead to

unexpected and often frightening outcomes seems
much more plausible. Things may seem strange
enough already. But in 2027 they will get even stranger.
One reason is that AI, already superhuman at cod-
ing, hacking and maths, may be about to become far
more powerful. Many in the industry expect 2027 to
herald the arrival of “recursive self-improvement”
(RSI): the process whereby AI systems meaningfully
improve their own successors, in a feedback loop that

leads to an explosion in AI capabilities. Some worry
that crossing this threshold will cause mankind to lose
control of the technology, as it starts to improve itself
in ways humans cannot comprehend. And RSI could
cause havoc in science, as AI models derive new results
that baffle humans, as is already happening in maths.
When it comes to cyber-security, the loss of con-
trol may already have occurred. In early 2026 Anthrop-
ic said its most powerful internal model, Mythos, was

too dangerous to release openly. As 2027 dawns, those
capabilities have proliferated. Open-source models
such as Kimi K3 from Moonshot, a Chinese lab, are ca-
pable of ﬁnding and exploiting cyber-security ﬂaws
that were once the preserve of America’s national
champions, with little ability to control who has access
to that power. At an AI conference in 2026, a majority
of leaders in the industry said they expected a cyber-
security incident costing $100bn, or 10,000 lives, to

happen in the next few years.
In theory, AI will improve cyber-security, by allow-
ing engineers to analyse software, and ﬁnd and patch
ﬂaws, before it is released. In practice, there is a lot of
old, vulnerable software out in the world already. Ven-
dors are rushing to issue security updates: Microsoft,
which used to issue 70-80 patches per month, issued
972 in September and 1,411 in October. But not all soft-
ware is actively maintained, and not all patches are ap-

plied (think of all those supermarket fridges). So the
tide of cyber-attacks will rise in the coming months.
The AI industry faces a testing and volatile year in
other respects, too. A popular and regulatory backlash
against data centres is intensifying, threatening the
construction of new infrastructure and spooking
investors. Anthropic’s public offering will expose its
ﬁnances to scrutiny; OpenAI is expected to follow.
And the labs face ﬁnancial pressure on two fronts.

First, spending on R&D shows no sign of slowing.
The huge cost of training runs is being exacerbated by
expensive “post-training” in which AI models’ abilities
are honed. If RSI kicks in, those abilities will surge, but
so will costs: AI software engineers can spend money
on computing power far more quickly than any human.
No wonder that, according to Anthropic’s prospectus,
released in October, the company expects to spend
$750bn on computing power in the coming years.

At the same time, the big American labs face grow-
ing pricing pressure. Competition from Chinese AI
labs has forced them to cut their prices, and the ﬁght is
far from over. In October DeepSeek, a Chinese lab,
made the software for running its models available
free online, to help users run AI models on their own
hardware rather than paying for cloud-based services.
Running AI models on desktop machines is largely a
hobby for now. But researchers at Stanford University

found that small AI models were just as good as cloud-
based models for over 80% of tasks. For many users, a
free, local model running on their laptop or smart-
phone may be good enough within a year or two—a
nightmare scenario for the big American labs.
But it would be good news for users of AI. With
costs pushed down by competitive pressure, and
capabilities continuing to improve thanks to pre-com-
mitted investment, the outcome could resemble the

explosion in connectivity powered by the telecoms
boom at the turn of the millennium. That boom did
not, of course, end well for the telecoms companies
themselves. The story of the past ﬁve years of the AI
industry has been of expectations and valuations soar-
ing, as reality struggled to keep up. In 2027 prepare for
the opposite: as AI systems start to change the world,
in increasingly strange ways, the collective suspension
of disbelief may come to an end. ●

T
HE OXFORD ENGLISH DICTIONARY is grateful for
your assistance. For more than a century the great
chronicle of the English language has welcomed sub-
missions from readers. These can be new words in the
historical record; old words with subtleties of meaning
that had eluded lexicographers; or, especially, cita-
tions that are older than any of those the OED already
has for a given entry, showing that a word is older than

previously thought.
Yet there is something the OED would rather you
did not send in: a new word you have just invented.
You might think that it is snappy and euphonic, and
meets a need. But, as lexicographers are keen to tell
anyone at a cocktail party, their job is not to include
good words and keep out bad ones. It is to deﬁne the
words in actual use, so that people who encounter
them can ﬁnd out what they mean. So if you have
coined a new word and you want it to get into the OED,

you must ﬁrst get people to use it.
What new words will be on people’s lips in 2027?
Prediction, like coining good words, is hard. Inventing
words that people will actually use in future is there-
fore harder still. That does not stop people trying.
Take “persevilience”, minted by Alastair Campbell, a
British writer and political adviser, who published a
book of that title in 2026. Sir Tony Blair’s former spin
doctor is not shy about his ambition: it is the “word the

world needs”, he writes. His text explicitly expresses
the hope that the good folks at the OED will include it.
If you haven’t guessed it yet—and you may not
have—“persevilience” is a portmanteau, a car-crash
involving two words: in this case, “perseverance” and
“resilience”. Mr Campbell says persevilience is greater
than the sum of these parts. But is it? The two words
are rather similar: it is hard to persevere if you are not
resilient to setbacks. And what is persevilience if not

“grit”, a good, old monosyllabic word popularised in
Angela Duckworth’s book of that title a decade ago?
The ﬁrst rule of a good neologism is that it must be
truly useful, applying to something that people are
itching to talk about but haven’t got a succinct name
for yet. How the world got by without “hangry” for as
long as it did, for example, is a mystery (though the
word is older than you might think: the OED has a cita-
tion from 1918). But once it was there, it was ready to

describe anyone forced to sit through a tedious pre-
sentation just before lunch.
Second, it helps a new word’s prospects if its mean-
ing is obvious the ﬁrst time you see it. If a word re-
quires people to do too much work to ﬁgure it out, its
chances are slim. Persevilience fails this test because it
is not obvious that the “ilience” bit has been borrowed
from “resilience”. With the best neologisms, you can
grasp their meaning immediately, as if the word had

always existed.
Rohit Barghava and Henry Coutinho-Mason, two
futurists, had a proliﬁc go at coinages in their recent
book, “Future Words”. Some of them satisfy these two
rules. “Tactilism”, for example, handily describes the
preference for buttons and knobs that, unlike touch-
screens, give tactile feedback. (Audi, Volkswagen and
other carmakers have committed to putting physical
controls back into cars hitting showrooms in 2027.)
“Mindfasting” could catch on as a term for giving your

brain a break from modern life’s endless bombard-
ment of incoming messages and information.
But you don’t need to be a futurist to know that
most coinages won’t succeed. Ms Barghava’s and Mr
Coutinho-Mason’s “sobrocrat” (from “sober” and the
“-crat” root found in “autocrat”) is “a wellness zealot
who feels entitled to audit what everyone else puts in
their body”. And a “resplurger” apparently buys lots of
used clothes in order to feel virtuous about saving the

planet. Perhaps, but do such people appear often
enough in most people’s lives to need a name?
Instead, it may help to start by spotting trends, to
identify where new words might catch on. The coming
year is expected to be the hottest ever, for example,
which may prompt more people to head to cooler
climes (the Nordics, say, rather than the Med) for a
“coolcation”—though whether they will call it that or
not remains to be seen. (And is “cooliday” better?) As

artiﬁcial intelligence is used to uncover security ﬂaws,
expect a wave of cyber-attacks against airlines, banks
and retailers in the coming months, which has already
been dubbed the “hackpocalypse”. And the use of AI
tools by populist politicians to create rabble-rousing
memes is, unsurprisingly, being called “slopulism”.
One reviewer of Mr Campbell’s tome, Kathleen
Stock, called him “husbristling and shrugnacious”, his
book “ambumptuous” but his prose “mawkward”.

Maybe so, but “Persevilience” made it onto the Sunday
Times bestseller list nevertheless. That may have more
to do with Mr Campbell’s popular podcast than his
skill at crafting language. But whatever the cause, his
critics will be disappointed to see good things hap-
pening to someone they dislike. They will surely be
shaking their heads in Freudenschade. ●

HOSE EXHAUSTED by a decade of volatile British
politics—be they voters or investors—can expect
little respite in 2027. But the country will at least
provide rich fare for students of political science. Andy
Burnham, Britain’s prime minister since July 2026, has
made sure the coming year will be dominated by big
debates. Several thorny questions of ideology and
constitutional theory will be on the agenda.
It is part conviction: Mr Burnham has been making

his pitch for years. But it is also an electoral rescue job.
Mr Burnham seized power from Sir Keir Starmer after
Labour’s polling collapsed, as progressive voters
shifted to other left-wing parties. By dangling big,
yet-to-be-deﬁned reforms—to fall on the other side of
a general election—the party hopes to lure them back.
The thorniest issue is Europe. Sir Keir sought to
turn down the volume with a low-key but limited
rapprochement. No more. Mr Burnham told Labour’s

conference in September that he would host an open
debate on where the country heads next, including
going “all the way” and rejoining the EU. That may
help Labour rebuild its electoral coalition: 79% of
those who voted for the party in 2024 say they would
like to rejoin. Some in the party want to promise a
referendum on re-entry in its election manifesto.
But consensus will be elusive. Although a majority
of Britons wish to rejoin, the wounds of 2016 run deep,

and Labour’s MPs and ministers are divided and
cautious. For some voters, Europe has become a
cultural issue as much as a geopolitical choice. And if
the populist-right National Rally triumphs in France’s
presidential election in 2027 that could cool the
appetite of some British progressives for closer
European ties.
Then there is electoral reform. Mr Burnham has
long argued for an end to Britain’s ﬁrst-past-the-post
system, which works well when two big parties

dominate but becomes dysfunctional and
unpredictable with ﬁve or more in contention. A
commission on an alternative will be set up, and
Labour will commit to a new system in its manifesto.
That would end the historical two-party dominance
of British politics for ever, and usher in an era of

▸ coalitions. But which model to choose? Do not be
surprised if the debate among election wonks
becomes more heated than the argument over whether
to change at all.
Next there is the intergenerational compact. Mr
Burnham has proposed a form of state-funded adult
social care—again, after the next election—which he
terms a “national care service”. It will be funded in part
by trimming the growth of the state pension. But how

much further taxation will be needed, and what will
the service entail? At stake is something much bigger
than service design: the balance of rights and
obligations between old and young.
The coming year will also reveal whether Mr
Burnham is the economic radical he claims, or a mere
tinkerer. He will ﬂesh out his aspirations for greater
“public control” of life’s “essentials”: water, energy and
housing. This, he says, does not necessarily imply state

ownership. He has offered a few clues. Water
companies should be accountable to mayors and
unable to pay “excessive” bonuses, he says; slum
landlords could have houses seized. But is this a
transformation, or just tweaking the rules for
industries that are already highly regulated?
Finally there is devolution: Mr Burnham wants a
shift of powers from a highly centralised government
to regional mayoralties. But how much, and whether
tax-raising powers are up for grabs, he has yet to say.

And when does the public get a say? Perhaps
sooner than he admits. Mr Burnham insists there will
be no election until 2029. Labour has a huge majority
in Parliament, and could lose scores, if not hundreds,
of seats in an election. By convention he is bound by
Sir Keir’s manifesto—but Sir Keir is gone, and Mr
Burnham has already made clear that he will set his
own agenda, and use power while he can.
Having laid out plans for after the next election,

might he be tempted to put them to the voters sooner,
so he can get on with rebuilding ties with Europe,
upending the constitution and much else? The
populists to the right, Reform UK, and to the left, the
Greens, have wobbled lately. The Conservatives
remain weak. Better, perhaps, to ﬁght them now. Mr
Burnham would relish a contest. A keen campaigner,
he loves making speeches and styles himself a people’s
tribune. In toppling Sir Keir, he proved willing to seize

the moment. Do not be surprised if 2027 turns out to
be an election year after all. ●

“T
HIS IS UNITING the right,” said
Robert Jenrick in January 2026,
speaking about his defection to Nigel
Farage’s populist outﬁt, Reform UK. “I
don’t think there’s a future for the
Conservative Party.” But a year later, it is
not clear that the former Tory immigration
minister picked the right horse.

The gap between Reform and the
Conservatives in opinion polls shrank from
around 13 percentage points in mid-2025 to
just three points in late 2026. And there is a
new contender to lead Britain’s right—the
far-right Restore Britain, led by Rupert
Lowe, a former Reform MP. In 2027 these
three parties will jostle to claim the mantle
of leading the right-wing opposition to
Andy Burnham’s Labour government.
Since the Conservatives’ landslide
defeat in 2024, Mr Farage has positioned

himself as the leader of Britain’s right.
Despite winning only ﬁve seats at the
general election, his party led opinion polls
from April 2025 to July 2026. In local
elections, Reform also struck deep into
traditional Labour and Conservative
heartlands. But amid these impressive
feats, Mr Farage has been dogged by
scandals about shady donations (he denies
wrongdoing). His personal ratings have
slipped. And his stunt of resigning as an
MP and calling a by-election in Clacton, his

constituency, made him look foolish and
irrelevant (even though he won easily).
Mr Farage has failed in his attempt to
destroy the Conservative Party. The Tories’
polling is hardly impressive—they have the
support of around 20% of voters—but that
is enough to deny Mr Farage uncontested
leadership of the right. Kemi Badenoch,
the tenacious Tory leader, is substantially
more popular than Mr Farage, and beneﬁts
from being the official leader of the
opposition in Parliament. The rise of

Restore, to the right of Reform, has also
complicated matters. It confounded
expectations by winning 9% of the vote in
the Greater Manchester mayoral
by-election in July. Nationally, it has the
support of between 2% and 6% of voters.
Some 44% of voters would consider
voting for at least one of the three right-
wing parties, according to YouGov, a
pollster. But half of the 28% of voters who
are considering the Conservatives would
not support Reform, making it hard for Mr

Farage to dominate the bloc. Three-
quarters of Restore supporters would
consider Reform, but pursuing those
voters—or cutting a deal with Mr
Lowe—might alienate the 8% of right-wing
voters who would consider voting for the
Conservatives or Reform, but not Restore.
In the local elections in May 2027 the
Conservatives, with 2,511 seats to defend,
have most to lose. If they are to lead a
right-wing bloc into the next election, they
need to hold most of these and improve on

their 2026 performance in heartlands such
as Essex, Kent, Norfolk and Suffolk.
Reform will hope to trounce the Tories in
these areas and become the main
opposition to Labour across the rest of the
country. And if Restore is going to be a
serious force at the next election, it must
easily win Great Yarmouth district council
(where Mr Lowe is the MP) and gain
councillors in areas that have voted for
Reform in previous local elections. As Mr
Burnham’s shine wears off and the next

general election starts coming into view,
the pressure for the right to consolidate
will cause intense partisan animosity. ●

RITICS OF THE Labour government
delighted in blaming it for Britain’s
woes in 2026. The big-spending rhetoric of
Andy Burnham, the new prime minister,
was said to have sent borrowing costs
soaring: in October the yield on ten-year
gilts hit nearly 5.5%, the highest since 2007
and the steepest in the G7 group of big rich
countries. Crippling energy prices were
partly put down to Labour’s reluctance to
drill for more oil and gas in the North Sea.

But it is a comforting ﬁction to think
that Britain could have sorted out its
problems in 2026 simply by having a better
government. True, Labour’s inability to
clamp down on spending did not soothe
the bond market’s nerves. But what 2026
really showed was how vulnerable
medium-size, open economies like Britain
are to events outside their borders. Britain’s
reliance on gas left it unusually exposed to
the conﬂict in the Middle East. And
because it lies outside a big currency bloc,

it had little shelter from the global rise in
bond yields, fuelled by the worldwide
AI-investment boom. In 2027, expect the
country’s economy to be buffeted by world
events once more.
How AI evolves will be particularly
important. Knowledge-based services,
from writing advertising copy to drafting
legal contracts, are the backbone of the
British economy. London remains one of
the world’s top two ﬁnancial centres.
Gloomsters fret that AI will replace these

jobs, with devastating consequences for
the economy. But the bull case is that
Britain, which has four of the world’s top
ten universities and the most venture-
capital funding in Europe, is particularly
well equipped to harness the technology.
There were encouraging early signs in
2026: computing-related services grew by
7% in the eight months to August
compared to the same period in 2025.
Growth in output per worker has
slowed to a crawl since the ﬁnancial crisis.

Recent headline growth numbers were also
underwhelming: the Office for Budget
Responsibility (OBR), an independent
watchdog, expects growth of 1.2% in 2026.
A speedy AI rollout could start to turn
Britain’s fortunes around.
The biggest threat is the same one that
dogged 2026: war in the Middle East, and
the higher energy prices that come with it.
The conﬂict has led the OBR to downgrade
its growth forecast for 2027 from 1.6% to
1.0%. The Bank of England expects

inﬂation to hit nearly 5% by mid-2027, more
than twice its target. On November 5th the
bank raised its rate to 4%, and the market is
pricing in several more quarter-point
increases in 2027. If the Iran war continues,
it will crimp spending and investment,
ending hopes of higher growth.
Geopolitical instability will also make it
hard for the government to deliver its
domestic objectives. The government must
conduct a spending review at some point
in 2027 to set departmental budgets. Mr

Burnham would ideally use it to fund the
causes he campaigned on, such as social
care or state-owned housing. But with
borrowing costs so high—Britain now
spends 80% more on servicing its debt
than it does on defence—the focus will
mostly be on preventing real-terms cuts.
And there will be strong demand to spend
anything left on defence.
Look out, too, for whether Mr Burnham
tries to deal with Britain’s long-standing
economic weaknesses in 2027. That could

mean removing the barriers to building in
Britain, such as onerous planning rules,
and reforming taxes to reward work. It
could also entail taking meaningful action
to contain the relentless rise in
government spending, whether on health
care or disability beneﬁts.
The signs are not promising. Mr
Burnham’s ﬁrst budget, on October 28th,
focused on handing out numerous freebies
with little else to show. But people
overestimate what a government can do in

a year, and underestimate what it can do in
a decade. The prime minister cannot
prevent a global bond crisis or end the
conﬂict in the Gulf. But he can push
through the reforms that will strengthen
Britain for the long term. ●

dependent on what happens elsewhere

HEN DONALD TRUMP ﬁrst met
Andy Burnham, in September 2026,
he said, “You call it United Kingdom,
Britain, they call it sometimes England. It’s
got more names than any other place.”
In law, the country is the United
Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern
Ireland, and 2027 marks the centenary of
that name’s adoption. The legislation

acknowledged that most of Ireland had
been carved out in 1922, rendering the
previous name—the United Kingdom of
Great Britain and Ireland—inaccurate.
The historic nature of this change was
not generally recognised at the time. The
100th anniversary will be similarly low-key,
with no big celebrations planned. The
birthday comes at a time when the future
of the union has never looked so uncertain.
The ﬁrst ministers of Scotland, Wales and
Northern Ireland all hail from nationalist

parties; they held a summit in September
where they agreed to work together to
hasten the break-up of Britain.
In all three of their countries, polls have
shown support for separation growing. But
they are not in exactly the same boat.

▸ prevailed in a referendum in 2014, the
nationalists have not given up. For the past
few years, polls on independence have
been “close to the 50/50 mark”, says Sir
John Curtice, a polling expert. The Scottish
National Party has vowed to hold another
referendum, but Mr Burnham says he will

Northern Ireland has a clearer route to a
public vote. The Good Friday agreement of
1998, which brought an end to decades of

violence, states that the secretary of state
for Northern Ireland must hold a “border
poll” if at any point it looks likely that a
majority of voters would opt for Irish
uniﬁcation. Surveys still show a majority in
support of the union with Britain, but
demographic trends look good for
supporters of Irish uniﬁcation because the
proportion of Catholics (generally
nationalists) is growing at the expense of
Protestants (generally unionists).
Wales is furthest from independence.

Support hovers around 30%. But 2026 was a
breakthrough year for Plaid Cymru, the
pro-independence party, which came ﬁrst
in elections for the Welsh legislature, the
Senedd, and now leads the government.
Backers of independence in all the
Celtic nations have the momentum.
Elections in May will show the strength of
support for ending the union. Voters in
Northern Ireland will elect members of the
assembly at Stormont, outside Belfast. The
nationalist party, Sinn Féin, will come top

for a second election in a row. Scotland and
Wales are holding local elections.
Though England is dominant in
Britain’s politics and economy, the largest
country in the union is often sidelined in
discussions about its future. In 2027 it will
celebrate 1,100 years since Athelstan, an
Anglo-Saxon king, became the ﬁrst ruler to
control the whole of what is now England,
after a victory in battle over Vikings who
ruled in the north. England’s birthday will

be marked with various events across the
country, while that of the United Kingdom
goes unnoticed. Supporters of the union
may see that as an ominous sign. ●

HE PRINCE REGENT, later King George
IV, did many bad things. He behaved

badly: at his wedding he became so drunk
he passed out. He ate badly: at a single
breakfast he ate “two pigeons…a glass of
champagne, two glasses of port and a glass
of brandy”. He dressed badly: in too-tight
silk he looked “like a great sausage stuffed
into the covering”. But for some Britons
perhaps the worst thing he did was that in
the 1820s he built Buckingham Palace.
“Buck House” occupies an odd place in
British life. It is a quintessential landmark,

appearing on fridge magnets and in ﬁlms.
It is also loathed: Edward VII insulted it (it
was like a “Scottish funeral parlour”);
Princess Diana hated it (she felt it had
“dead energy”); the Germans, perhaps
thoughtfully, bombed it. King Charles III
has disdained it: he prefers to stay in the
more bijou Clarence House nearby.
Now Britain has had to fork out a lot of
money to redo it. The reﬁt will ﬁnish in
March. There is a lot of it to redo: 775
rooms, 52 private bedrooms, 188 staff

bedrooms, a lake, two islands, several
magniﬁcent Rembrandts and numerous
revolting carpets. Much will be left
unchanged. Partly this is economic. The
refurbishment, despite costing £369m
($490m) and taking ten years, is on the
thrifty side: it is a “lifts, loos and electrics”
type of reﬁt. Partly this is aesthetic. Dan
Jones, a historian and author of “Castles”,
says Britain is in a “seal-it-in-aspic, change
nothing” era of historical preservation.

This is typical. Palaces offer a
monarchy, perhaps even a country, in
microcosm. Once, Britain had martial
monarchs who built martial homes: the
warrior, Edward III, redid the warlike
Windsor Castle. The Victorians offered a
decorative but distant monarchy. Walter
Bagehot, a 19th-century editor of The
Economist, wrote that monarchy puts
“petty life” on the throne but elevates it
through mystery. It is vital not to “let in
daylight upon magic”.
The 20th century changed things: not

merely daylight but ﬂashbulbs and even
commoners were let in when, in 1993,
Buckingham Palace was opened to the
public. Today, you can buy a ticket to tour
the palace for £33 (under-ﬁves go free). It
reveals not a magical monarchy but a
managerial, middle-class one: it was
George VI who ﬁrst called the royals “the
Firm”. Peep through the frosted windows,
and you see net curtains, ﬁling cabinets
and coffee cups. In 2003 a tabloid “exposé”
revealed that Elizabeth II ate cornﬂakes

from a Tupperware box. The queen, wrote
Zadie Smith, a novelist, always seemed
“distinctly lower middle-class”.
The mystery, then, is gone. Yet every
day long queues form at Buckingham
Palace and, when the queen died, Britons
were drawn towards it. Perhaps Bagehot
got it wrong: it is not the magic of
monarchs that matters, but the mundanity.
Like everyone, they are born, they live, they
eat cornﬂakes, they die. And sometimes
they have to get their electrics redone. ●

Buck
What is the meaning of
Buckingham Palace?
Catherine Nixey Britain correspondent,
The Economist



















### Page 30

30 Britain The World Ahead 2027
I
SPENT MY childhood in a
very unstable country, with
severe economic shocks,
capital controls, a coup, and
elections annulled because the
military dictatorship didn’t like
the result. This taught me two
things. The ﬁrst is that you
cannot rely on government to
solve every problem—in some
cases it is the problem. The
second is that the only defence
against the unexpected is to
build resilience.
Britain has done the

opposite. After decades of
peace, complacency has set in.
Even the succession of shocks
in recent years has not shaken
us from our stupor. We still
look for easy explanations and
quick ﬁxes. When prime
ministers kept changing, many
assumed the problem was
simply the Conservatives. But
Sir Keir Starmer was gone after
barely two years. The instab-
ility runs much deeper than
who happens to be in power.
The chances are that in the
near future Britain will face

some combination of energy
shock, defence shock, ﬁnancial
shock or terror threat that
causes social upheaval. Each
requires its own response, but
if I were prime minister today, I
would be getting Britain ready
for all of them.
Start with economic
resilience. Britain’s public debt
is now around 94% of GDP. The
last time it was this high, we
were paying off two world
wars. Clearly we must learn to
live within our means, and
dismantle the absurd
restrictions that limit growth.

But we must also think
carefully about how the next
economic shock might unfold.
Artiﬁcial intelligence is
phenomenal technology, but
the excitement around it
reminds me of the internet
boom 30 years ago. Some worry
about AI destroying jobs;
others worry about a knock at
the door from the Terminator. I
worry about a bubble bursting.
They always do. When capital
is ﬂowing into an industry, it is
easy to start a company. A
rising tide lifts all boats, but

transformative technology
does not make every business
sound. An economic downturn
exposes the weak ones.
I am not saying AI will fail,
or trying to predict when an AI
downturn might happen. My
concern is whether our
government is ﬁnancially
strong enough, given the huge
budget deﬁcit, to deal with
such a scenario. In the global
ﬁnancial crisis, governments
bailed out the banks to protect
the ﬁnancial system. It is not
clear they are strong enough to

do the same again. And when a
crisis comes, it is not govern-
ment’s role to save every job or
cover every loss. Instead, it
should protect the economy’s
foundations and remove bar-
riers that stop new ﬁrms taking
the place of those that fail.
Resilience goes well beyond
the economy. Terrorist groups,
criminal gangs and rogue
individuals now have access to
technologies that make them
very difficult to deter. Hackers
recently found a way to turn
fridges in some European

supermarkets up to 50°C.
We must be realistic: much
of Western deterrence relies on
America. But Britain must
restore a credible measure of
national deterrence. That
means protecting critical
infrastructure and contributing
to NATO-led deterrence in
Europe, with a focused military
and faster procurement.
Another aspect of
deterrence receives much less
attention. Hostile states are
recruiting people in Britain.
They ﬁnd the vulnerable and
foolish online, and persuade

them to carry out acts whose
purpose they do not under-
stand. The arson attack on the
Starmer family home is a
disturbing example: men in
Britain, recruited online,
presumably by Russia.
Part of our deterrence must
be to reduce the supply of these
useful idiots. Who would have
thought that wanting criminals
to be more patriotic would
become a national-security
objective? Yet here we are. Guy
Ritchie-style villains are one
thing. Criminals willing to

betray Britain to hostile powers
are inﬁnitely more dangerous.
We must respond in schools
and with the national story we
tell ourselves. If we let the idea
that Britain is racist run amok,
or let ethno-nationalism take
hold, we weaken the ties that
bind people together. Social
cohesion is a question of
national resilience as well as
national identity.
In all these areas it is natural
to ask what government can do.
But we should ask what we can
do ourselves. Seeing the

government as a safety blanket,
absorbing all risk and
misfortune, has made us forget
how much agency and personal
responsibility we retain.
We protect ourselves from
ﬁnancial risk by saving, from
losing our livelihoods by
learning new skills, and from
societal breakdown by
engaging with the world
around us. We cannot control
which shock will come next.
But we can build resilience,
to prepare for whatever is
thrown our way. ●
Britain needs to prepare for the next shock

The instability runs
deeper than who
happens to be in power

31The World Ahead 2027
Europe
E
UROPE IS ACCUSTOMED to crises arriving from
outside: columns of Russian tanks, salvoes of
American tariffs, container ships full of subsidised
Chinese goods. In 2027 disruption will come from
within. National elections will take place across the
European Union, including in four of its ﬁve biggest
countries. The immediate consequence of ballots in
France, Italy and Poland—as well as the upshot of
Spanish elections to be held in November 2026—will

be political torpor, as incumbents avoid some difficult
Europe-wide decisions. The longer-term question
could prove more profound as right-wing populists
gain yet more inﬂuence.
France will go ﬁrst, in April and May. But the
trouble will start before anyone casts a vote. National
elections have a habit of turning wannabe European
statesmen into timid local politicians. Agreement over
deepening the EU single market or pledging billions to
succour Ukraine is tricky at the best of times. At

election time, prime ministers contemplating a
difficult compromise in Brussels suddenly remember
the farmers, pensioners and other domestic groups
who need mollycoddling instead. With one big
country after another entering campaign mode in
2027, the EU risks spending much of the year waiting
for somebody, somewhere, to ﬁnish voting.
There is never a good time for yet more EU stasis.
But two big decisions with long-term repercussions
will have to be made one way or another in 2027. One

concerns Ukraine. A €90bn ($101bn) funding package
agreed on in 2025 was meant to provide enough money
for two years. In fact a surge in defence spending—and
America’s continued reluctance to contribute—means
some kind of new funding mechanism will be required
sooner. Some national governments are angling for the
EU to conﬁscate Russian assets and, in effect, hand
them over to Ukraine. This will still prove as difficult in
2027 as when it was last mooted in 2025.

The other tricky dossier is the EU’s seven-year
budget. A new iteration must be agreed on for the
2028-34 period. National leaders had hoped for a deal
in 2026, but this seems unlikely. The budget, worth
Political
paralysis

will slow the EU to a crawl
