▸ €2trn over seven years, could in theory include lots of
money towards programmes funding innovation that
would help raise the EU’s weak productivity. In
practice, election-minded politicians are likely to
divert a big sum once again to the budget’s perennial
winners: farmers.
Political paralysis may prove the least of Europe’s
worries. Depending on their outcome, the elections
could alter the political complexion of the EU itself.

Populists of various guises will either be vying for
office or could become kingmakers in ways that would
bring them closer to power. None is enamoured of the
EU; several have pro-Russian sympathies and are
ambivalent about Ukraine.
The most consequential result will be in France.
Marine Le Pen’s National Rally no longer wants to
leave the EU. But to have one half of the traditional
Franco-German “engine” at the heart of the club run
by a politician who plainly does not buy into the

project (and whose party was once funded by a
Russian bank) would be a ﬁrst in eight decades of
European integration. A Eurosceptic president in
France could, in theory, be offset by a dynamic
chancellor in Germany with lots of political capital to
spend on EU matters. Friedrich Merz is not that man.
State elections could weaken him further in 2027.
In Italy the populists may offer stability. Giorgia
Meloni, though of the populist right, has been

steadfast in her commitment to the EU (perhaps
because being part of the club helps Italy get away
with running budget deﬁcits while having huge debts).
But she may need the votes of more Brussels-bashing
parties to return to office after elections due in the
second half of 2027.
Finally, Poland will vote in parliamentary elections
no later than the autumn. Donald Tusk’s government
came into office in 2023 promising to repair Poland’s
relationship with the EU after eight years under the

populist Law and Justice party. But tricky domestic
politics have resulted in few reforms to enthuse Mr
Tusk’s liberal base. A poll of Polish polls suggests the
outcome could go either way.
The temptation in Brussels will be to view 2027 as a
year to be endured, one dedicated to keeping the show
on the road rather than forging ahead. But that may
underestimate what is coming. National elections do
not merely interrupt European politics. Occasionally

they transform it. ●
Political
paralysis may
prove the least
of Europe’s
worries
F
RANCE COULD deliver Europe a
thunderbolt in 2027. For the ﬁrst time
voters may elect a Eurosceptic nationalist
president, who distrusts Berlin and
Brussels as much as NATO, and was once
close to Russia’s Vladimir Putin: Marine Le
Pen of the populist-right National Rally.
Her victory, at a two-round vote on April
18th and May 2nd, is not inevitable. But she
is the favourite in the polls. If Ms Le Pen

were to succeed Emmanuel Macron, who is
constitutionally barred from running for a
third consecutive term, it would not only
shake France but upend the guiding
Franco-German dynamic at the heart of
the European Union.
True, Ms Le Pen no longer wants a
“Frexit”, nor to pull France out of the euro.
But her plans would set France on a
collision course with Germany and the
union’s institutions and rules. She
promises to halve France’s net contribution

to the EU budget, contest how that budget
is spent and end ﬁnancial aid to Ukraine.
The reality of governing, a bond-market
crisis, or both, might temper a Le Pen
presidency’s hostility to the EU, as
happened when Giorgia Meloni took
office in Italy. But France would certainly
lose its long-standing role as the bloc’s
generator of ideas, summits and coalitions
that bring members closer together. EU
decision-making would jam up. France
would respect its basic commitments to

NATO, but “France First” would be the
underlying impulse as the country turned
inwards and closed its borders.
Can anyone beat Ms Le Pen? It remains
possible. Some voters may recoil at the
ballot box, especially if the centre can rally
around a credible candidate. But the search
for that candidate will be painful. The
Socialists’ Raphaël Glucksmann will stay
in the race. If he falters, François Hollande,
a former president, will try to jump in. If

Edouard Philippe, a centrist former prime
minister, is better placed than his rival,
Gabriel Attal, the latter will drop out in his
favour. But if Mr Philippe’s campaign stalls,
centrists may scramble for a backup. A
surprise candidacy from an outsider,
possibly Christine Lagarde, cannot be
ruled out. But voters could end up with a
choice between Ms Le Pen and Jean-Luc
Mélenchon of the populist left.
France will be a turbulent place before,
and after, the vote. Campaigning will get

nasty and markets will be jittery. If Ms Le
Pen wins, her victory will be greeted by
widespread street protests. Whoever wins
will ﬁnd it hard to govern. Legislative
elections due after the presidential vote are
likely to return another fragmented
parliament, depriving the new president of
a working majority. A President Le Pen
would try to get round this by seeking a
coalition, probably under Jordan Bardella,
with the centre-right and holding a
referendum on questions related to

“national preference”, including tighter
immigration and citizenship rules. A
centrist president would struggle with
sclerotic policymaking in the face of a
divided lower house, and an obstructive
and bitter National Rally.
Either way, the end of the Macron
decade will be acrimonious and volatile.
The president will leave under a cloud of
unpopularity. The country wants change,
but whoever wins in 2027 will struggle to
bring stability to a febrile France. ●

33The World Ahead 2027 Europe
I
N FEBRUARY 2007 Vladimir Putin delivered a speech
that shocked the world. Speaking to the Munich
Security Conference, he openly challenged the
American-led world order that emerged after the
collapse of the Soviet Union and put Russia on a path
of confrontation with the West.
Nearly 20 years later, Mr Putin predicted that “a
new world order is inevitable and…will occur within
the foreseeable future.” His war in Ukraine, he implied

in October at the Valdai Forum, a ﬂagship foreign-
policy conference, was forcing the world to recognise
the new reality. “We are only beginning to outline,
feeling our way towards the contours of what is to
come,” he said. The war also forced the organisers to
move the conference from Sochi to Moscow, because
of the threat of Ukrainian drones.
That same month Mr Putin, long obsessed with the
idea of a Yalta-style global carve-up into spheres of
inﬂuence, suggested a trilateral meeting with

presidents Xi Jinping and Donald Trump. And on
November 4th, celebrated as a holiday of national
unity, he walked through an exhibition of “Christian
values” in Moscow and stopped next to a modern
statue displaying Churchill, Roosevelt and Stalin in
Yalta in 1945. “Here are the leaders of the three most
important countries gathered together to discuss how
to settle the world after the war,” he explained to
children brought for the occasion.
Yet while the Yalta conference of 1945 was the result

of the Soviet victory in the Great Patriotic War, as the
second world war is known in Russia, Mr Putin is
touting a new Yalta not as a result, but as a substitute
for victory. His war in Ukraine has now lasted longer
than the ﬁrst world war and has cost Russia at least
370,000 dead and more than a million wounded—
more than any war Russia has fought since 1945.
In this time Mr Putin has failed to capture the
whole of Donbas, the task he set for his army in 2022.

The chances that he will do so in the coming year are
slim. In October some 54,000 Russian troops were
killed or wounded—the highest monthly losses since
2022—even as they ceded some territory.
Unable to defeat Ukraine on the battleﬁeld, Mr
Putin has switched to attacking the rear, hoping to
break Ukraine and destabilise Europe. In recent
months he has intensiﬁed aerial attacks on Ukrainian
cities using drones and ballistic missiles against
critical infrastructure, including heating and water

supplies, taking advantage of Ukraine’s shortage of
interceptors. His goal is to make cities unliveable and
to force a ﬂood of refugees into Europe which would
put pressure on its febrile politics and increase the cost
of supporting Ukraine. He is also intensifying
grey-zone attacks on European countries that support
Ukraine’s military production. A Russian drone
discovered in Leipzig in August, laden with explosives,
was just one example.
As he tries to cut Ukraine off from Western

ﬁnancial and military support, Mr Putin will aim to
make the best use of favourable conditions, which
include another two years of Mr Trump’s presidency;
upcoming elections in France; and the war in Iran,
which is keeping oil prices high. For now, Russia’s
economy still lets Mr Putin continue his war without
affecting most people’s incomes. But economic and
political resources are being depleted faster than they
can be replaced. Sign-up bonuses have been slashed,

growth has stalled and large regions, including the
Urals, Siberia and the south of Russia, are in recession.
The ﬁrst three years of war were largely ﬁnanced
from accumulated oil and gas revenues and through
high oil rents. But the conﬂict is now being ﬁnanced by
extracting money from the population and private
businesses. As the war sucks up resources, the civilian
economy is contracting. This does not mean that the
Kremlin will run out of money. But it does mean that it

must either scale down or stop the war, or break the
social contract with the population and the elites, who
are already feeling alienated.
As economic resources become more scarce, the
regime is becoming ever more coercive. If there is one
way in which Mr Putin is matching Stalin, it is by
turning the FSB, the successor to Stalin’s secret police,
into the dominant political force inside the country,
tightening control over society and expanding
repression. The average length of prison sentences in

Russia has increased to 11 years. By terrorising Ukraine
and his own people, Mr Putin hopes to preserve his
power. But neither Ukraine nor Russia can win the war
of attrition he has unleashed. Both are losing. ●
Much talk,
but no deal
The Russia-Ukraine conﬂict will grind on

34 Europe The World Ahead 2027
⏩
F
OR EUROPE’S industry, China long held
the promise of riches. A vast market was
there for the taking, even if Chinese
authorities pressed European ﬁrms to form
joint ventures with locals to make sure
domestic competitors or suppliers could
grow around them. That growth has now
become unbearable for the former
beneﬁciaries. Chinese ﬁrms are making
inroads into the markets for cars, wind
turbines and other products that had

hitherto been deemed Europe’s home turf.
New technologies such as artiﬁcial
intelligence, where China is ahead of
Europe, are making Chinese companies
even ﬁercer competitors.
Cue panic, especially in Germany. As a
result, the European Union will toughen its
trade defences in 2027. It has started
numerous investigations into China’s
government subsidies for its ﬁrms, which
European rivals say give them unfair cost
advantages. It is considering stronger

measures to diversify supply chains (read:
away from China) to make itself less
vulnerable to economic blackmail. Rules
for subsidies and procurement in Europe
may soon include “Buy European” clauses
to keep more local demand on the
continent. And Europe is building a
“second-strike” capability, such as a partial
shut-out from its market, should China
retaliate too strongly against its trade
measures. It is strong stuff.
How will China respond? In previous

trade spats, China has used targeted
measures to hurt those backing punitive
action. When the EU put tariffs on Chinese
electric vehicles (EVs), on the basis of
unfair subsidies to the industry, China
went after brandy (and thus France, a
leading supporter of the tariffs) and some
agricultural goods.
The coming round of Chinese
responses will probably focus on the
sectors where Europe acts against it. When
the European Commission started an
investigation into Chinese chemicals in

September, for example, China opened its
own investigations into European
chemicals a few days later. If Europe
widens its anti-subsidy investigations to
whole industries instead of individual
ﬁrms, China may also widen its retaliation.
It could, for example, make life even more
difficult for European ﬁrms operating
within its borders.
That said, the trade spat will probably
not escalate too far. China’s economy is not
doing so well that its leaders are willing to

add further pressure. And Europe remains
the most open large market for Chinese
ﬁrms; a low tariff wall makes it only slightly
less attractive. Even with a tariff on EVs,
sales in the EU for BYD, the largest Chinese
EV-maker, increased by 154% in the ﬁrst
nine months of 2026, compared with the
same period in 2025, as high petrol prices
boosted European demand. Whatever the
EU does, China will respond
proportionately and then move on.
But this continuous retaliation in

multiple sectors will mean that China and
Europe will ﬁnd themselves in a
permanent, mid-level trade war in 2027.
That will hurt Europe’s economy, which
has been remarkably resilient in the face of
another big energy shock. Tariffs on
Chinese goods, far from beneﬁting
Europe, will hurt its consumers and
downstream customers in global markets.
Pushing China out will also intensify
competition in other markets around the
world where European producers face even

ﬁercer (and untariffed) competition from
Chinese companies. The gain for
European producers from being shielded
from Chinese competition at home will be
of little beneﬁt and merely buy European
ﬁrms some time.
The seemingly easy option of boosting
Europe’s industry through protectionism
will be politically popular in Europe, but
will slowly give way to frustration at
meagre results. Then the problem will be
that tariffs, once in place, are difficult to

reverse. Europe’s steel tariffs, supposedly a
temporary measure, were recently made
permanent. It will take further industrial
decline before liberal policies have any
chance of making a comeback. ●
Retaliate,
rinse, repeat
Europe and China are entering
a permanent mid-level trade war

T
HE ROAR of jet engines is becoming an
increasingly familiar sound over
Keﬂavik, an air base in Iceland originally
built by America during the cold war. Now
its squat concrete bunkers shield mainly
European NATO ﬁghter jets from the
driving Arctic wind—and from a resurgent
Russian threat. So intense is the activity at
times that a European ﬁghter pilot
complains that sleep is often disturbed by
patrol aircraft heading out to hunt for

Russian submarines.
There have been similar increases in
activity across many of NATO’s European
members as they bolster their defences
against Russia, which is escalating its
campaign of sabotage and other “hybrid”
attacks, for instance using drones. The
urgency comes from two ticking clocks.
The ﬁrst is a countdown, after any ceaseﬁre
in Ukraine, of how much time Russia
would need to reconstitute its armed
forces to threaten a NATO member.
Estimates vary, and many are kept secret,

but those from Denmark’s defence-
intelligence service are fairly typical: it
reckons it could be as little as six months
for a limited attack, to around two years for
a regional war against several countries in
the Baltic region.
The second clock measures how much
time Europe will have to replace American
ﬁghting power and crucial capabilities,
such as intelligence. Some of these are
being withdrawn by the Trump administ-
ration, which complains about bankrolling

the defence of Europeans who are not
pulling their weight.
In a bid to keep America engaged,
NATO leaders agreed in 2025 to increase
core defence spending to 3.5% of GDP by
2035 (from a previous minimum of 2%). Yet
several key European members, including
Britain and France, are far off track. Some
worry that this could annoy President
Donald Trump and prompt him to
accelerate America’s drawdown. A key
indicator will be whether NATO tries to
avert this by cancelling its annual summit,

scheduled for July 2027.

35The World Ahead 2027 Europe
▸ misses much of the increase in Europe’s
ability to defend itself, and to keep
providing arms to Ukraine. In many cases
this entails building factories to produce
drones, often with the help of Ukrainian
ﬁrms, which are then supplied to Ukraine.
This ensures that countries develop the
industrial capacity to build drones, rather
than simply building up stockpiles of
drones that could soon become obsolete.
Britain, for instance, pledged to supply

120,000 drones to Ukraine in 2026. These
include long-range models that are
striking targets deep inside Russia as well
as interceptors that aim to shoot down
Russian drones.
Though the conﬂict in Ukraine has
highlighted how drones are changing how
wars are fought, it has also demonstrated
that older weapons systems are still
needed. That includes artillery, which is
impervious to electronic warfare, and
indeed to fog and rain, which can ground
or blind small drones. Europe’s production

capacity for 155mm artillery ammunition
has increased ﬁvefold to around 1.6m
rounds a year since 2022, according to the
International Institute for Strategic
Studies, a European think-tank.
Some European countries are retooling
civilian factories to produce armaments.
Volkswagen, a German carmaker, is selling
a struggling car factory to a consortium
that includes Rafael, an Israeli defence
ﬁrm, which plans to use it to make air-
defence missiles. Renault, a French

carmaker, plans to make explosive drones
and army vehicles.
According to Denmark’s defence-
intelligence service, Russia believes that
Europe’s increasing defence capabilities
will pose a threat to it by 2030. That leaves
Russia with a narrow window of
opportunity to intimidate Europe into
halting its support for Ukraine, or to try to
shatter NATO’s unity by escalating hybrid
attacks. This makes 2027 a risky year for
Europe—and one in which its best hope of

deterring Russia lies in accelerating its
rearmament, to close Russia’s window of
opportunity more quickly. ●
The two
Turkeys
F
ROM THE outside looking
in, Turkey has seldom
looked better. The country
has become an arms-industry
juggernaut. It spends only 2%
of GDP on defence, but earns
far more inﬂuence with its
drones, warships and ar-
moured vehicles than most
NATO allies. It has also be-
come a broker, mediating
between Ukraine and Russia,
America and Iran, and Ethio-

pia and Somalia. In relations
with its Arab neighbours,
ideology has taken a back seat
to pragmatism, allowing
Turkey to mend relations with
Saudi Arabia and Egypt,
among others. A decade ago,
President Recep Tayyip
Erdogan and Muhammad bin
Salman, the Saudi crown
prince, were not even on
speaking terms. These days,
after Turkey signed a mutual
defence treaty with the king-
dom, they are sworn allies.
Mr Erdogan is riding a
wave of other foreign-policy

victories. He has persuaded
European leaders to stop ha-
ranguing his government about
its dismal record on the rule of
law. (Donald Trump never
pretended to care, which is one
reason relations with America
are the best for over a decade.)
Mr Erdogan has also declared
victory over the PKK, the Kurd-
ish militia that fought Turkey
for four decades before pledg-
ing to disband and disarm in
exchange for a partial amnesty.
He can also claim to have won

the battle for Syria. Under the
Assads, Syria had been Tur-
key’s most dangerous regional
rival. Since the dictatorship’s
collapse, it has become Tur-
key’s closest Arab partner and a
magnet for its exports, in-
vestment and military advisers.
The trouble for Mr Erdogan,
as 2027 dawns, is that things
are messier at home. The war
on soaring consumer prices,
which he expected to win in
2026, is dragging on. Inﬂation,
which approached triple digits
in 2022, has subsided, but only

to 30%. An investment-fund
scandal, which wiped tens of
billions of dollars from the
country’s stock market and
which regulators allowed to
fester for months, has shaken
conﬁdence in Turkish assets.
The OECD, a rich-country club,
has repeatedly downgraded
Turkey’s growth outlook. It
expects the economy to ex-
pand by 3.6% in 2027, and
inﬂation to remain above 20%.
The tension between these
two Turkeys will grow as elec-
tions approach. Mr Erdogan,

who faces voters in 2028 (per-
haps sooner), will do what he
can, aided by a docile media, to
make the ﬁrst Turkey obscure
the second. The country’s
foreign policy used to be an
elite sport for career diplomats,
presided over by generals. Now
it has turned into a vote-
winner, and a distraction from
problems at home. In 2027
Turks will hear less about the
price of butter and more about
the production of guns.
But Mr Erdogan is taking no
chances. His main rival for the

presidency, Ekrem Imamoglu,
has been locked up since 2025
on trumped-up corruption
charges. The head of the main
opposition has been deposed
by a court verdict and forced
to set up a new party. Prosecu-
tors may go after another
potential rival, Mansur Yavas,
the mayor of Ankara.
The bad news for Mr
Erdogan is that even a third-
choice candidate could give
him a run for his money. So
expect the crackdown on the
opposition to kick up a gear.
The strongman also needs to

clear a legal path to another
term. To run again, he must
call an early election or per-
suade some opposition MPs
to amend the constitution to
allow him a third term.

36 Europe The World Ahead 2027
I
AM SICK and tired of all the
Europe-bashing. For the past
20 years, a standard talking
point for politicians, business
leaders and diplomats has been
that the European Union has
fallen behind America and
China, is about to be overtaken
by India and is doomed to
decline into mediocracy. The
worst thing is that this
argument is often made by
European business leaders.
They complain that Europe is
not competitive, that it lacks

the central pillars of the
artiﬁcial-intelligence stack and
that the EU is too bureaucratic.
There are three problems
with this narrative. The ﬁrst is
that it risks becoming a self-
fulﬁlling prophecy. The second
is that business leaders are the
ones who are supposed to build
competitive companies and
present compelling arguments
for better regulation. And the
third is that it is, generally
speaking, not true.
In 2025 I took part in a
conference where the

moderator asked ChatGPT
about Europe’s competitive-
ness relative to America and
China. The answer was rather
mixed, and less damning than
the actual conversation that
followed. In the tech industry
America is strong on capital,
compute and AI companies.
China is known for research,
industrial deployment and
rapidly advancing AI models.
Europe is good at scientiﬁc
research, engineering and
equipment, with companies
like ASML in semiconductors
and Nokia and Ericsson in

networks. Europe also has a lot
of critical minerals.
After listening to an hour of
EU-bashing, this time mainly
from American tech leaders, I
decided to go to Gemini (yes,
another American AI app) and
ask a different question: to
rank the top ten countries in
terms of quality of life. The
answer got both a laugh and
applause. Nine out of ten
countries were European. I
checked the answers on Claude
as well, by the way, and the
result was roughly the same.

My point is that when you
look at global rankings on
metrics such as democracy,
safety, health care, education,
the environment or work-life
balance, European countries
dominate the top ten in each
case. In the business world,
where metrics are often about
shareholder value, crude
accounting and proﬁt,
competitiveness looks rather
different from what matters in
day-to-day life. But
competitiveness is not only
about growth, and Europe is a
great place to invest.

It is a beacon of stability.
How about those upcoming
elections, you ask? The EU has
27 member states and Europe
has almost 40 democratic
states overall. Elections are
conducted on the local,
national and European level on
a regular basis. The results shift
from right to left and from the
centre to the extremes. That’s
democracy. Live with it.
And yes, the EU can over-
regulate, and be slow and
bureaucratic, but it delivers
predictability, and it is capable

of adapting. When it feels the
security threat from Russia in
the east, what does Europe do?
Supports Ukraine and commits
to raising defence spending to
5% of GDP. When it feels
political pressure from across
the Atlantic, what does it do? It
takes more responsibility for
security, shares the burden
with America and forges free-
trade agreements with India,
Mercosur and many others.
Europe’s defence
awakening is one of the most
signiﬁcant geopolitical shifts

of recent years. After years of
underinvestment, the
continent is rapidly building
strength, evident not just in
rising defence budgets but also
in the transformation of
traditional defence ﬁrms and a
surge of innovative defence-
technology unicorns.
In the post-cold-war era
trade, energy, currency,
technology and competition
policy were supposed to bring
Europe together. In today’s
world they are weaponised and
used as instruments of power.
The EU is waking up to this

reality. In trade, currency and
competition, it has a range of
tools to project geopolitical
power. All nations and regions
are mulling over their strategic
dependencies. In this unstable
world, Europe is for many an
indispensable partner. Many
countries, large and small, are
seeking closer economic,
technological, political and
security ties with Europe.
Europe is strong and is on
the right track. In 2027 it needs
to understand three things.
First, with the world changing

ever faster, picking up speed is
essential. Second, strength
comes from co-operation and
scale: Europe stands united
and falls divided. Third, this is
a world of geopolitical
turmoil—a world Europe did
not envisage or seek. But it
must learn to live in it, while
constructing pathways to a
more multilateral world order.
The coming year will pose
challenges. Every year does.
But Europe is better placed
than ever to face them. ●
Making the case for Europe

In this unstable world,
Europe is widely seen as
an indispensable partner

ONALD TRUMP will surely be the last person to
acknowledge this reality—let alone accept it—but
the midterm elections of 2026 marked the beginning
of the end of the Trump era. He has dominated
American politics for 12 years—but Mr Trump has
proven more effective at disruption than construction,
obliterating norms and assumptions about how basic
institutions of American life should function. Now the
breadth of the repudiation of his leadership at the

midterm elections will force Republicans to confront a
dismal reality: there may be no institution Mr Trump
has damaged more than the Republican Party itself.
With former Republican strongholds such as Texas
and Ohio now looking like swing states, and former
swing states such as Pennsylvania and Georgia
trending Democratic, the Democrats suddenly have
far more open paths to the 270 electoral votes needed
to win the presidency. They also have a new map to

show them the way, thanks to the politicians—such as
Senators-elect Adam Hamilton of Kansas, Josh Turek
of Iowa and James Talarico of Texas—who
demonstrated how to win on once-forbidding terrain.
Republicans, by contrast, are lost in the wilderness.
Early polls have Vice-President J.D. Vance favoured
to be the Republican nominee. He has tried to position
himself as the heir to Mr Trump’s MAGA movement
while at the same time manoeuvring to establish his

own political identity—a treacherous task for any
vice-president. Almost from the start of the Iran war,
anonymous White House sources let it be known that
Mr Vance opposed America’s participation. And in the
wake of the midterms, Mr Vance tried to blame the
outcome on the war.
This characteristically too-clever-by-half strategy is
unlikely to work. The administration Mr Vance helps
lead is not unpopular just because of the war. Potential
challengers, who, sensing weakness, were circling

even before the midterms, will not allow Mr Vance to
evade responsibility for that war (he did not prevent it,
after all), nor for the trade wars, corruption, violent
deportation tactics and assorted proofs of
incompetence that also alienated so many Americans.
Several senators, including Ted Cruz of Texas, John

▸ Kennedy of Louisiana and Rick Scott of Florida, have

they will ﬁnd it far easier to make respectful noises
about Mr Trump while distancing themselves from his
unpopular policies. Their challenge will be hashing out

what, if not Trumpism, their party stands for.
For their part, Democrats must move beyond
simply opposing Trumpism. Their midterm winners
pointed the way by shedding the divisive social
positions of the progressive left while embracing its
populist economics and an emphasis on clean
governance and decency. That creates an opening for a
candidate such as Representative Alexandria
Ocasio-Cortez of New York, a master of social-media
politicking, though she may instead challenge Senator

Chuck Schumer for his seat. Senator John Ossoff, who
easily won re-election in Georgia, enthralled activist
and moderate Democrats alike by attacking Trumpian
corruption. As the rising generation of Democratic
leaders steps forward, primary voters are likely to have
numerous governors and senators to choose among.
But to build on the party’s midterm gains, the
Democrats’ Congressional leadership will have to
deliver on the commitment to bipartisanship made by

those candidates, such as Mr Talarico, who staked out
new terrain. That means not just investigating Mr
Trump and pressing him to end his war but trying to
strike deals with him to reduce living costs, whether of
energy or health care. Americans have shown how
quick they will be to ﬁre public servants who forget
that they are, in fact, servants rather than masters. ●

Trumpism
I
N HIS SECOND term, Donald Trump has

already left an indelible mark on Amer-
ican foreign policy. His unprecedented
threats towards Canada and Denmark
have led to a collapse in trust among NATO
allies. Military raids, strikes and muscle-
ﬂexing in the Caribbean have put the
western hemisphere back at the heart of
American statecraft. And his war on Iran
has done grave damage to America’s mil-
itary stockpiles and its global standing.
Expect a no less radical agenda in 2027.
Mr Trump, bruised from a thumping in

the midterm elections in November, will be
besieged by investigations and hearings on
the home front. Like many a lame-duck
president before him, he will turn to for-
eign policy for solace. His ﬁrst task will be
to deal with the fallout from Iran. The
Pentagon may pull back from its front-line
bases in the Gulf states, which have been
vulnerable to Iranian drones and missiles,
perhaps regrouping in Israel and Jordan.
In Europe, Mr Trump will be tested by

an increasingly aggressive Russia, which is
intensifying its “hybrid” attacks, such as
sabotage and drone incursions. As the Iran
war abates, America will redouble efforts
to ﬁnd a diplomatic settlement in Ukraine,
a task which will become harder if Vladimir
Putin initiates a fresh mobilisation of
Russian soldiers, allowing him to launch
futile ground offensives for longer.
Watch for two positive signs. One is if
Mr Trump grants Ukraine a licence to

produce interceptors for Patriot air-
defence systems. The other is if John
Ratcliffe, the director of the CIA, starts
playing a larger role in negotiations, edg-
ing out Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner,
the envoys who have so far pushed largely
pro-Russian proposals. But don’t hold your
breath: Mr Trump has repeatedly shied
away from turning the screws on Mr Putin.
In Asia, Mr Trump has sought a detente
with China, putting difficult security
issues such as Taiwan and the South China

Sea on the back burner. But having won
control of both chambers of Congress,
Democrats could demand that Mr Trump
proceed with a delayed arms package for
Taiwan, triggering a Chinese response.
Just as 2026 began with a surprise in the
Caribbean, 2027 could be dominated by
America’s squeeze on Cuba, and even a
military attack to topple its regime. Cuba’s
economy is expected to shrink by more
than 5% in 2027, having contracted by 10%
in 2026 amid domestic mismanagement

and an American blockade. Expect Mr
Trump to resort to more gunboat diploma-
cy to extract concessions from the regime,
even if he stops short of invasion.
America’s traditional friends have
become accustomed to Mr Trump’s erratic,
sometimes predatory approach. Canada
will inch closer to Europe, economically
and militarily. The results of a Pentagon
review of troop numbers in Europe will
become clearer, with allies such as Spain
braced for punishment. European allies

will maintain their defence-spending
spree, as they rush to ﬁll gaps left by Amer-
ica’s retrenchment (Germany alone plans
to increase spending by a third in 2027).
And allies will pay close attention to the
country’s domestic politics for signs of
where foreign policy is heading. If J.D.
Vance, the vice-president, who broke
publicly with Mr Trump’s policy on Iran
and Israel on November 4th, looks likely to
earn Mr Trump’s endorsement, allies will

read that as a sign that America’s inward
turn, away from allies, will deepen. Should
Marco Rubio, the secretary of state, stay in
contention, that would give succour to
those who want America to play a more
muscular and interventionist role.
Mr Trump has long kept friends and
foes guessing. With the end in sight, he is
likely to become more mercurial, not less.
But he will be increasingly hemmed in by a
hostile Congress. Both his allies and his
adversaries will be looking to see who, and

what, might be coming next. ●

39The World Ahead 2027 United States
Y
OU COULD call it bad luck. Inﬂation in America
has now been above the Federal Reserve’s 2% target
for nearly six years. Each time it looked like it might be
dipping back down, Donald Trump has done
something to send it up again, whether that be
“Liberation Day” tariffs or starting a war with Iran.
So any hopes for lower inﬂation in 2027 must be
tempered by the knowledge that Mr Trump is still
president, and more than capable of sending prices

soaring again. Even so, 2027 could be the year that
America ﬁnally sees off its post-pandemic inﬂation.
One reason for such optimism is that the Republicans’
drubbing in the midterms may have narrowed the
scope for further chaotic Trumpian interventions.
That raises the enticing possibility that the usual
economic drivers of inﬂation, rather than Mr Trump,
will determine what happens to prices in America.
And on that front there is reason to hope for a break-

through. The Fed has moved from cutting interest
rates to raising them, and has signalled decent odds of
further rises in 2027. So far, Kevin Warsh, whom Mr
Trump appointed as Fed chair last summer,
has—despite his grandiose talk of “regime change”—
largely behaved like an ordinary central banker.
Monetary policy may not be very tight, but nor is it
loose. The splurge of tax giveaways from Mr Trump’s
“One Big Beautiful Bill” has happened, so the ﬁscal

boost that also helped sustain inﬂation in 2026 ought
to fade away. Without control of Congress, Mr Trump
will struggle to offer further goodies on that scale.
America’s jobs market is not likely to be a source of
inﬂation either. The unemployment rate is low by
historical standards, but still above where it was a few
years ago. The ratio of unemployed people to job
vacancies, a common measure of labour-market
conditions, is around its recent average. Wage growth

is, if anything, looking low.
Pockets of America’s economy are certainly still
looking hot. GDP growth has been running at a healthy
2%. Investment in artiﬁcial intelligence is booming.
Share prices have been on a tear. Even with a big rise in
bond yields, overall borrowing conditions have stayed
buoyant. All that might have been enough to raise
inﬂation anyway in 2026, even without the oil crunch.
Those supports should persist during 2027, barring an

unwelcome unwinding of the AI boom.
Pull it all together, then, and the odds of inﬂation
falling look good. Will it go all the way to 2%? Investors
fret that the Fed has learned to live with inﬂation a
little above its target. Mr Warsh has insisted that
“There is no soft implicit target—not on this
committee’s watch. There’s only a target, and it’s 2%.”
Hitting 2% may be a stretch, then, but getting close
ought to be possible. Ordinary Americans may not

notice, though. The reason they are incensed, and have
made “affordability” the political cause of the
moment, is that the absolute level of prices is much
higher than in 2019. Lower inﬂation now will not
scratch that itch. Nor will the fact that wage growth
has more than kept up with inﬂation. But wait long
enough and memories will fade, just not in 2027.
The dip in inﬂation may be only a short reprieve,
however. Government borrowing is unsustainable and

nothing that happens in 2027 is likely to change that.
But debt crises take a long time to brew. Eventually the
government may decide that devaluing its debts
through inﬂation would be less painful than raising
taxes or cutting popular social programmes. But that is
a problem for another year. ●

accomplished?
This could be the year America
ﬁnally beats inﬂation

O
N JANUARY 3RD Democrats will take
the majority in the House and Senate.
With that will come control of the agenda,
power of the purse and, crucially,
subpoenas. In lieu of actual lawmaking in
2027—no one anticipates much of that
when Donald Trump wields veto
power—expect a deluge of investigations
into corruption within the government and
what Democrats have taken to calling the
Trump “crime family”.
There will be probes of policy,

personnel and alleged pay-offs. There will
be hearings galore: about shady pardons,
the fatal shooting of protesters in
Minneapolis, miscalculations in Iran, the
Qatari jet, the ballroom and the ﬁrms that
underwrote it, the “anti-weaponisation”
fund and more. Democrats hope scrutiny
and their control over the budget will force
Mr Trump to make concessions on Iran,
deportations and tariffs.
Extracting information from the
administration itself will be hard. Mr

Trump and his staff will defy subpoenas
and assert executive privilege. So instead
Democrats will target ﬁrms and
individuals who patronised it while
winning contracts, pardons, regulatory
relief or merger approval. The idea is that
naming, shaming and hauling third parties
before Congress will act as a deterrent.
Given all the fertile material that
Democrats could potentially probe, their
challenge will be to pick judiciously and
craft a compelling narrative for the public.

They will need to connect corruption in
government to costs borne by American
taxpayers. They will be most persuasive if
they are not seen to be pursuing a political
vendetta. As Brian Schatz, likely to be the
Democratic whip in the Senate in 2027,
told the Bulwark, an online publication: “I
think it’s fair to say that people don’t want
us waking up every morning thinking
about how we’re gonna screw Donald
Trump. They want us thinking about how
we’re gonna reduce everyone’s prices.”

That raises the big question of whether
Democrats will try to impeach Mr Trump
for a third time. House backbenchers will
clamour for it. Democratic congressmen
have already introduced at least seven
impeachment resolutions since the
president’s return to office. In September,
one of them got backing from 147
Democrats (about 70% of the caucus) in an
unsuccessful procedural vote.
In private, many Democrats admit
impeachment would be counter-
productive. The Senate will not remove Mr

Trump—that would require Republican
support for a two-thirds majority—and the
whole business reinforces partisanship. Yet
there will be appetite for it among the
Democratic base and lawmakers in safe
seats. Hakeem Jeffries, the presumptive
speaker of the House, may satisfy them by
instead putting more of Mr Trump’s
cabinet secretaries through the wringer.
Pete Hegseth, the secretary of war, looks
particularly vulnerable, assuming he still
holds that post come January.

An obsessive focus on accountability
will bring diminishing returns for
Democrats. The president is largely
immune from prosecution, a result of a
Supreme Court decision in 2024. His staff
will probably be, too—do not be surprised
if Mr Trump issues blanket pardons to
everyone in his orbit, foreclosing criminal
inquiries by a future Democratic
administration. The takeaway for
Democrats is that going after him and his
allies will not yield what they so badly

want. Instead they will have to put forward
a positive agenda of their own.
Democrats will thrash out the future
direction of their party in what is sure to be
a lively and contentious presidential
primary. If anything, though, the midterm
elections showed that Democrats can,
after all, do two things at once. They can
ﬁght among themselves over policy; but
they can also unite to wallop Republicans
in an election. ●

the swamp
Divided government will mean little

legislation—but lots of investigations
Kennett Werner Washington correspondent,

N HIS SECOND term, Donald Trump’s
obsession with immigration has
manifested as an attempt to “launch the
largest deportation programme in
American history”. Initially, the campaign
was heavy on theatrics and light on results.
Immigration and Customs Enforcement
(ICE) agents assaulted protesters,
separated immigrant parents from their
watching children and killed two

American citizens. Lately, however, the
administration’s tactics have been quieter
and more effective. ICE agents are setting
monthly arrest records and immigration
judges are issuing removal orders at an
astounding clip. Republican hardliners
want to pick up the pace in 2027.
Yet while ICE steps up deportations, it is
worth dwelling on the president’s longer-
standing immigration obsession. Mr
Trump ﬁrst began talking about building a
“big, beautiful” border wall—and forcing

Mexico to pay for it—more than a decade
ago. Now a steel and concrete structure is
rising across the desert. This high-tech
“smart wall” will include buoys ﬂoating in
the Rio Grande and huge surveillance
towers as well as the wall itself: vertical
black beams more than 9 metres (30 feet)
tall. It will cost nearly $47bn. The
construction frenzy to ﬁnish the wall by
2028 will ignite conﬂict with locals and
change the face of America’s southern

The seeds of conﬂict have already been

communities interested in keeping the
border as it is. Protests have cropped up in
places where building has begun. In
southern Arizona, for instance, protesters
camped near 200-year-old cottonwood
trees for weeks to try to prevent them from
being bulldozed to make way for the wall.
Customs and Border Protection (CBP) has

started construction on the Tohono
O’odham nation, a Native American
reservation, despite the tribe’s objections.
“It’s like they’re trying to get rid of us,” says
Keanu Stevens, a youth-council member.
Expect more protests as building speeds
up and spreads out.
Others worry about the wall’s impact
on the environment. Construction in the
borderlands has been made easier in recent
decades by bills that let the federal govern-
ment waive landmark environmental laws,

such as the Endangered Species Act.
Arizonans tell tales of the endangered
jaguars that wander back and forth
between Sonora, Mexico and Arizona—the
most famous of which was called “El Jefe”
(”the boss”). Conservationists have long
hoped that the big cats might re-establish a
breeding population in America. But a wall
meant to keep migrants out will block
jaguars and other creatures, too.
Another migrant surge in 2027 is
unlikely, but not because of the wall.

Migrant encounters at the border are
already low (see chart on previous page).
They dropped sharply at the end of Joe
Biden’s term when he restricted asylum. Mr
Trump clamped down further, and his
deportation campaign is now proving to be
a successful deterrent.
In 2027 those in both parties eyeing the
presidency will begin to sketch out their
positions on immigration. Some
Republicans will try to distance them-
selves from Mr Trump’s aggressive
mass-deportation policy, which many

Americans think has gone too far, but they
will unanimously support greater border
security. For their part, Democrats face a
quandary. As usual, they will be tempted to
do the opposite of Mr Trump in all things.
But any perceived leniency on immigration
could inspire migrants to embark upon the
long, treacherous journey north—wall or
no wall—with all the political strife that
would bring. ●

EBATING REPUBLICANS’ massive tax
and spending bill in the summer of

2025, Mike Johnson, the House speaker,
assured Americans that it would “secure
and strengthen” the social safety-net “by
eliminating fraud, waste and abuse”. In
2027, as many of the deepest cuts from the
One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) kick in,
voters will be able to judge for themselves.
Changes are coming to numerous
programmes. Compared with what would
have been spent, the OBBBA will reduce
federal spending on Medicaid, the
health-care programme for the poor, by

about 7% in 2027, according to the
Congressional Budget Office (CBO), a
non-partisan scorekeeper (see chart). In
combination with the ending of subsidies
for the Affordable Care Act (“Obamacare”)
marketplace insurance, the number of
Americans without health coverage will
rise from 26.6m in 2025 to 34m in 2027, the
CBO calculates.
Meanwhile, almost 15% less will be
spent on the Supplemental Nutrition
Assistance Programme (SNAP), also known
as food stamps. Together, the cuts amount

to the most signiﬁcant changes to these
programmes in decades.
Start with Medicaid, which provided
insurance for a ﬁfth of Americans in 2025.
From January 1st 2027, certain recipients
will have to work 80 hours a month in order
to receive the beneﬁt. Mr Johnson
defended such work requirements as
“good for [recipients’] dignity”. But just 8%
of working-age, able-bodied Medicaid
recipients are unemployed without a
reason (like being at university).

Though there will be exemptions for
those caring for children or too sick to
work, implementing the requirements will
create bureaucratic hurdles which many
will fail to overcome. When Arkansas tried
to bring in work requirements during
Donald Trump’s ﬁrst administration, most
of the people who lost coverage were not
bingeing daytime television, but rather
could not keep up with the paperwork.
The federal government has not made
the roll-out any easier. The law allows

people with certain conditions (such as

▸ cancer or addiction) to be exempted, but
administrators had also required them to
show that the condition “signiﬁcantly
impairs” their ability to work. This would
have meant setting up a new standard, and
training doctors on it, in just a few months.
Courts blocked the rule after Democratic-
led states sued, but the legal back-and-
forth has made setting up the new regime
all the messier.
Other changes are coming to health

care, too. In October 2027, stricter federal
funding rules for Medicaid will come into
effect, scaling down support for the
programme. Funding was already falling
for plans bought through the Obamacare
marketplaces: subsidies were boosted
during the pandemic but expired at the
end of 2025. In 2026, 5m stopped buying
coverage, according to the CBO. In 2027,
another 4m will drop off. People without
insurance are more likely to go without
care (even vital things like prescriptions or

surgeries) and face ﬁnancial risks.
Cuts to food stamps will also start in
2027. In the past the federal government
has paid the full cost of the beneﬁt, but
from October states will start chipping in
up to 15%. The amount they will pay is
determined by the size of their over- or
underpayments in previous years. This
sounds reasonable, but the food-stamp
formula is ﬁnicky and changes based on
hours worked or other expenses.
States have been adding bureaucracy

and tightening access in anticipation.
Nationwide, enrolment is down by 5.5m
since July 2025, including at least 1.5m
children. “It’s just really, really poor policy,”
says Lauren Bauer of the Brookings
Institution, a think-tank. “We are seeing
dramatic declines in rolls—far greater than
what should have been anticipated.”
States can step in to ﬁll the gap left by
cuts. But, unlike the federal government,
states have to balance their budgets, which

means they must either raise taxes or cut
services they provide in other areas.
Democrats in Congress will try to reverse
the cuts, but ﬁnding the money will be
tricky. Benjamin Sommers, a health
economist at Harvard University, sees the
cuts as deliberately aligned. They are a
crisis “brought on by the willingness of the
administration to make people’s lives
much more complicated”, he sighs. “And to
add a lot of red tape.” ●
I
N “THE WRONG PARIS”, a rom-com on

Netﬂix, a young woman thinks she is
signing up for a reality-TV show in France
but ends up in Paris, Texas. But in the real
world, a growing number of viewers are
choosing to escape to cowboy country.
Taylor Sheridan’s “Yellowstone”, a
series about ranchers, and its spinoffs have
proved popular in places with no wild
mustangs, including Britain and France.
Inspired by the success of Mr Sheridan’s
“Westernverse”, more saddle-packed series
will stream in 2027: 42 Western-themed TV

shows and ﬁlms were commissioned in
America between January 2025 and June
2026, 45% more than in the same period
ﬁve years earlier, according to Ampere
Analysis, a research ﬁrm. Westerns coming
to the small screen in 2027 include Net-
ﬂix’s adaptation of “Lonesome Dove”, a
bestselling novel by Larry McMurtry.
People do not just want to watch cow-
boys, but to listen to them, too. In the past
ﬁve years Americans’ streams of country
music on Spotify are up 90%. Globally

streams have more than doubled. The
fastest-growing markets on Spotify are
Nigeria, South Korea and Egypt.
When Ella Langley croons about
“Choosin’ Texas” (a top song of 2026), she
captures the mood of the moment. Line
dancing has become fashionable among a
younger set. In the past ﬁve years searches
worldwide for “cowboy boots” and “medal-
lion belt” on Google have risen by 80% and
450% respectively. Vogue identiﬁed “west-
ern” as a top fashion trend of 2027, and

designers are piling in.
What has spurred the stampede? Cow-
boy culture evokes alluring open land,
self-sufficiency and connection to nature.
It is the opposite of the indoor screen life
lived by Gen Z and millennials. Celebrities
opting for country sounds and looks are
also more diverse than in the old days.
Beyoncé released a country album and can
now claim the top-grossing country con-
cert tour ever. Even K-pop stars, including
those from BTS and Blackpink, have em-

braced the “Wild West” aesthetic.
Cowboys often beckon during tense
times. “Spaghetti Westerns” (named for
their Italian directors) were all the rage in
the 1960s-70s, as audiences grappled with
social and political upheaval. The simplic-
ity of country songs—about beer, broken
promises and true love—offers a distrac-
tion from inﬂation, AI and elections. “All
hat, no cattle” used to be an insult. Today—
and in 2027—it’s a description of the many

people enthralled with the American
south-west. The herd is only growing. ●

T
HIS IS A time of uncommon
and increasing instability.
The world has shifted from
American hegemony to
multipolarity, globalisation to
economic nationalism, belief in
progress to fear about the
future—and with such speed
that the way things used to be
seems almost forgotten.
Today’s world is unlike
anything seen since the end of
the second world war. The cold
war, and the American unipolar
moment that followed it, were

not peaceful times, but were to
some extent predictable. Now
prediction, never mind
certainty, is a mug’s game.
Ominously, the situation is
somewhat similar to that at the
beginning of the 20th century.
Back then, people were also
turning against forms of
globalisation that did not work
for them. The hegemony of
empires was being challenged
by new powers and resistance
to colonialism. Nationalism
and racism abounded, and acts
of terror multiplied. Tensions

rose until ﬁnally, in the summer
of 1914, a terrorist attack in
Sarajevo set off the ﬁrst world
war. That cataclysmic conﬂict,
in turn, led to a global
depression and, within a few
years, a second world war, even
more terrifying than the ﬁrst.
What can be done to
prevent a similar scenario
playing out in the late 2020s?
The answer lies in a blend of
compromise and counter-
balance that was notably
absent in 1914. Back then, war
broke out because the great

powers refused to settle, and
smaller countries relied on
them for their own security.
Today it is up to political
leaders—and, where possible,
their publics—to ensure that
does not happen again.
Willingness to compromise
is conspicuously missing from
today’s great-power relations.
Just like before 1914, leaders
want things their own way,
even when that is blatantly
impossible. Persistence is
regarded as a virtue, and
ﬂexibility and trade-offs as

paths to defeat.
Donald Trump, touted as a
dealmaking president, is
bogged down by trade wars,
conﬂicts with allies, and a real
war of his own making.
Vladimir Putin, supposedly a
hard-bitten realist, is unwilling
to end a war that is costing
Russia almost 2,000 dead and
wounded per day. Both fear the
perception of weakness more
than the consequences of war.
Xi Jinping, once hoped to be a
reformer, has taken a
maximalist, uncompromising
approach both domestically

and towards China’s relations
with its neighbours.
At the same time the
weaponisation of trade
dependencies, and the use of
tariffs and export controls,
threaten global growth and
make it harder for countries to
co-operate on universal issues
such as climate change,
poverty alleviation, global
health and the regulation of
artiﬁcial intelligence.
If wars and occupations in
Ukraine, Iran and the
Palestinian territories are
allowed to roll on, they will,

ultimately, give rise to much
larger conﬂagrations, just as
happened a century ago. In the
cold war, ideology and
bipolarity created buffers
against great-power conﬂict.
Today the potential for wars to
spread and spin out of control
is real and immediate.
For America, ﬁnding a way
to end the war with Iran is the
priority. Brokering an end to
Russia’s war in Ukraine will be
more difficult, but is no less in
America’s strategic interest,
because it could stabilise

relations with both Europe and
Russia, and weaken the Russia-
China axis. As for Taiwan,
America and China could agree
that the status quo is the best
that can be achieved for now,
and neither independence nor
takeover are viable options.
Small and medium-size
powers also have a role to play,
by acting as a counter-balance
to great-power domination. In
today’s multipolar world, great
powers do not feel bound by
international norms. That
means smaller powers in

regions under threat need to
band together for their own
security. This is especially true
for Europe and South-East
Asia, for which America can no
longer be deemed a trust-
worthy ally. Such regions need
integrated defence systems to
protect their sovereignty and
help keep the peace.
Can a repeat of the follies of
the past be avoided? The
ultimate test is whether the
great powers can put their own
houses in order. Back in 1914
the powers marched to war, not

because they felt strong and
buoyant, but because their
leaders feared domestic
weakness and division—and
worried that their positions
would only weaken in future.
Such anxieties are all too
visible in Washington, Moscow
and Beijing today, and in a
good few other places too. In
2027 leaders in all these
capitals must strive to avoid a
combination of fear and
resentment pushing the world
towards the precipice of a new
global conﬂict. ●
How to avoid another world war

Ominously, today’s
situation is somewhat
similar to that at the start
of the 20th century

The Americas
L
ONG FORGOTTEN, Latin America is now top of the
agenda in Washington. The attention from
President Donald Trump and Marco Rubio, the
Cuban-American secretary of state, involves a new
version of an old idea: the Monroe Doctrine of the 19th
century. This purported to protect Latin America from
interference by foreign (mostly European) powers but
morphed into the idea that the United States could
freely interfere to pursue its own interests.

The latest version, dubbed the Donroe Doctrine,
has a little of the former (with China the biggest worry)
and a big dose of the latter. It reached extraordinary
heights in January 2026 with the seizure of Venezuela’s
strongman, Nicolás Maduro, by US special forces, and
the installation of his deputy, Delcy Rodríguez, as a
pliant authoritarian who then did a big, dodgy oil deal
with Mr Trump. His administration’s strikes on alleged
drug-trafficking boats have continued, too, along with

the strangling of communist Cuba’s economy.
In 2027 the United States’ relations with Latin
America will be shaped by two contradictory forces.
Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, a left winger, won re-election
in Brazil, but the Trump administration will enjoy
strong ideological alignment in the region after wins
for right-wingers in Peru and Colombia in 2026 added
to many others already in office, from Argentina to El
Salvador. That should help it hit drug gangs and keep

out China. Yet the loss of both houses of Congress to
the Democrats in the midterm elections means Mr
Trump’s actions will face much greater scrutiny.
Exactly how these two dynamics play out will vary.
Consider Venezuela. Negotiations sponsored by the
United States are under way to lay the groundwork for
presidential elections. But they may not come until
2028. In 2027 there should at least be changes to the
courts, the repeal of the most repressive laws used

against opposition politicians, and the announcement
of an election timetable. That might include regional
or even parliamentary elections later in the year.
Democrats, and some Republicans, will call for
elections to be held sooner. Democrats will also
scrutinise the ﬂow of Venezuelan oil money that the

▸ US controls. Oil production should gradually increase,
boosting the economy. But it is unlikely to happen fast
enough to convince Messrs Trump and Rubio
triumphantly to bring forward elections, or so slowly
as to generate big protests that might force them to.
The biggest unknown is María Corina Machado, the
opposition leader. After many blocked attempts to
return to Venezuela, she returned home in October
2026. That could galvanise the streets and add

pressure for elections. But much depends on the
extent to which she is willing to break with Mr Trump.
His war on drug gangs will change, too. There will
probably be fewer of the (almost certainly illegal)
strikes on speedboats. Instead, expect a focus on
boarding and removing the crews from larger
refuelling and logistics vessels, before sinking them.
Meanwhile, US military operations against gangs
on land in Latin America will probably increase. This is

already happening in Ecuador—a raid in September
involved 50 US soldiers and stacks of high-tech gear.
Pete Hegseth, the secretary of war, says Colombia and
Honduras have also agreed to joint military operations
on their soil. Mr Rubio has asked the 18 Latin countries
in the Shield of the Americas, a coalition against drug
gangs, to designate two dozen gangs and cartels as
terrorist organisations. Many see this as a way to
provide legal and political cover for greater US military

involvement in raids against such groups.
The Trump administration will apply further
pressure to the left-wing government in Mexico to
allow more American action in the ﬁght against gangs.
And Daniel Ortega’s dictatorship in Nicaragua could
face much tougher American sanctions in the coming
year, especially after he said that there will be no more
elections at all. (One was due in November 2027.)
But the greatest uncertainty hangs over Cuba.
America’s strategy of harsh economic pressure and

hints of military action, while dangling possible talks
about a change in leadership, has not worked so far. Mr
Trump may try to cut a less radical deal. In September
Mr Rubio, whose own future appears uncertain, said
there are people in the regime whose hands are not
“stained with blood”, that America could work with.
With Mr Trump in charge, anything could happen. ●

“I
AM NOT proposing a third
bloc in order to become a
great-power rival, only with
better manners,” Mark Carney
told the European Parliament
in September, as Canada
moved towards a closer rela-
tionship with Europe. The

prime minister did not need
to specify which country was
the subject of his jibe. Canada
has been a constant target of
Donald Trump’s scorn and
tariffs in the president’s sec-
ond term. In 2027 Mr Carney
will need to show Canadians
that his efforts to reduce
dependence are succeeding.
But he must also pursue
knotty negotiations with the
Trump administration to
renew the continental free-
trade pact that Mr Trump
negotiated in 2018. Those
talks will be complicated by

the fact that Mr Trump’s
effort to portray Canada as a
manipulator of that agree-
ment has backﬁred. Voters in
American swing states were
irked at increased costs when
tariffs were imposed on Cana-
dian goods. Mr Carney found
himself ﬂush with cards to
play that Mr Trump kept
insisting he didn’t have. No
one advising the prime min-
ister expects the president to
take this setback gracefully.
Mr Carney has signalled
that he is willing to wait for
Mr Trump to come to him and

that he is prepared to wait out
the two remaining years of
the Trump presidency to get a
satisfactory deal. But that is
probably a bluff. Canada’s
effective tariff rate for access
to the 340m people in the
world’s most valuable con-
sumer market is around 8%,
even with onerous sectoral
tariffs, but Mr Trump has
threatened additional tariffs
starting on January 1st 2027.
He will remain a menace to
Canada’s economy, despite
losing control of Congress.

So Mr Carney will have to
deal with Mr Trump even as
he seeks to reduce depen-
dence. The share of exports
sent southwards fell as low as
66% in mid-2026—the lowest
in 30 years. Railways, roads,
pipelines and defence pacts
were built on a north-south
grid, knitting the neighbours
together. Mr Trump has
popped those old stitches.
Meanwhile, Mr Carney has
inked new trade deals with
China and Indonesia, and a
deal with India is due to be
signed by the end of 2026.

Negotiations with the Euro-
pean Union on closer associa-
tion should conclude in June.
It will be one of more than 20
deals struck since Mr Carney
took office two years ago.
He wants to double Cana-
da’s non-US trade to C$600bn
($430bn) in 10 years. To reach
this ambitious goal, Mr Car-
ney must boost Canada’s
feeble growth. That means
streamlining regulations to
unlock foreign investment to
build roads and ports needed
to extract critical minerals

and energy resources.
Building this infrastruc-
ture and diversifying the
economy will take decades.
The United States will always
be Canada’s largest trading
partner. Mr Carney’s chal-
lenge for 2027, and beyond, is
to start to reorient Canada’s
economy to allow genuine
freedom of action.

W
HEN HE IS inaugurated on January
5th, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva of
Brazil will join an exclusive club of
presidents worldwide who have been
democratically elected to four terms. He
will be able to shape power for a
generation, thanks to an unusually large
number of vacancies in Brazil’s regulatory
agencies and its Supreme Court. Given
that his Workers’ Party (PT) has already
held power for 18 of the last 24 years, Lula,

as the president is known, has done more
than any other person to deﬁne modern
Brazil. Now aged 81, this is his last dance.
What can be expected?
To guess what Lula’s ﬁnal term might
look like, it is helpful to consider how his
political philosophy has evolved, from his
early life of poverty and his time as a
left-wing union leader. Having failed in
three elections, he ﬁnally secured the
presidency in 2002 by moderating his
radicalism. In his ﬁrst term he appointed

capable centrists to lead the central bank
and economy ministry. He was fortunate
that a booming China wanted Brazilian
commodities, and he used the money to
pay off Brazil’s foreign debts, expand
welfare schemes and slow deforestation.
But in his second term, Lula got carried
away with a cosy industrial policy which
delivered subsidised loans to politically
friendly businessmen. A corruption
scandal eventually landed him in jail (his
convictions were later overturned).

Prison and old age further tempered his
radicalism, with loyalty emerging as his
guiding principle. In his third term, from
2023, he surrounded himself ever more
with loyalists, appointed his own lawyer to
the Supreme Court and defended Vladimir
Putin and Nicolás Maduro, Venezuela’s
former autocrat. He doubled down on his
industrial policy and revved up public
spending. This boosted growth but forced
the central bank to raise interest rates to
crushing levels, as debt increased by more

than ten percentage points. Centrists who
had voted for him felt sidelined.
What now? Lula secured his fourth
term partly by embracing a style of
social-media campaigning that he had
never used before. Now he must grapple
with a transformed country that he does
not understand well. Right-wing parties
swept Congress. The economy is once
again driven mainly by agriculture, the
only sector with productivity gains.
Organised labour is weak. Growing
numbers of gig-workers and the

self-employed ﬁnd the big state a burden
not a help. More of the faithful are
evangelicals; Lula’s base is Catholic.
His preoccupation will be his legacy.
He wants to be remembered as a champion
of the poor, of democracy and of the
environment. Doubling down on populism
is not how to do it. He needs to get serious
about ﬁscal reform. Only lower interest
rates and more competition can unleash
animal spirits and lift the poor. And only
impartial appointments to agencies and

the Supreme Court can strengthen
democracy. Brazil has changed. Lula’s
approach must change, too. ●
Deﬁning an era
What to expect from Lula’s
fourth and ﬁnal term
Ana Lankes Brazil bureau chief,
The Economist
J
AVIER MILEI has achieved a lot. He
inherited an economic mess. Inﬂation
was running at 13% per month before he
took office in late 2023. Mr Milei, wielding
the chainsaw he campaigned with,
dramatically cut spending. That has
helped pull inﬂation down to about 2% per

month. This in turn reduced poverty. He
has also cut swathes of red tape, freeing up
the economy. Voters rewarded Mr Milei for
all this in midterm elections in 2025.
Yet many analysts and prediction
markets now put Mr Milei’s chances of
re-election in October 2027 at around 50%.
The biggest reason is that an economy isn’t
just about inﬂation. After a painful
contraction in 2024, the economy grew in
2025, should do so in 2026 and is forecast
to grow in 2027, too. That would be a big

achievement. But the government
originally budgeted for 5% growth in 2026,
and looks set for a mere 1%.
The growth is uneven. Oil, mining and
agriculture are booming, but sectors that
employ more people, such as industry and
construction, are struggling. As a result,
there are hundreds of thousands fewer
Crunch time
for Milei

▸ formal jobs in the private sector than when
Mr Milei took office. Real wages in the

Voters now worry more about wages and

scandals have hurt him, and his expletive-
laden aggression turns off some voters.
His re-election in 2027 depends on
avoiding an economic crisis and improving
growth, jobs and wages. There is still time

to do that. The bigger question is whether
he will focus on jobs and wages, even if that
slows the ﬁght against inﬂation.
In an interview with The Economist, Mr
Milei denied that this trade-off even exists.
Yet his team seems to be quietly debating
it, and there are a few signs they are
prioritising growth. The most important is
a minor relaxation of the money supply,
which has allowed the peso to modestly
depreciate. A more substantial shift would

help business, especially industry and
construction, by boosting lending and
making exports more competitive.
Mr Milei’s chances are helped by a
divided, weak opposition. Within
Peronism, the main leftist opposition, the
likely front-runner is Axel Kicillof, the
governor of the province of Buenos Aires
He is closely associated with the spending
excesses of the past and his net approval
rating is as bad as Mr Milei’s. He also lacks
the backing of Cristina Fernández de

Kirchner, a former president who is under
house arrest for fraud but is still inﬂuential.
Sergio Massa, whom Mr Milei defeated last
time, may run again, as may Juan Grabois, a
ﬁrebrand congressman.
Many others are considering a
third-party run. Hernán Lacunza, a
centre-right former ﬁnance minister,
promises free-market economics, without
the dogmatism and swearing. Myriam
Bregman, a Trotskyite leftist who has the
highest approval rating of any political

ﬁgure, may stand too.
The rules of the game will also matter.
Mr Milei is trying to eliminate the
mandatory, nationwide open primary
before the election. With no primary, two
Peronists running in the ﬁrst round would
split the vote and boost his chances of
victory without a run-off. However, his
electoral reforms may not pass. It would be
better, and wiser, for him to focus on
getting the economy roaring. ●
O
N FEBRUARY 28TH Salvadorans will go
to the polls to vote for a president.

Nayib Bukele, who was ﬁrst elected in 2019,
will almost certainly be re-elected for a
third time. Since taking office he has
stacked the courts, tamed the security
forces and rewritten the constitution to
scrap term limits.
The justiﬁcation for his authori-
tarianism, and for his popularity, is his
success at crushing crime. Using a
heavy-handed approach—85,000 were
jailed, often on ﬂimsy charges, and mass
trials are only just beginning—Mr Bukele

reduced El Salvador’s murder rate from 51
per 100,000 in 2018 to less than two.
Mr Bukele also enriches himself and his
inner circle. But his tough-on-crime
approach has inspired other politicians to
follow. In Colombia, Abelardo de la
Espriella, a hardline right-winger, won the
election in 2026 on a promise to crush
crime and gangs. In Peru, Keiko Fujimori
was inaugurated in July promising an “iron
ﬁst” and a mega-prison modelled on the
one Mr Bukele built in El Salvador. In

Ecuador, Daniel Noboa has used his
country’s security crisis to bar opposition
parties and intimidate the constitutional
court. There will be more examples in 2027.
Donald Trump has boosted this group,
and gone further. American forces are
likely to continue striking boats in the
region that Mr Trump says are being used
by “narco terrorists”. His new alliance
encompassing 18 regional governments,
the Shield of the Americas, may expand.
Mexico shows that the erosion of

democracy does not require a right-wing
government or a security crackdown. It has
elected half its judiciary by popular vote,
on a turnout of just 13%, and will elect the
other half in 2027, entrenching the ruling
Morena party’s grip on the courts that were
supposed to check it.
Will voters keep voting for this in 2027?
Polling for AmericasBarometer found that
65% of Latin Americans now call demo-
cracy the best system of government, up by
six points since 2023, the ﬁrst rise in a

generation. Yet the same survey found that
large majorities also favour “illiberal
majoritarianism”: an elected leader, but
one unencumbered by courts, opposition
or minority rights. It is part of a broader
global pattern of disillusionment.
Yet the signals are mixed. The same poll
found that in Argentina, Chile and
Uruguay, support for democracy still
tracks support for the liberal safeguards—
trustworthy courts, a free press, minority
rights—that are meant to come with it.

Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva defeated a
would-be strongman in Brazil. In
Guatemala, an anti-corruption president
has been taking on entrenched elites.
And it is clear that nobody likes an
old-school dictator. Daniel Ortega,
Nicaragua’s 80-year-old autocrat who has
ruled since 2007, commands no real
popularity at home. The most exportable
model for 2027 will still be Mr Bukele’s:
authoritarianism that delivers. ●

the tough guys

L
ATIN AMERICA is generally thought of as a place of
youngsters. But, taken as a bloc, the region will in
2027 enjoy its last year of demographic bonus. In 2028
the number of dependants will outgrow the working-
age population for the ﬁrst time in six decades,
according to the UN. By then, 11% of people in the
region will be over 65, and 15% over 60.
That may sound modest compared with Japan,
where 30% of people are over 65, or Italy, where about a

quarter are. But Latin America is the region where the
median age has increased the most since 2000. Other
parts of the world are older, but it is ageing faster.
And unlike many ageing places that got rich ﬁrst,
Latin America has not. The region is less developed
and less healthy than Europe or much of East Asia.
Many fear it did not make full use of its bonus while it
lasted. Between 1965 and 1990, when East Asia rode the
same demographic wave, its GDP per person grew by

around 6% a year; Latin America’s grew by only 1.2% a
year between 2000 and 2024. East Asia used the
windfall to invest in education and female
employment; Latin America mostly did not.
Supporting more dependants with ﬂat or declining
wealth is hard. Pensions are inadequate, particularly in
countries such as Mexico where more than half of
workers toil in the informal sector. Fully 96% of
Japanese over 60 draw a public pension. In Latin
America contributory pensions cover less than half of

the workforce. Non-contributory pensions are
expensive. As a percentage of GDP, the region spends
almost as much on health as its wealthier counterparts,
though less on a per-person basis.
Ageing societies have social costs, too. From the
United States, some pensioners have moved to Central
and Latin America for cheap care in their old age. But
few Latin Americans can afford such care themselves,
and public alternatives are scarce. Unpaid care work

(usually by women) is estimated to be equivalent to a
quarter of GDP in some countries, and holds back
female participation in the labour force.
Some countries are taking action. Colombia’s
overhaul of its pensions will come into force in 2027.
Among other things, it will extend basic coverage to
those who never had a pension. Costa Rica has taken
steps to integrate migrant populations. It aims to
formalise more of its migrant workforce, of whom 58%

are already employed, as a way of plugging gaps left by
locals. Mexico City has passed its own care law, a ﬁrst
step towards the national system the government is
aiming for. Argentina is trying to raise the retirement
age for women to match that for men by 2030.
But it is not enough. Such measures must go
further, and faster. Uruguay offers a model: in 2015 it
launched a care system covering children, disabled
people and pensioners. The underlying ﬁxes are ones

the region has long struggled with: productivity,
decent public education, better infrastructure and
adopting new technology faster.
There may be some silver linings. Smaller cohorts
of children mean more can be spent on each one: the
UN reckons the region’s school-age population will
shrink by 38m by 2050, which could be a chance to
raise educational quality. Labour shortages are pulling
in young migrants, mostly Venezuelans, who can ease
the crunch. Whether the region can not just cope but

prosper will depend on the pace of change. ●

I
T IS NOT often that America sets up a new military
command overseas these days, but in 2027 the
Indo-Paciﬁc region will get two. In Japan a new
headquarters will be staffed up to take charge of all
American troops in the country. And in Australia,
American submarines will sail from a naval base south
of Perth, the ﬁrst new American submarine port
abroad in half a century. The moves are big upgrades
for two of America’s most important allies in Asia as

they prepare for the possibility that China will try to
conquer Taiwan.
That America is doubling down on these alliances
may come as a surprise at a time when Donald Trump’s
administration is bogged down in a war against Iran
and is becoming an increasingly ﬁckle patron of
Europe’s security. But America has been a steadier
friend to Asia than to Europe in Mr Trump’s second
term. His focus on Europe and the Middle East has
mostly left China hawks in the Pentagon and among

America’s military brass free to quietly implement
plans long on the books. Both the submarine base and
the new Japan HQ were initiated under Joe Biden.
This has come as a relief in the region. Unlike
Uncle Sam’s browbeaten NATO allies, who boast their
own impressive arms industries and two nuclear
powers, Asian allies have no Plan B if America were to
abandon its promises to defend them. They have
instead courted Mr Trump assiduously, promising to

invest hundreds of billions of dollars in America. South
Korea even gave Mr Trump a golden crown in 2025.
Much of this ﬂattery seems to have worked. But that is
not to say that Asian allies are breathing easily. There
are two big risks on the horizon.
The ﬁrst is the emergence of a two-tier system of
alliances in Asia. Already in 2026 America’s new
national-defence strategy announced that it would
increase its commitment to the “ﬁrst island chain”, the

string of archipelagos stretching from Japan to the
Philippines. Some analysts interpret the strategy as an
indication that Thailand and South Korea, allies on the
Asian continent, now lie outside America’s new
defensive perimeter. That would not be a big deal for
Chain reaction
