▸ Thailand, which has distanced itself from America
since the end of the cold war. But it would be a seismic
shift for South Korea. Pentagon boffins say they
merely want South Korea to take more responsibility
for its own security. But a similar exclusion by Dean
Acheson, America’s secretary of state in 1950, led
North Korea to invade the South.
A decision is also looming in South Korea in
relation to “opcon”, the question of who would

operationally control South Korea’s armed forces in a
conﬂict with the North. For decades this responsibility
rested with an American general. Left-wingers in
South Korea like President Lee Jae Myung have long
campaigned to have it transferred to one of their own.
Now, officials in the Trump administration are
working to give Mr Lee and his crowd what they want,
though some American military officers believe South
Korea is not ready. A decision in 2027 to transfer opcon

might foreshadow American troop withdrawals, a
longtime goal of Mr Trump’s. Such mixed signals could
invite Kim Jong Un, North Korea’s dictator, to
challenge the South.
The other risk is Mr Trump’s own unpredictable
impulses. His ill-judged war against Iran has drained
munitions needed to deter Chinese aggression and
forced the redeployment of some navy ships from the
Paciﬁc to the Persian Gulf. An escalation there in early
2027 could do yet more damage.

Mr Trump also seems reluctant to defend Taiwan,
though it is part of the ﬁrst island chain. Visiting
Beijing in May he mused about the challenges of
ﬁghting a war to defend the island and said he would
use arms sales to Taiwan as a bargaining chip in talks
with Beijing. That made his commitment look shaky.
One stabilising inﬂuence has been Admiral Samuel
Paparo, head of America’s Paciﬁc Command, whose
quiet diplomacy has soothed allies’ anxieties. But he

will ﬁnish his three-year tour in mid-2027. Mr Trump’s
pick to replace him will play an outsize role in whether
Asian allies can continue to trust America. ●

Aftershocks
from Iran
S
HORTLY AFTER the war in
Iran began, Singapore’s
foreign minister called it an
“Asian crisis”. About 80% of
the oil and 90% of the gas that
usually passed through the
Strait of Hormuz was bound
for the region’s markets.
Poorer countries without oil

reserves were especially
exposed. The Philippines
declared an energy emergen-
cy; Sri Lanka imposed a four-
day week to conserve fuel.
Other governments across
Asia were forced to act,
implementing measures to
ration demand, boost sup-
plies and inﬂuence markets.
Those efforts—aided by
weaker Chinese energy de-
mand and increased exports
from non-Gulf producers—
mostly worked. Asian econo-
mies proved largely resilient.
The Asian Development Bank

estimates that emerging
economies in the region grew
by 5% in 2026, only slightly
below the 5.5% of 2025. And
though prices rose—sharply in
some places—it was not
enough to foment unrest.
The relative stability could
be shattered in 2027, especially
if the conﬂict widens and
affects alternative shipping
routes from the Middle East.
Governments’ scope to re-
spond will diminish. Months of
subsidies and price caps have
strained ﬁnances, and emer-
gency stocks are low. Even if

the ﬁghting stops, damaged
facilities in the Gulf mean that
normal supply chains will take
time to recover.
All this will intensify the
search for secure energy sup-
plies, especially in countries
such as India and Indonesia
that hope to fuel growth by
building power-hungry data
centres. In some places, des-
peration could revive the dirty
habit of burning coal. This has
already propelled global coal
demand to a record high, where
it could remain if gas prices

stay elevated.
Governments will also
hasten investment in renew-
ables. Solar energy is expand-
ing rapidly and is now the
region’s third-largest source of
electricity, behind coal and
hydropower. That momentum
will build as the war strength-
ens the case for renewables.
Nuclear power will gain appeal,
too. India has released a frame-
work for developing nuclear-
power projects. The scheme
could be launched in 2027.
The quest to rewire grids
could also reshape foreign

relations. China’s inﬂuence will
increase further as demand for
its solar panels and batteries
rises. Russia, too, could beneﬁt
as countries seek its oil and
nuclear know-how. Events in
the Middle East could even
bring Asian countries closer
together. In South-East Asia, a
long-mooted regional grid
could ﬁnally gather some
impetus. Countries have also
discussed creating a shared
fuel stockpile, even as many
look to build their own.
Yet while Asian countries

manage the ramiﬁcations of a
conﬂict to their west, they must
also contend with a shock from
the east. Meteorologists expect
El Niño, the climate pattern
caused by a shift in winds over
the eastern Paciﬁc, to last
until February. It has already
wreaked havoc. Drier condi-
tions have aggravated ﬁres in
Indonesia, leading to wide-
spread toxic haze.
The biggest concern is
food. Farmers have been
affected by disruptions to fuel
and fertiliser supplies. Com-

bined with El Niño, this could
cause crop yields to fall and
food prices to surge. In con-
ﬂict-torn countries such as
Afghanistan and Myanmar,
the result could be a humani-
tarian catastrophe. Even in
stable countries, hunger
could upend politics. In 2026
Asia was shaken by an energy
crisis; in 2027 it could be
rattled by a food one.

O
NE-SIXTH OF humanity lives in India,
and one-sixth of India lives in Uttar
Pradesh (commonly known as UP). The
giant state in northern India’s Hindi-
speaking cow belt has a population of
240m. With 80 out of 543 seats in the
Indian parliament, UP sways national
politics. It also carries cultural heft, as the
home of several of India’s holiest (and most
controversial) sites. Of the 14 prime
ministers who have led India since

independence, nine had their
constituencies in UP.
In the spring of 2027, UP goes to the
polls. For the past decade it has been ruled
by Yogi Adityanath, a ﬁrebrand Hindu
priest, who has built roads, crushed crime
and targeted the state’s Muslim minority.
Diminutive and always clad in saffron, Mr
Adityanath is a big ﬁgure in Narendra
Modi’s ruling Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP),
second in star power only to the prime
minister himself. But after two terms he

faces a tough challenge. It will be a
momentous election that could shape the
next chapter of Indian politics.
To see why, ﬁrst consider Mr Modi, who
has been in office for 12 years. India’s
strongman is not a native of UP, but he
chose as his constituency the ancient city
of Varanasi, on the banks of the Ganges. In
2024 he opened the Ram temple at
Ayodhya (220km north of Varanasi) on the
site where, in 1992, a Hindu mob tore down
a well-known mosque. The temple marked

the fulﬁlment of a decades-long campaign
and is now a potent symbol of Mr Modi’s
goal of turning India into a Hindu state.
In May Mr Modi won a landmark
election victory in West Bengal, extending
his party’s grip beyond its Hindi
heartlands. But not everything has been
going his way. The summer was dominated
by huge protests, as India’s aspirational
youth voiced anger about education, jobs
and vote-rigging. Other scandals have
challenged the government, including a

case of embezzlement at the Ram temple.
Mr Modi’s electoral prowess has proved
durable, but many are wondering (again)
whether it is ﬁnally on the wane.
UP will be the test. Early reports from
the ground suggest voters are unhappy.
They blame Mr Modi’s government for
rising prices, and say it has not done
enough to get them jobs. Mr Adityanath
remains popular, and can point to several
successes. But the opposition coalition is
well organised and likely to run a tight

campaign. It is led by Akhilesh Yadav’s
Samajwadi party, a leftist outﬁt which has
support from lower castes and Muslims
and has governed the state four times.
What makes the election even more
intriguing is the role of Amit Shah, Mr
Adityanath’s main rival to succeed Mr
Modi. As the prime minister’s right-hand
man, Mr Shah wields immense power
within the government, in which he is
home minister, and as the BJP’s chief
strategist, with inﬂuence over election

campaigns and party management. Some
say that Mr Shah, who at 61 is 15 years
younger than Mr Modi, is impatient for the
top job. He and Mr Adityanath, who is 54,
reportedly often clash. In UP, there are
already rumours of Mr Shah interfering
in the campaign.
If the BJP manages to hold on to UP,a
re-emboldened Mr Modi will probably
ﬁght the next national election in 2029
(there are no term limits in India). Mr
Adityanath’s star would continue to rise,

and he may be unstoppable by the time Mr
Modi eventually steps down. If the BJP
loses in UP, however, Mr Adityanath would
be out of a job, talk of a Modi succession
would come sooner, and Mr Shah would be
in a stronger position.
Months of campaigning lie ahead (the
election is likely to be in March). No chief
minister has won three straight elections in
UP. Mr Adityanath could put money
straight into the bank accounts of millions
of voters, or resort to ugly rhetoric to divide

the opposition vote. Whatever happens,
the outcome will reverberate for years. ●
Succession
à la Modi
DELHI
A state election will signal whether the
prime minister’s power is waning
Tom Sasse South Asia bureau chief,

I
N LATE SEPTEMBER, India’s president
will swear in B.V. Nagarathna as chief
justice of the country’s supreme court.
For the ﬁrst time in the country’s nearly
eight decades as a constitutional republic,
the head of the highest authority charged

with upholding that constitution will be a
woman. Yet despite the high symbolism,
the reality may disappoint. Such
appointments are determined by seniority
and retirement age—and Justice
Nagarathna is due to retire a little more
than a month after her elevation.
It is a ﬁtting metaphor for the place of
women in India’s public life. Consider
another branch of government. In 2023
India’s lower house passed the Women’s
Reservation Act, which set aside a third of

seats for women, by a thumping majority of
454 votes in favour to two against. Yet this
cross-party show of support for women
also included a clause that would delay
implementation until the completion of a
contentious redistricting process, making
its goal of empowerment subservient to an
unrelated political drama. After elections
in 2024, women occupy a mere 14% of seats
in the lower house.
Women are under-represented in
India’s public life. Indeed, they are

under-represented in nearly all walks of
life. The female labour-force participation
rate of around 35% is far below the global
average of 49% and roughly on par with
that of Saudi Arabia. Admittedly it has
improved recently, probably because of a
rise in self-employment. But only one in
ﬁve formal workers in manufacturing is a
woman, a proportion that has remained
steady for decades.
Women are not seen out and about
much, either. A recent study looked at their

presence on the streets of several Indian
Ladies in

found that just 16% were women.
Much of this has to do with a
conservative culture in which a woman’s
place is still often considered to be at

outside the home brings a loss of face for

her husband, who is seen as having failed
in his role as a provider. Parts of India
remain dangerous for women and girls.
Worries about safety give even liberal
parents and in-laws a reason to insist that
women stay cloistered.
Yet attitudes are slowly changing. In
recent years several Indian states have
made public buses free for women, and
numbers of those using them have risen as
a result. Many states have also started
giving unconditional cash handouts to

women voters. Though the schemes—
which for some mysterious reason always
seem to be announced just before
elections—are criticised as expensive
bungs that eat into capital spending and
weaken state ﬁnances, they have been
found to increase both household
consumption and savings.
Women are also starting to become
more visible in public life. Huge youth
protests in Delhi and other major cities in
mid-2026 were remarkable for many
reasons, not least the robust participation

of women. Girls now outnumber boys in
school enrolment and higher education. In
the broader manufacturing sector, which
includes informal employment, women’s
share is up from about a quarter at the start
of the century to a third today. And the last
time India held a national election, in 2024,
female voter turnout was higher than male
turnout for the second time in a row.
Seven states will go to the polls in 2027,
including Uttar Pradesh, a bellwether that

is home to 240m people (see previous
story). Expect plenty of schemes aimed at
female voters in the run-up to the
elections. Women may not yet have
adequate representation in Parliament, but
they are increasingly studying, working
and deciding who represents them. ●
↑ Venturing out
I
F INDONESIA ALWAYS seems to be at a
crossroads, partly it is because a good
year is hard to tell from a bad one. In both
cases, economic growth trundles along at
around 5%, supported by a growing

population, now at 289m. But beneath the
headline ﬁgures, Indonesia’s economy
struggled in 2026. The currency, foreign
investment and the labour market are limp.
Under Prabowo Subianto, the populist
president, all three have weakened. If this
persists in 2027, how will he react?
Start with the rupiah, Indonesia’s
currency. After adjusting for inﬂation and
weighting by the size of the country’s
trading partners, it is at its weakest point
since the global ﬁnancial crisis of 2007-09,

having slipped by 13% since Mr Prabowo
came to power in 2024. A drastic loss of
conﬁdence in the stock market has not
helped. Indonesia is on track to be the
world’s worst-performing market in 2026.
But the biggest cause has been Mr
Prabowo’s erratic governance style,
marked by the deteriorating rule of law,
proﬂigate programmes and incoherent
policymaking. These have also weighed on
foreign direct investment. Flows of FDI
rose sharply under Mr Prabowo’s

predecessor, Joko Widodo, as ﬁrms sought
to capitalise on Indonesia’s vast mineral
wealth. Since 2024, they have ﬂatlined.
As America’s war with Iran sent the
price of oil imports soaring in mid-2026,
Indonesia’s economic course looked
unsustainable. Under pressure, Mr
Prabowo has adjusted somewhat. He has
reshuffled his cabinet and trimmed his
most spendthrift policies, including a
national school-meals programme.
As a result, Indonesia’s ﬁscal deﬁcit will

probably stay within the 3%-of-GDP limit
prescribed by the law, which could remove
one source of strain on the rupiah. But the
political pressure on Mr Prabowo will not
relent in the coming year. His popularity
has sagged to historic lows. Some polls put
his approval rating below 50%, down from
over 80% in 2025, primarily owing to
concerns about Indonesia’s economy.
Unfortunately for Mr Prabowo, such
concerns reﬂect reality. Life is not getting
much better for the average Indonesian.

The biggest reason is a broken labour
market. Between 2018 and 2025 real wages
(ie, after adjusting for inﬂation) for middle-
and high-skilled workers shrank by 1-2%

economic woes

The underemployment rate, which

presented no solutions. More Indonesians,

including graduates, are falling into low-
paying informal employment.
With a refreshed cabinet, falling
popularity and the economy a shambles,
you might think the time is ripe for a
Prabowo policy pivot in 2027. That seems
improbable. Mr Prabowo’s convictions,
including the notion that foreigners are
stealing Indonesia’s resources, run deep.
He will not want to jeopardise his
nationalist bona ﬁdes, especially as a
presidential election in 2029 looms. Rivals,

including Mr Widodo and Dedi Mulyadi,
the governor of West Java, are circling. And
a volte-face might upset a delicate balance
between political factions. A raid in July on
the home of Indonesia’s most senior
anti-corruption prosecutor revealed the
extent of the inﬁghting between police-
and army-aligned camps.
Instead, Mr Prabowo may double down
on his populist approach. His government
is preparing a programme of direct cash
transfers to households beginning in early

2027, worth as much as $10bn. More
resource nationalism looks likely, too.
One test will be the full implementation
of Danantara Sumberdaya Indonesia (DSI),
a new state agency overseeing commodity
exports, set for January 1st. Once pitched
by Mr Prabowo as a central body to control
key resources, officials now claim DSI will
act merely as a watchdog. As a matter of
policy, that is sensible—but it may not be
enough for the restless Mr Prabowo. ●

HE GREAT survivor of Australian
politics, Pauline Hanson, was on a
winning streak in 2026. Inﬁghting and an
identity crisis within the conservative
coalition of Liberal and National parties
catapulted Ms Hanson’s populist-right
One Nation party into the mainstream
after three decades on the fringes.
Ms Hanson enters 2027 as a serious
contender against the establishment
parties, also including centre-left Labor
which governs federally and in ﬁve of the

country’s eight states and territories. With
the ﬁnancial backing of Gina Rinehart, a
mining magnate who is Australia’s richest
person, One Nation wants to dramatically
slash the country’s migration intake and
dump the net-zero target and the
transition to renewable energy.
South Australia’s state election in
March was an early test for One Nation’s
strong polling results. It achieved a
remarkable swing of more than 20% and
won almost a quarter of the popular

vote—more than the formal Liberal
opposition. Then One Nation won its ﬁrst
lower-house seat in Western Australia.
Even in Victoria, a progressive heartland,
One Nation was polling strongly ahead of
a state election on November 28th.
In an effort to arrest haemorrhaging
support, the Liberal-National coalition has
lurched to the right. This has not helped it
in the polls. Australia’s most populous
state, New South Wales, has an election in
March 2027. In rural areas One Nation is

outpolling Labor and the coalition.
Benjamin Moffitt of Monash University
argues that One Nation is transforming
from a one-issue party into a broader
populist force. Recruitment of senior
politicians and public ﬁgures has bolstered
the party’s image as “anti-elite, not merely
an anti-immigrant party”, he says. Its focus
has shifted, from Islam and national
security to the consequences of migration
for the housing crisis—messaging that
resonates with mainstream voters.

But a lack of political discipline may yet
prove Ms Hanson’s undoing. In 2026 she
had to apologise after saying there were
“no good Muslims” and that Aboriginal
people were “the most primitive race on
earth”. Like Nigel Farage in Britain, she has
a history of falling out with members of her
own party once they are elected.
Still, One Nation is professionalising. It
is now using software to vet candidates,
after it emerged that Ms Hanson had hired

a convicted rapist as the party’s federal
campaign manager, and journalists dug up
candidates’ antisemitic remarks and
criminal pasts. But such things matter less
to voters bent on trashing the status quo.
If the federal Labor government can
persuade voters it is dealing with housing
prices and cost-of-living pressures, and the
coalition can get its act together, they may
yet take the wind out of Ms Hanson’s sails
ahead of the next federal election in

mid-2028. They do not have long. ●

HE VISION of the “Free and
Open Indo-Paciﬁc” (FOIP),
which has now come to
symbolise Japanese diplomacy,
was proposed by Abe Shinzo in
2016. Since then Japan has
gained the support of many
countries and steadily advan-
ced its efforts to bring growth
and prosperity to the region.
But now, ten years later, the
international community is
confronted with new realities:
intensifying geopolitical
competition; accelerating

technological innovation; and
the rise of the global south.
With attempts to unilaterally
change the status quo by force
or coercion proliferating in
several parts of the world, the
free, open and stable
international order is in
jeopardy. In response, I
announced an updated vision
for FOIP during my visit to
Vietnam in May.
Clearly the Indo-Paciﬁc is,
and will remain, a crucial
region that holds the key to
international peace and
stability. A core and founding

principle of FOIP is that Japan
will fulﬁl its responsibilities to
build an international order in
this region based on freedom,
openness, diversity, inclusive-
ness and the rule of law. In this
challenging environment, it is
also essential for Japan and
other countries in the region to
build resilience, and the
capability to determine their
own future amid complex
interdependent relations. To
this end, Japan continues to
provide the co-operation to

meet the needs of each country
in all domains, including
economic, social and security,
working together with the ally
and like-minded countries.
As we look towards 2027, let
me introduce some concrete
initiatives towards the
realisation of this vision. It
starts with securing a stable
energy supply and the
strengthening of supply-chain
resilience. Japan has launched
the POWERR Asia initiative to
strengthen supply resilience in
Asia, and is formulating a plan

to strengthen the economic
and energy resilience of the
Gulf countries. Stronger
collaboration between the
Indo-Paciﬁc and the Gulf will
contribute to a stable energy
supply in both regions.
The challenges we face are
by no means conﬁned to
energy. As technological
competition intensiﬁes,
particularly in AI, securing
access to cutting-edge
technologies and ensuring data
security is a priority for every
country. With the “FOIP Digital
Corridor Initiative”, Japan is

promoting the development of
reliable infrastructure,
including undersea cables,
Open RAN, and satellite
communications across the
Indo-Paciﬁc, from North
America to South-East Asia
and the Paciﬁc islands.
As international supply
chains become ever more
intertwined, it is imperative
that economic security under a
rules-based economic order is
advanced, both to achieve
growth and to respond to
economic coercion. Japan plays
an indispensable, complemen-

tary role in trade and invest-
ment in both the Indo-Paciﬁc
and Europe. “Made with Japan”
is a future we must pursue to
make our economies stronger
and more prosperous together.
Japan is promoting the
expansion and upgrading of
the Comprehensive and
Progressive Agreement for
Trans-Paciﬁc Partnership.
It is also important for the
region as a whole to secure the
safe and free sea lanes and
other logistics routes that
underpin supply chains. Japan
has consistently supported the

enhancement of the maritime
law-enforcement capabilities
of South-East Asian and Paciﬁc
island countries. In 2023 we
launched the “Official Security
Assistance” programme, and
we will continue to expand our
assistance to countries in the
region. To lead the region in
strengthening cyber-resilience,
Japan will make more use of the
ASEAN-Japan Cybersecurity
Capacity Building Centre,
based in Thailand.
Japan’s own efforts to
strengthen itself are just as

vital. Under my policy of
responsible and proactive
public ﬁnances, we will further
enhance our technological
strengths and industrial
competitiveness. Here, too, we
will co-operate with the ally
and like-minded countries. In
December 2026 the revision of
three documents, including the
National Security Strategy, will
decisively strengthen the
country’s defence capabilities.
Just four years have passed
since the previous revision, but
we think this is vital, given the

speed of change facing the
international community.
I believe that, for the
countries of the region and for
Japan, becoming more resilient
and prosperous together
through such efforts represents
the surest path toward the
realisation of FOIP. Together
with the many countries that
share this conviction, I intend
to build an Indo-Paciﬁc that
will serve as the foundation for
the peace and prosperity of the
international community. ●

I
N THE AUTUMN of 2027 thousands of delegates,
from exemplary workers to high-ranking
Communist Party members, will assemble amid the
marble pillars and red carpets of the Great Hall of the
People in Beijing to conﬁrm who will rule China until
2032. There will be no democracy involved—the
outcome will be decided in advance by China’s current
leader, Xi Jinping, and his colleagues. It is all but
certain that he will remain in power for another ﬁve-

year term. There is no sign he ever plans to step down.
The week-long party congress will be the 21st since
the party’s founding 105 years ago. It will choose a new
Central Committee of about 370 members that in turn
will rubber stamp Mr Xi’s choice of candidates for the
Politburo, a body of about 25 people at the apex of the
party. There will be rumours—in the private rooms of
Beijing restaurants and the reports of foreign
intelligence analysts—about which officials are rising

and which are toast. As often, there may be speculation
that Mr Xi himself is in trouble: a few days out of
public view can trigger that. But it is likely to be wrong.
There are no signs that Mr Xi’s grip is slipping as he
approaches what will be his fourth ﬁve-year term in
power. His anti-corruption campaign has decimated
senior ranks of the party and dismantled its patronage
networks. Rising stars once tipped to succeed Mr Xi
have been arrested or sidelined. Among the 200 or so

voting members of the current Central Committee,
perhaps a ﬁfth have been purged in the past ﬁve years.
Still, the congress in 2027 will be revealing about how
Mr Xi’s rule will evolve in the years to come.
One thing to watch is whether there are any
changes to Mr Xi’s titles. At the moment he holds three
key offices: that of president of China, a largely
ceremonial role, and the far more important jobs of
general secretary of the party and commander-in-chief

of the armed forces. (Mr Xi abolished term limits for
the presidency, the only title that had them, in 2018.)
He might use the congress to hand the title of
general secretary, say, to a trusted lieutenant, or to lay
the groundwork to pass on the title of president
(which is a government job, not a party one) at a
Chairman of
everything
Xi Jinping will begin another
ﬁve-year term as China’s leader

▸ parliamentary session in 2028. He will be 74 and may
wish to step back from some of the day-to-day
business of ruling. There is precedent for such a move:
Deng Xiaoping remained the party’s de facto leader in
the 1980s without being its general secretary. Deng
held on to his leadership of the armed forces, however,
and it is unlikely that Mr Xi will let go of the sharp edge
of his power either.
Alternatively, Mr Xi might give himself yet another

job at the congress: that of party chairman. Strongly
associated with Mao Zedong, the title was abolished in
1982 to signal that the party had adopted a more
collective style of decision-making. But that collective
spirit has all but disappeared under Mr Xi. His
centralisation of power and emphasis on ideology have
Maoist overtones. State media sometimes call him the
“helmsman” or “the people’s leader”—both titles
strongly associated with Mao. If Mr Xi were to become

chairman it would suggest he is comfortable being
seen as ruler for life.
Another question concerns who takes the senior
roles around Mr Xi. On the ﬁnal day of the congress he
will walk onto a stage in front of a crowd of journalists.
He will be followed by a small group of (almost
certainly) men, walking in order of seniority. That will
mark the ﬁrst official reveal of who is on the new
Politburo Standing Committee, which currently has
six other members. In theory, all but one are old

enough to retire, according to a generally respected
convention that Politburo members step down at 68.
Mr Xi has already granted himself an exception to
this unwritten rule. Will he give the same slack to his
ageing lieutenants? If the new Standing Committee is
much the same as the old one, it would mean Mr Xi has
even less respect for party norms than is now assumed.
It might also suggest that he is wary of younger party
members, with whom he has had little chance to build

up trust. That might cause problems down the road:
senior positions clogged up with gerontocrats would
slow down promotions for other ambitious officials.
Another faint possibility is that a much younger
politician might be parachuted into the Standing
Committee. That was how Mr Xi joined in 2007, when
he was 54. Any such person would be seen as a
leader-in-waiting, to perhaps take over after the
congress in 2032. But an heir-apparent could start to

drain Mr Xi of his own power—and he seems in no
hurry to let that happen. ●
There are no
signs that
Mr Xi’s grip
is slipping
I
F RIFTS IN China’s slowing economy
were becoming more apparent in 2026,
uneven development and AI will worsen
social strains in 2027. On the one hand, the
technology, advanced manufacturing and
export sectors are racing ahead. On the
other, old engines of growth, especially
property, continue to sputter, sending
workers into the ballooning gig economy.

This year it is expected to swell to 320m
workers, according to the China New
Employment Forms Research Centre. Even
the government’s more conservative esti-
mate of 200m in 2025 is equivalent to more
than two-ﬁfths of the urban workforce. As
the gig economy expands, China’s two-tier
economy is becoming entrenched.
Among those entering what the govern-
ment prefers to call “ﬂexible employment”
are many bright minds: with the number of
new graduates at a record high, youth

unemployment is running at nearly 20%.
Overall, China’s official unemployment
rate is around 5%, though Li Daokui of
Tsinghua University estimated in July that
the true level, using a broader deﬁnition,
could be twice that. Amid growing anxiety
among workers and officials over un-
employment, policymakers will place
greater emphasis on safety-nets and re-
distribution. But their proposals are inade-
quate given the sweeping changes afoot.
At a lecture in March, Li Shi of Zhejiang

University pointed to a jump in the wealth
Gini coefficient (which measures wealth
inequality) from 0.45 in 1995 to more than
0.7 in 2023. China is struggling to increase
the size of the cake and to divide it, he said.
Part of the problem is that the strategic
sectors that the government aims to dom-
inate are not labour-intensive. Though the
cake will probably not grow much in 2027,
dividing it will become a more urgent issue.
For a start, policymakers will try to

bring more gig workers into China’s tradi-
tional social-security system. Employees
and their employers together pay about
35% of salary in premiums; but many low-
income workers do not enroll because they
prefer ready cash. Firms are not required to
enroll gig workers, but more now offer the
option. In October the government vowed
to use data to identify unenrolled workers.
That prompted outrage online, as people
complained about having to give up some

of their income in order to pay into a pen-
sion fund that may run out of money. Un-
der today’s rigid system, which is not built
for gig workers, “mandating social-security
contributions feels more like an obligation
than a right,” says Nie Riming of Shanghai
Institute of Finance and Law, a think-tank.
Cities are also removing red tape to
grant gig workers, who are often rural
migrants, more welfare beneﬁts. In Sep-
tember the government proposed a three-

year campaign to include more gig workers
in basic medical-insurance schemes for
employees, for example. But as AI adoption
accelerates and economic growth slows,
more jobs will become precarious. Building
a serious safety-net ﬁt for today’s workers
is becoming a more pressing task.
Parked outside a new subsidised
dormitory in Beijing (part of a food-
delivery company’s efforts to show that it is
improving welfare), Mr Li, a 27-year-old
driver from Henan, is pessimistic. China’s

big companies are all outsourcing their
labour, he says: “Fewer and fewer can really
offer protections, and everyone is forced to
do gig work like us.” His earnings have
fallen as more and more workers have piled
into food delivery, though he remains
grateful for the freedom of not having to
work in a factory. “Class has already been
solidiﬁed,” he says. “There is little road left
for us latecomers.” ●

59The World Ahead 2027 China
T
HE YEAR 2027 has long been a focal point for those
who plan for a potential Chinese invasion of
Taiwan. Around the start of this decade, American
officials say they received intelligence suggesting that
Xi Jinping, China’s leader, had ordered his armed
forces to be ready to capture the island by that year,
which marks the centenary of the People’s Liberation
Army (PLA). That intelligence led to a highly unusual
public warning in 2021 from Admiral Philip Davidson,

who led America’s Indo-Paciﬁc Command at the time.
“I think the threat is manifest during this decade, in
fact in the next six years,” he told a Senate hearing.
So began talk of a “Davidson window” for a
Chinese attack by 2027. American officials have since
clariﬁed that Mr Xi ordered the PLA to develop the
capability for a Taiwan invasion by then, not
necessarily to launch one. In March 2026 America’s
spooks declared in an annual report that they did not

consider a Chinese attack likely in 2027. Nonetheless,
the coming year will be a critical one for China,
America and Taiwan as they each race to absorb the
lessons of wars in Ukraine and Iran while another
potential window for Chinese aggression looms: the
ten months in 2028 between presidential elections in
Taiwan and those in America.
Mr Xi is hoping that the Kuomintang (KMT), the
biggest opposition party, will return to power in the
island’s presidential poll, which is expected in January

2028. The KMT’s leader, Cheng Li-wun, wants closer
ties with the mainland and travelled there in 2026 to
meet Mr Xi. She opposes independence for Taiwan.
But the island’s ruling Democratic Progressive Party
(DPP), in power since 2016, rejects Mr Xi’s
preconditions for talks and says Taiwan is already an
independent nation. So if the current Taiwanese
president, Lai Ching-te, wins a second term in 2028,
Mr Xi may conclude that the only way to achieve

uniﬁcation is through force.
During 2027 China is thus expected to intensify
efforts to manipulate public opinion on the island
through public pronouncements and covert use of
social media, artiﬁcial intelligence and cyber-attacks.
It could also escalate military exercises around Taiwan
to try to intimidate voters, as it did before a
presidential poll there in 1996. On that occasion it ﬁred
missiles into waters nearby, prompting America to
send two aircraft-carriers to the area. That effort

backﬁred: Taiwan elected the candidate to whom
China objected. But Mr Xi will be conscious that
China’s military capabilities are far greater today and
that the war in Iran has depleted America’s weapons
stocks, eroding its capacity to help defend Taiwan if
called upon to do so.
He may also calculate that Donald Trump is less
committed to Taiwan’s defence than any previous
American president. Mr Trump has made a series of
remarks suggesting he has little regard for the island

and has even publicly questioned the logic of ﬁghting
a war with China so far from American shores. He has
also said that he views arms sales to Taiwan as a
bargaining chip with China, and has delayed
approving the latest package, worth about $14bn,
which was approved by Congress in January 2026.
That is hampering Taiwan’s efforts to adopt a
“porcupine strategy” to defend itself with more
lightweight, mobile (and mostly American) weapons.
So if the DPP wins again in January 2028, the period

between then and America’s election in November
that year could be dangerous. The PLA may still not be
conﬁdent of pulling off an amphibious invasion of
Taiwan. Its operational readiness has been
undermined by Mr Xi’s decision to purge almost his
entire military high command. The PLA must also now
adapt its invasion plans to account for rapid advances
in drone and anti-drone warfare during the wars in
Ukraine and the Middle East.
But Mr Xi may decide to act anyway, possibly

ordering a blockade or a customs quarantine rather
than a full invasion, if he senses that America’s next
president will be more committed to arming and
defending Taiwan. Mr Xi may also grow more
determined to show progress towards uniﬁcation as he
embarks on his fourth ﬁve-year term as China’s
supreme leader after the Communist Party congress in
late 2027. The “Davidson window” may not have
closed after all, but simply widened. ●
To invade? Or
not to invade?

TAIPEI
China still doesn’t know
what to do about Taiwan

60 China The World Ahead 2027
⏩
T
HE COMING year will mark the 20th
anniversary of an unusual press
conference. In March 2007 Wen Jiabao,
then China’s prime minister, faced
reporters after a meeting of the country’s
parliament. He was asked a soft question:
could China continue its fast growth (over
10%) and low inﬂation (under 3%)? His
answer was surprisingly edgy. There was
no time to “boast”, he said. China’s
development was “unstable, unbalanced,

uncoordinated and unsustainable”.
The “four uns”, as they quickly became
known, encapsulated many people’s fears
about China’s miracle growth. The
country’s rapid progress was unstable, Mr
Wen explained, because of excessive
lending. It was unbalanced because coastal
cities had raced ahead of rural backwaters.
It was uncoordinated because manufact-
uring investment was out of sync with
consumption, leaving the economy reliant
on exports. And growth was unsustainable

because of its toll on the environment.
Is this relevant to China’s prospects 20
years later? On the face of it, no. The
economy has changed beyond recognition.
In 2007 it was classed as “lower middle-
income”, according to the World Bank. In
July 2027 it will probably be crowned a
high-income country (see chart).
China’s more prosperous economy is
also more pedestrian. It is now growing at
less than half the rate Mr Wen refused to
boast about. It will reach the official

growth target of 4.5-5% in 2026, but
policymakers will not aim for anything
more ambitious in the year ahead.
China will also fall short of the 3%
inﬂation rate that Mr Wen was quizzed
about. In his time low inﬂation was a rare
and praiseworthy accomplishment. In
today’s China it is a chronic and dangerous
condition. Prices fell for three years in a
row from 2023 to 2025, by some measures.
The Iran war brought that run to an end, by
pushing up fuel and petrochemical costs.

But in the year ahead those high prices will
be the baseline from which further
inﬂation is measured. If commodity prices
stop increasing or even fall, China could
easily slip back into deﬂation in 2027.
Despite China’s transformation over
the past two decades, some of the
imbalances Mr Wen identiﬁed still afflict
the country. A glaring example is its trade
surplus. Rapid expansion of manufact-
uring capacity is still out of sync with
lacklustre growth in consumer demand.

The result is a surfeit of goods, produced
but not absorbed at home, which China
instead sells to the rest of the world.
Another old imbalance that still haunts
China’s policymakers is debt. After Mr
Wen warned about excessive credit
creation in 2007, the problem only got
worse. To combat the global ﬁnancial
crisis, China let local governments borrow
through off-balance-sheet ﬁnancing
vehicles. The debts of these vehicles now
exceed 60trn yuan ($9trn), or 45% of GDP.

In response, the central government has
imposed tighter ﬁnancial discipline on
local officials. That hit investment.
Provincial and city officials are trying to
repair their balance-sheets rather than
expanding their infrastructure.
Reversing this slowdown is probably
China’s best hope for maintaining growth,
warding off deﬂation and narrowing the
trade surplus in 2027. The government has
begun mobilising money to spend on what
it calls the “six networks”: telecoms,

computing, logistics, power, underground
city pipelines and water. It could spend
25trn yuan over ﬁve years.
Critics will point out that this spending
may narrow one imbalance (the trade
surplus) only by worsening another
(China’s overreliance on investment). That
is true. It would be better if policymakers
instead boosted the economy through a
rapid increase in consumer spending. Alas,
saving habits do not change overnight, and
consumer conﬁdence cannot be ordered

into existence. It has been almost 20 years
since Mr Wen noted that consumption and
production were uncoordinated. The fact
they remain so all these years later is
telling: that particular “un” will not be
solved in 2027. ●

H
UMANOID ROBOTS are popping up all
over China’s biggest cities. They are
making espressos in cafés, taking orders at
shops, grabbing soft drinks and handing
them (very slowly) to customers. In some
cases they are preparing dishes in
restaurants. These performances are
conducted to great fanfare. A human-
looking bot in public almost always draws

a crowd of onlookers. Humanoids have
also become ubiquitous at conferences and
government events.
Social media are full of such
encounters. That seems to be the point.
Legions of droids are not meant to serve
the public, but to wow them, and the world.
In June China’s leaders told local
governments and state-owned enterprises
to have 10,000 humanoids doing real work
by the start of 2027. So more are coming.
But they will still not be very useful.

China is the undisputed leader in
humanoid production. Hundreds of ﬁrms
that once focused on the automotive
supply chain now make parts for robots,
which share many similar ﬁttings. In just a

few years, the world’s most advanced
humanoid supply chain has sprung up in
Shanghai and several surrounding cities,
including the AI hub of Hangzhou. This
has helped spawn the world’s biggest and
most sophisticated humanoid-makers,
such as Unitree, which in August became
the ﬁrst listed company of its kind.
Chinese ﬁrms made almost all of the
estimated 50,000 bots that were produced
globally in 2026. The production ﬁgure will

at least double in 2027, reckon analysts at
Morgan Stanley, a bank. The existence of
100,000 new droids means the chances of
seeing one, and even drinking a coffee
made by one, will go up considerably, at
least for urban Chinese. Amid the surge in
supply of low-cost parts, the number of
industrial ﬁrms diversifying into
humanoid production is also growing. By
the third quarter of 2026 the number of
humanoid-makers may have surpassed
300. More will join the rush in 2027.

The appeal of humanoids is that they
are supposed to be versatile, and ﬁt well
into environments built for humans. In
practice, however, few humanoids are
being deployed in real workplaces because
they are terrible at completing most tasks.
Where they are doing useful work, it
almost always involves repetitive
movements, without any variation in the
surrounding conditions. Their clumsiness
will not diminish signiﬁcantly in 2027
because the amount of data required to

create a useful humanoid is vast.
Generating data for this kind of robot is
also very expensive. It costs 500-700 yuan
($75-105) to produce an hour of data.
Experts reckon 100m hours of data will be
needed to develop a bot that can excel at a
useful number of tasks. That would cost
roughly $7.5bn and take years to produce.
The state wants to help. A boom in
training centres, where humanoids
perform tasks for the sole purpose of
creating data, has begun over the past two

years. About 90 had been partially or fully
built by mid-2026, most of them
apparently backed by local governments.
Many more will be announced in the
coming year. For a glimpse of how China’s
humanoids are doing in 2027, these
training facilities will be the places to look.
But it is going to take a lot longer for the
robots to make a perfect cappuccino—or,
indeed, to learn to do anything else that is
useful in mainstream society—than many
people currently imagine. ●

A
MERICA AND China would appear to
be running in very different races when
it comes to artiﬁcial intelligence. As Amer-
ica focuses on building superintelligence,
China is said to be set on diffusing the
technology throughout its economy. Amer-
ican models are expensive and closed,
while Chinese ones are cheap and open-
weight (meaning anyone can download
and run them). And while researchers in
San Francisco fret about AI sending man-
kind extinct, engineers in Beijing and

Hangzhou worry only that their models
might contradict China’s leaders. Expect
all this to change during 2027, which will
be a year of AI convergence.
Chinese AI ﬁrms will increasingly
resemble American ones, even if their
models do not catch up. Start with the
technology itself. Like their American
peers, China’s AI labs are set on developing
superintelligent models, or artiﬁcial gener-
al intelligence (AGI). Liang Wenfeng of
DeepSeek, a leading lab, recently told

investors that “in our hearts we’re thinking
about AGI.” Two other important players,
Tang Jie of Zhipu and Yang Zhilin of
Moonshot, feel similarly. To build AGI,
Chinese labs are training ever-larger mod-
els of the kind pioneered by their Amer-
ican rivals. ByteDance, among others, is
said to be training a 10trn-parameter
system (meaning it contains 10trn adjust-
able weights and biases learned during its
training process), which would rival the

size of Anthropic’s Mythos model.
The push to train larger models is
changing the unit economics of China’s AI
industry. It means that China’s most capa-
ble models will not be able to run on per-
sonal computers, unlike many of today’s
freely downloadable open-weight models,
and instead will increasingly be accessed
from data centres, or the cloud. Big train-
ing runs and cloud computing can be
costly, especially in China, where comput-
ing resources are limited by America’s

export ban on the most capable AI chips.
So Chinese labs will raise prices as Amer-
ican ones cut to compete. Even when their
models are hosted and served by third
parties in other countries, labs like Moon-
shot, based in Beijing, will demand a slice
of the revenues through stricter licensing.
Bigger Chinese models will mean more
capable ones, too. As in America in 2026,
in 2027 China’s labs and government
regulators will have to confront models

that pose serious risks. There will be more
security incidents of the sort already seen
by American labs, in which frontier mod-
els behave in unexpected ways and hack
into external systems. That may prompt
the government to implement stricter
pre-release safety testing, and increase the
need for America and China to co-operate
to ensure models are not misused or
accessed by bad actors.
China’s president, Xi Jinping, presents
open-weight models as his country’s gift

to the world. He says they are essential for
ensuring developing countries are not left
behind or beholden to American tech
Model
alignment

to AI will converge

F
OR A TASTE of the changing winds of
Chinese inﬂuence, head to YouTube, an
American video-sharing platform. Type in
the search term “China”, and you will
encounter video titles such as “China’s
INSANE megacities”, “China is NOT what
we expected”, and indeed “Western media
LIED about China”. It would be wrong to
dismiss this as mere social-media clickbait.
Across the world, public opinion of China
is improving. In 21 out of 35 countries

surveyed by the Pew Research Centre, a
think-tank, in February to May 2026, public
opinion on China had turned more positive
since 2024. It is now seen more favourably
than America in most of those countries
(see chart), including American allies such
as Australia, Britain and Canada.
In 2027 this gap will grow. Perceptions
of China will improve as Chinese brands
go global and Donald Trump’s unpredict-
able behaviour tarnishes America’s
reputation with friends and foes alike.

Joseph Nye, a scholar who coined the
term “soft power”, once said that the best
propaganda is not propaganda. For
decades China’s government tried to
cultivate a positive image abroad. It built
Confucius Institutes to promote Chinese
language learning and cultural exchange. It
poured resources into its international
media machinery—Xinhua, China Daily
and CGTN—the better to “tell China’s story
well”. It lent pandas to foreign zoos,
sponsored scholarships for foreign

students and extended aid to developing
countries. But all this did little to improve
perceptions of China abroad.
Where state efforts gained little
traction, the private sector has picked up
the slack. Chinese brands are moving into
foreign markets and have become more
adept at promoting themselves. This has
created a halo effect that burnishes the
image of China itself. BYD became the
world’s top-selling maker of electric
vehicles in 2025. Western consumers love

drones from DJI, fashion from Shein, and
bingeing content on TikTok.
Look for further examples in 2027.
Mixue, a tea and cold-drinks chain, has
already overtaken McDonald’s in the
number of global outlets, but wants more
outside China. So does Luckin Coffee,
another chain, which by June had 36,000
stores, just shy of the Starbucks tally of
41,000. And Unitree, a Hangzhou-based
robotics company which had a stellar IPO
in August, is expected to sell more

humanoid robots than any other company.
China remains weak in its output of
creative media. While South Korea has
K-pop and Japan has anime, China’s
cultural exports are not widely consumed
by foreigners. But there are signs that this
may be changing. “The Early Spring”, a
Chinese rom-com about a woman in a
workplace romance with her boss, became
the second-most-watched non-English
show worldwide on Netﬂix, a streaming
platform, in August 2026. China also

shines in video games. “Black Myth:
Wukong” wowed gamers in 2024; a sequel,
“Black Myth: Zhong Kui”, is on the way.
The growth of China’s soft power is not
inexorable. It is a consequence of both
increasingly positive attitudes towards
China and negative ones towards America,
which have intensiﬁed as Mr Trump’s war
on Iran has pushed up prices around the
world. But there will be a world after Mr
Trump, and global opinion can change
quickly. Recall the days of covid-19, when

China’s cover-up of the outbreak drew
worldwide condemnation, and public
opinion of the country fell to a low. In 2027,
though, China is well placed to increase its
soft power still further. ●

ﬁrms. Chinese researchers argue that
open-weight models are safer than closed
American ones because they can be
independently inspected, and they reduce
the risk that power is concentrated in a
handful of ﬁrms. But regulators have put in
place the tools to limit access to China’s

very best models. The age of openness may
be coming to an end.
The two superpowers’ AI sectors will
become more alike in their ambitions,
economics and anxieties. But their ability
to realise those ambitions may diverge.
Beyond price and safety, the most
important geopolitical question will be
whether China can catch up with, or even
leap ahead of, America on performance.
One gap will certainly not close: American
ﬁrms have been urgently building out new

computing capacity—some 38 gigawatts
(GW) are expected to come online next
year—while Chinese ones will probably
only see 5-8GW of new capacity as they
grapple with American export controls and
the limited capacity and performance of
domestic chipmakers.
To overcome this disadvantage, many
Chinese model-makers in 2026 turned to
“distillation”—a process of training their
models using the outputs of superior
American ones. Some experts doubt that
distilled models can ever exceed the

frontier, keeping them permanently in
second place. Researchers at Chinese labs
worry that relying too much on distillation
will lead them to skip building their own
reinforcement-learning infrastructure,
which could be essential to developing
self-improving AGI. Some ﬁrms, like
ByteDance, are deliberately forgoing
distillation methods for these reasons.
One way to keep on keeping up with
America will be for China to press its big
advantages: cheap electricity and talent.

Mr Xi has exhorted local leaders to
concentrate their efforts, to reduce
redundant data-centre building. Central
planners will urge a concentration of
computing power in the country’s west,
where solar and wind energy are abundant
and chips can be pooled for big training
runs. They will also enforce restrictions on
foreign investment and the movement of
researchers, to ensure their most promising
technologists are not snapped up by
Americans with deep pockets.

China’s AI ﬁrms will increasingly
resemble their American rivals in 2027. But
to close the gap that really matters, the
country has more work ahead. ●

Middle East & Africa
I
N NOVEMBER 2025 Donald Trump hosted the Saudi
crown prince at the White House. He bragged that
the Middle East was “close to truly everlasting peace”.
Three months later the American president started the
most disruptive regional war in half a century. It seems
futile to make forecasts when the people shaping
events are unreliable narrators.
Still, predict we must. So here is a bold forecast: the
Middle East will not reach everlasting peace in 2027.

Even if the Gulf avoids a return to all-out war, the
alternatives hardly look harmonious. America and Iran
could continue their recent cycle of talks and threats,
leaving the Strait of Hormuz closed to most vessels. Or
they could agree to a wobbly interim deal and start
negotiations over Iran’s nuclear programme, which
would be long and probably futile. Either way, the
trajectory is bleak.
Nor is there likely to be a lasting end to the region’s
many other conﬂicts. Expect little reconstruction in

Gaza, whose 2m people have endured another year of
misery, and little progress in Lebanon towards
disarming Hizbullah. The Saudis cannot endure
another year of widespread disruption caused by
Houthi missile attacks. They might sue for peace with
the Iran-backed rebels, but doing so will not stop
Yemen’s long-running civil war. A deal between
America and Iran may not suffice to end it either.
Simmering conﬂict will be the region’s base
case—even in the Gulf, which had long seemed

immune. Having realised that America is no longer a
reliable protector, Gulf states will look elsewhere to
bolster their security. This will prove a struggle. The
Mecca pact, a mutual-defence agreement signed in
August by Saudi Arabia, Pakistan and Turkey, might
grow to include Egypt and other states. But its
members are reluctant to ﬁght one another’s wars. It
will be more of a geopolitical hedging club: think
BRICS, not NATO.
Analysts have lately been fond of predicting a

Middle East split into competing blocs, one led by
Saudi Arabia, the other anchored by Israel and the
United Arab Emirates (UAE). Reality will be messier.

new order

preferred interlocutor for Iran, a country the pact is
ostensibly meant to defend against.
America will start its retrenchment from the
region—the key word being “start”. It will not rush to
close military bases when it is the only actor capable of
escorting ships through Hormuz. China, meanwhile,
will continue trying to position itself as a mediator. But
its diplomatic efforts are unlikely to yield dramatic
results, nor will it emerge as a regional military power.

Israel will remain isolated, even if Gadi Eisenkot
succeeds in forming a government and unseating
Binyamin Netanyahu. Gone are the days when many
Gulf rulers saw a relationship with Israel, whether
open or discreet, as a useful counterweight against
Iran. Most now view it as a source of chaos, not a bul-
wark against it. There is little prospect of expanding
the Abraham accords in 2027, no matter how much Mr
Trump might implore Saudi Arabia to join.

If Hormuz remains disrupted, Gulf economies will
be strained. After a 4.8% contraction in 2026, the IMF
expects the region to rebound and grow by 9.1% in
2027. But that depends on the strait reopening by
April, which may be too optimistic. Some Gulf states
will be tempted to make side-deals with Iran if they see
no other way to restart their economies; others may
need to tap their sovereign-wealth funds. A prolonged
slump in the Gulf would have consequences in other

Arab countries, which depend on aid, investment and
remittances from the rich petrostates.
Those are the safe bets; a lesson from 2026 is that
the outlandish ones are underpriced. Will a new
Democratic Congress withhold funding for the war in
Iran? Perhaps, but even some progressive Democrats
are doubtful, lest they be accused of bolstering the
regime. Might Iran grow tired of America’s threats and
launch a pre-emptive strike? Possibly. Could the

regime dash for a nuclear bomb in 2027? It has the
motive but probably lacks the means. And then there
are the Trumpy wild cards: might a president who once
threatened to “obliterate” Iranian civilisation decide to
shake hands with his Iranian counterpart? It seems
implausible. But a year ago, so did the Iran war. ●

B
INYAMIN NETANYAHU,
Israel’s longest-serving
prime minister, may not be in
office in 2027. As 2026 drew to
a close, he had lost his co-
alition’s majority in a hard-
fought election and his rivals
were scrabbling to form a
government and replace him.
It is a Sisyphean task to ﬁt
together the ill-ﬁtting parties
of the Knesset, Israel’s parlia-
ment. Even if Gadi Eisenkot,

the former general who leads
the largest opposition party,
pulls it off, the result is likely
to be unstable. And Mr Netan-
yahu will not leave public life.
At 77, he has no retirement
plans and is convinced he is
still Israel’s inevitable leader,
having served a total of over 19
years in office. Just as he has in
the past, he will be planning his
next comeback.
His party, Likud, has been
purged of any potential chal-
lengers to his leadership. Even

though it won only 20% of the
vote, it remains the largest
party in the Knesset, maintain-
ing its alliance with hard-right
and ultra-religious parties.
Israel does not lack chal-
lenges. Its army occupies near-
ly two-thirds of the Gaza Strip,
but Hamas, the militant Islam-
ist group that carried out the
October 7th massacre, rules the
rest of the territory. In Leba-
non, Israel still confronts Hiz-
bullah, the Shia militia that
dominates that war-torn coun-

try. Hizbullah’s patron, Iran,
remains an implacable enemy.
Another round of warfare with
Iran is a distinct possibility. At
home, as the election results
demonstrate, Israel is as divid-
ed as ever, riven by existential
questions over the its demo-
cratic and Jewish identity.
Abroad, it fears losing the
support of its Western allies,
especially America.
It seems absurd for a coun-
try with so many pressing
concerns to be obsessed with
the political survival of one

individual. Israel urgently
needs to move on from Bibi.
His support, as shown by the
election results, comes from a
minority of Israelis, but his
power to continue setting the
agenda is almost certain to
remain a key factor in any of
three likely scenarios for 2027.
If an opposition “govern-
ment of change” is ﬁnally
inaugurated, it will have trou-
ble staying in power. Mr Netan-
yahu, his political allies and
media outriders will keep up a
continuous barrage, opposing

it at every turn—especially if
the new government either
includes or relies upon the
votes of the Arab-Israeli
parties, whom Mr Netanyahu
accuses of being “terrorism-
supporters” and having
“stolen the election”. He will
chip away at the new coalition
and probe for defectors.
If Mr Eisenkot or other
opposition leaders join Mr
Netanyahu in a unity govern-
ment, it will be an unhappy
cohabitation in which none of
the partners will be capable of
trusting each other, much less

implementing the policies
that Israel urgently needs.
Such a government is unlikely
to last long.
In the third scenario, in
which none of the political
players is capable of forming
a coalition, Mr Netanyahu
will remain as caretaker prime
minister and Israelis will
return to the polls in 2027 for
another election. And it
would, once again, be a refer-
endum on one man.

Uncertainty looms after

65The World Ahead 2027 Middle East & Africa
T
HE ECONOMY is tanking. America
besieges its ports. The supreme leader
remains hidden. Yet for all the opposition
ranged against it, at home and abroad, Iran
will limp through 2027 with the Islamic
Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), the
regime’s enforcers, ﬁrmly in charge.
Donald Trump will continue to make
threats. But he will stop short of a ground
invasion. The multi-ﬂeet force he has
assembled will be too costly to secure

Congressional approval. Israel will not go
it alone. Mr Trump will continue to offer
negotiations, not least in the hope of
reducing oil prices. After seeing Mr
Trump’s mauling in the midterms, some
Iranians may be tempted to seek better
terms. But most decision-makers will
reason that he cannot be trusted. Twice
Iran negotiated with America, only for Mr
Trump to join Israel in launching a war
while talks were under way. Past precedent,
they say, suggests he would soon breach

any deal. Expect talk of talks to continue,
for the rest of his term, unresolved.
The war footing will also hasten the
IRGC generals’ ascent to power. No one
will be quite sure when the theocracy has
passed. State television will continue its
standard diet of Koranic recitations. But
the clerics who have led the Islamic
Republic since its inception in 1979 will
recede further from view. Loyalists will
insist that Mojtaba Khamenei, the nominal
supreme leader, is running the country

from hiding, for fear of another Israeli
assassination. But he has not been seen
since Israel killed his father in February
2026. The real decision-makers, the
National Security Council, a body of
military and civilian ﬁgures, will ﬁnd this
absence convenient. No supreme leader
will overrule them. The regime’s
ideological core will remain loyal.
The ship of state will run more
smoothly than it did under Mojtaba’s
father, the late Ayatollah Ali Khamenei,

surprising those who predicted the
regime’s collapse. The civil service, under
Masoud Pezeshkian, the president, will be
spared the supreme leader’s interference.
Blackouts will be less frequent than before
the war, partly because the high cost of
electricity will curb demand, partly
because of repairs of war damage. Rising
prices will dampen petrol consumption,
ensuring availability for those who can
afford it. Tensions between civilian
officials, army generals and the IRGC will

persist. But the existential threat from
abroad will prevent their escalation into
factional inﬁghting.
America’s punishing sanctions will
continue to bite. Yet some revenue will
trickle in through trade across Iran’s seven
land borders. Trains, trucks and ships on
the Caspian Sea will bring more affordable
goods from China, replacing those that
once arrived from Dubai. Exports of
copper, gold and some oil will take the
same routes by return. Iranian oil will make

its way into Iraq, where it will be relabelled
for onward sale. Smuggling will continue
to offer rich pickings for regime cronies.
Iran’s people will suffer more than their
rulers. Starved of raw materials and
domestic demand, factories will close.
Hundreds of thousands will lose their jobs.
The cash-strapped government will
struggle to pay salaries. Mr Trump’s
sanctions will curtail Iranians’ ability to
travel abroad and seek help from overseas.

Foreign intelligence agencies, led by
Israel, will seek to exploit Iranians’ misery
and smuggle in arms. Some Kurds in the
east may follow the Baluchis into waging a
low-intensity insurgency. But most
Iranians will have little appetite for adding
civil war to their list of woes. And the
violence will give the regime a pretext to
tighten its grip. For the ﬁrst time since the
Iran-Iraq war, checkpoints will appear on
major roads and in city centres. Public

executions will proliferate. The war has
opened a Pandora’s box across the region.
Inside Iran, however, little has changed. ●

HAT A DIFFERENCE a year makes. In
January 2026 the United Arab
Emirates (UAE) and Saudi Arabia were top
investment destinations. Analysts
predicted growth across the Gulf on the

back of increasing populations and new
industries. Abu Dhabi became one of the

chips and secured $1.2bn in funding. But
America’s war with Iran punctured the
Gulf’s reputation for safety. Recovery will
call for a sharper economic strategy.
First, damaged infrastructure will need
rebuilding. Gasﬁelds and oil-pumping
stations are costly to ﬁx. The strike on

Qatar’s Pearl gas-to-liquids plant could
cost as much as $20bn to repair. Fixing
these assets will distract from plans to
boost manufacturing facilities and embark
on new projects, such as data centres.
Leaders will also have to secure their
critical infrastructure, if they wish to regain
investors’ conﬁdence. How can newly
constructed data centres be protected
against drones and missiles? And as
growth prospects sag, there is also the
burden of keeping domestic investment

moving. PwC, a consultancy, says the
region has $2trn of megaprojects due for
completion by 2035 in transport, property,
energy and utilities. Governments may
have to rethink many of them.
Until recently, Gulf investors were
doubling down on bets overseas. Now
some have pulled back on foreign tech
investments. How they change their stance
will have global implications for venture
capital and private equity.
Simply relying on the latest technology,

and America’s support, is no longer
enough. The UAE has done well, quickly
moving to fortify its domestic economy.
Saudi Arabia’s ﬁnances are strained. After
big investments at home, it will have to
lean more on established pillars such as
Aramco, the state oil ﬁrm. Qatar can no
longer depend on a steady stream of gas
revenues. In 2027 Gulf rulers will have to be
much more tactical. ●

T
HE COMING year will be a quiet one for
Africa’s election-watchers. Only four of
the continent’s 54 countries are due to hold
presidential or general elections, and
perhaps only two offer a plausible opening
for an opposition victory. That will do little
to restore faith in democratic institutions.
Afrobarometer, a pollster, ﬁnds that
popular demand for democracy is falling,
with citizens in many countries more open

to non-democratic alternatives. Across the
continent, imperial-style presidencies are
increasingly the norm. In 2027 many will
entrench themselves further.
South Sudan’s vote symbolises all of
this. Set for December 2026, it is the ﬁrst
since independence from Sudan in 2011.
Salva Kiir, the ageing president who rules
like a medieval king, was once hailed as a
potential democrat. Yet the state barely
functions, the economy is in crisis and the
poll is expected to be a sham, if held at all.

In 2026 Tanzania’s president, Samia
Suluhu Hassan, responded to anger at the
violent repression that followed a ﬂawed
election by tightening her grip on all
institutions. Abiy Ahmed, Ethiopia’s prime
minister, followed a sweeping, if
questionable, election victory in June by
edging closer to an executive presidency.
Zimbabwe changed the constitution to
extend presidential terms, pushing the end
of Emmerson Mnangagwa’s rule from 2028
to at least 2030.

Of the three big countries voting,
Angola offers the dimmest prospects for a
democratic reboot. The ruling MPLA has
held all the levers of state for nearly half a
century, and the president appoints all 18
provincial governors. Even if UNITA, the
most popular opposition party, were to
overcome the obstacles placed in its way,
the MPLA might not cede power quietly.
In Nigeria, which votes in January,
President Bola Tinubu is widely expected
to waltz to victory. Though many poor

Nigerians are unhappy with his economic
reforms, which hurt living standards, an
economic rebound is starting to gather
pace. Mr Tinubu’s personal poll ratings
have improved. His party controls 31 of the
country’s 36 state houses, and the
opposition is weak and divided.
William Ruto, Kenya’s president, is
more unpopular than Mr Tinubu. Since
2023 his government has faced mass
protests. A survey by TIFA Research, a
pollster, recently found that 68% of

Kenyans had little to no trust in the
president. But the same pollster also
concluded that Mr Ruto remains the
favourite to win in August. The
opposition’s best chance may lie in
mobilising young Kenyans to vote.
If 2027 has any surprises in store, they
may come from beyond the ballot box.
Uganda’s 81-year-old dictator, Yoweri
Museveni, is starting to totter. Niger’s
unpopular junta saw off a coup attempt in
2026, but could face another. Ethiopia and

Eritrea may be on the brink of war.
Elections in Africa are often predictable.
African politics rarely is. ●

incumbent

HENDELEGATES arrive at the UN
Climate Change Conference, COP32,
in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia’s capital, in
November 2027 they will be welcomed by
hosts who are keen to put on a show. Abiy

Ahmed, the bombastic prime minister, will
highlight the Grand Ethiopian
Renaissance Dam, Africa’s largest source
of hydropower, which opened in 2025.
There is a good chance he will ask visiting
leaders to plant trees as part of his Green
Legacy Initiative, an effort to reverse mass
deforestation. Chauffeurs will transport
guests in electric vehicles, increasingly
prevalent since Ethiopia banned the
import of petrol-powered cars in 2024.
Yet Ethiopia is also a reminder of the

continent’s vulnerability to climate
change. In Africa’s second-most populous
country, 60% of people work in agriculture.
Warmer and more volatile weather has
complicated efforts to improve cereal
yields. More frequent droughts imperil the
tens of millions of animals raised in
Africa’s largest livestock producer. The
country’s famous coffee plants are at risk
from erratic rainfall and higher
temperatures. Floods that killed over 100
people in March can be seen as harbingers

of a future in which natural disasters are
more common and more dangerous.
Ethiopia’s experience reﬂects how,
despite efforts to respond to climate
change, Africa remains at the mercy of
external shocks. El Niño, a weather pattern
which adds to climate woes (see
International section), will highlight that
fragility ahead of COP. So, too, will the Iran
war, which has raised the cost of
agricultural inputs.
Quite how the weather and the war
affect Africa will vary across the

continent’s 54 countries. Sahelian
countries, such as Niger and Burkina Faso,
might suffer from drier conditions because
of El Niño, but they use relatively little
fertiliser. Kenya and Tanzania use more,
but they are forecast to have above-average
rainfall. Southern Africa faces a double
whammy. Drier conditions are associated
with lower maize harvests. South Africa,

Though cereal yields in sub-Saharan Africa
have improved since the 1960s, Asia’s have
grown faster. Agricultural productivity in
the region has stagnated in the 2020s,
according to the Food and Agricultural

borrowing costs for countries vulnerable to

At COP32, African countries can cite
examples of how they are dealing with a
hotter planet. In 2025 renewable sources
accounted for more of Africa’s generated

electricity than coal for the ﬁrst time. Many
countries are promoting “green bonds”
and drought-resistant seeds.
Yet African policymakers, with
justiﬁcation, feel let down by the rest of the
world. In Paris in 2015 they signed up to
pledges to limit their carbon emissions. In
exchange they were promised aid, labelled
as “climate ﬁnance”, that would help them
grow and green their economies. The
money received has fallen far below the
pledges. Africa receives less than a quarter

of the climate ﬁnance it needs, says Carlos
Lopes of the University of Cape Town. The
new era of aid cuts means there will
probably be even less on offer at this COP.
Africa is more resilient than outsiders
assume. But as the shocks experienced in
the coming year will show, there is a limit
to the blows the continent can withstand
without them affecting its potential. ●

HERE COULD hardly have been a stark-

er contrast between the two superpow-
ers’ attitudes towards Africa. For much of
2026, America was focused on grubby
third-party deportation deals. The Trump
administration has now signed agreements
with at least 12 African countries to send
them deported migrants and criminals. It
also raised tariffs. China, by contrast,
scrapped tariffs on almost all imports from
Africa. Yet the positive economic impact of
this diplomatic masterstroke, announced

in May, was hard to discern: in the ﬁrst
eight months of the year Africa’s trade
deﬁcit with China grew by 34% to $80bn.
That deﬁcit will widen further in 2027.
As rich-world trade barriers spread, China
is rerouting more of its exports to Africa.
Yet trade imbalances are not the whole
story of the continent’s economic relations
with its biggest bilateral lender. Less no-
ticed is the surge in private Chinese in-
vestment, particularly in manufacturing. In

the ﬁrst six months of 2026 Chinese in-
vestment announced across all sectors in
Africa reached $33.5bn, up 254% year on
year, according to Christoph Nedopil of
the University of Queensland. Much of this
was pledged by private companies. In
Ethiopia a single Chinese ﬁrm promised
more than $14bn in investments, from
green-ammonia production to electrical-
equipment manufacturing.
Many Chinese companies, such as
carmakers, are building factories abroad in

order to skirt tariffs and maintain access to
Western markets (see Business section). In
Africa the bigger draw is often the prospect

▸ of selling to African consumers. Proﬁts
inside China continue to be squeezed, and
competition is ﬁerce. In Africa “the margin
differential tends to be higher than in most
other places, for almost every product,”
says Kartik Jayaram of McKinsey, a
consultancy. China’s economic
engagement has long been deﬁned by vast
public-infrastructure investments, but
ﬂows of private capital into industrial
projects will play a much bigger role.

The ﬂow will not just be into factories
that stitch clothes and assemble mobile
phones. Those ﬁrst appeared over 20 years
ago. Africa’s nascent renewable-energy
industries, such as solar panels and electric
vehicles (EVs), will be most affected.
Countries like Ethiopia and Kenya, with
growing demand for electricity, and
governments keen to promote local
manufacturing, will beneﬁt most. Huajian
Group, which arrived in Ethiopia in the
early 2010s to set up a shoe factory, is

moving into EV assembly. Dongfeng, a
carmaker, is doing the same in Kenya.
Chinese factories will be a disruptive
force. A steel plant that opened in
Zimbabwe in 2024 is gobbling up market
share across southern Africa and forcing
rival operations to close. Chinese
cement-makers are muscling into some of
Africa’s biggest economies. Established
electric-motorcycle ﬁrms in east Africa,
many of which have Western founders but
import components from China, face little

competition from Chinese rivals for now.
But that is unlikely to last, says Tom
Courtright, a Nairobi-based researcher.
Chinese ﬁrms, he notes, “innovate faster,
work harder, accept lower margins, and
probably have better access to capital”.
This will fuel fears that China is more
likely to hobble than help African attempts
to industrialise. Over the past four decades
manufacturing’s share of GDP in
sub-Saharan Africa as a whole has fallen
from 18% in 1981 to just 10% in 2024. Mo

Dewji, a Tanzanian industrialist and one of
the continent’s richest men, is not alone in
believing indigenous textile industries
died “because people are importing
products from China and India”.
Generally, though, Africa should
beneﬁt as Chinese ﬁrms shift from simply
exporting their wares to establishing
production locally. Evidence from new
Chinese factories in South-East Asia
suggests they often rely on imported
Chinese components. But with time,

predicts Carlos Oya of the School of
Oriental and African Studies in London,
Chinese factories in Africa will create their
own domestic supply chains. New jobs and
technology transfer should follow. It will
spark competition, which should also help.
The African ﬁrms carrying out basic
assembly today could be the cutting-edge
manufacturers of tomorrow. ●
S
INCE 2018 policymakers from Addis
Ababa to Accra have spent a lot of time
preaching the gospel of the African

Continental Free Trade Agreement
(AfCFTA). The plan has long promised
more integration for Africa, and
opportunities for the continent’s
economies to interact with each other as
much as they do with the rest of the world.
Yet in material terms it has not been hugely
transformative. In 2027 that could start to
change. The ﬂagship intracontinental
payments system at the heart of the plan is
ﬁnally gaining ground.
The Pan-African Payments Settlements

System (PAPSS) launched in 2022, but only
became available on most people’s mobile
phones—where Africans primarily do their
banking—in 2026. With the help of African
Export-Import Bank (Afreximbank), the
body behind it, Africans across the
continent can now pay each other more
quickly. PAPSS works in 30 African
countries and transaction volumes have
risen by 1,000% since 2025. Much of this
growth has come from Nigeria. As PAPSS
expands to more countries in 2027, usage

will increase further. Some expect Africa’s
entire cross-border payment market will be
worth $1trn by 2035 with its help.
It is a welcome innovation. Until a few
years ago, transfers even between neigh-
bouring African countries required a slow,
costly journey through dollars ﬁrst, in the
form of the SWIFT global payments
network. The paperwork at each end could
take days, and businesses and consumers
alike were hostages to the opening hours of
their banks. In some countries startups

provided nifty app-based alternatives, but
with varying degrees of success. Kenya,
Nigeria and South Africa, where banking
infrastructure was more advanced, sped
ahead. But much of the continent lagged.
The aim of PAPSS is to ease and
cheapen cross-border payments for
everyone. A Rwandan airline can transfer
funds from its Accra office; Nigerian
students studying in Ghana can more
easily pay their school fees; Malawians
working in Mozambique can send money

home. Facilitating such everyday
transactions should bring Africans closer.
Some worry that PAPSS has been too
slow and has been superseded already. In
the years before it went live, ﬁntech
companies in some countries found
workarounds to accomplish the same
goals. Payaza, for example, handles $1.9bn
in cross-border transactions a month, for
clients like Kenya Airways and Adidas.
Using digital currencies, such as dollar-
linked stablecoins, is quicker than PAPSS,

says Payaza’s boss, Seyi Ebenezer.
Politics and stubborn central-bank
governors have also hampered PAPSS’s
progress. South Africa, for example, which
is Africa’s largest economy, has refused to
join it. But after all the delays, PAPSS is at
last getting into ordinary Africans’ hands.
By the end of 2027, its backers expect
transfer costs to fall to just 1% of each
transaction. Let the money ﬂow. ●
