S
OME CHANGES, once begun, play out unstoppably,
easing the prophet’s burden. The trade winds in the
tropical Paciﬁc, which blow from east to west, normal-
ly keep the ocean’s hottest waters penned into a “warm
pool” on its western side. It is the hottest seawater on
the planet. But two to four times a decade these trade
winds falter in a way that lets water spread towards the
east. This weakens the trade winds further, allowing

the warm pool’s waters to spread farther still to the
east. The process—known as El Niño—feeds on itself
for about six months, pushing the temperature in the
Not just a
warm-up
The effects of the outsize El Niño will
be brutal, and responses insufficient
Oliver Morton Senior editor and
planetary-affairs editor, The Economist

74 Mapping conﬂict

⏩
central and eastern Paciﬁc higher, until a slower,
countervailing oceanic response takes hold.
The peak of the current El Niño, which began in
June 2026, is expected at the end of November; the
build-up of heat in the atmosphere, which lags what is
happening at sea, will come about three months later,
in early 2027. This is pretty normal timing for an El
Niño. What is not normal is this event’s size. The

strength of an El Niño is measured by comparing the
temperature in a particular part of the central-eastern
Paciﬁc with its long-term average. This anomaly is off
the charts (in some cases literally). Since mid-
September it has been higher than it was at the peak of
the 2015-16 El Niño—until now the strongest on record.
That one caused widespread crop failures and live-
stock losses, pushing 60m people into food insecurity,
according to the UN’s World Food Programme (WFP).

El Niño’s effects are global because, as the heat in
the Paciﬁc redistributes itself, the overlying atmos-
phere heats up and the geographical distribution of
high-pressure regions and low-pressure regions shifts.
In some places where pressure is usually high, it drops;
in some of those where it is usually low, it increases.
This see-sawing, most dramatic over the Paciﬁc and
Indian oceans, ripples through the rest of the tropics,
changing weather patterns around the world.

The resulting regional changes are broadly
predictable. Many of those expected early on in the
process have already made themselves felt. The Indian
monsoon was weak, leading to lower crop yields and
depleted reservoirs. Indonesia has been very dry—and
C002

### Page 70

70 International The World Ahead 2027
▸ as a result has been having the worst ﬁre season since
2015-16. In the Atlantic, where El Niño’s effect on
winds at high altitudes suppresses storms, the
hurricane season was remarkably quiet. As expected,
the effect in the eastern Paciﬁc has been the opposite:
there it has been the most violent hurricane season on
record. California, where El Niño tends not just to
bring wet weather but also increases sea levels a little,

is braced for storms.
More will follow in 2027. The build-up of heat in the
atmosphere means it is certain to be the hottest year
on record worldwide. Human systems add to the lag. If
the harvest fails in southern Africa this southern
summer, the worst impacts on food security will be felt
only in the next growing season, in late 2027.
While climatic goings-on complicate the picture of
what weather can be expected, economic and political
events will complicate the situation when it comes to

impact. America’s war with Iran has driven up the costs
of fuel and fertiliser. Grain exports from Ukraine and
Russia were much lower after the 2026 harvest than in
previous years of their war. Venezuela and Colombia,
at risk of drought, are recovering from earthquakes.
Even so, over time, the overall predictability of El
Niño provides opportunities for pre-emptive action to
fend off the worst effects. The past decade has seen
much progress in ﬁnding ways to turn forecasts into

action, both among humanitarian organisations and in
speciﬁc countries. The Philippines now has a law that
lets the government declare “imminent disasters”, thus
allowing various sorts of action and spending.
In May the WFP and the UN’s Food and Agriculture
Organisation launched an appeal to donor nations for
$202m to fund anticipatory action, including cash
transfers and agricultural assistance. The plan
targeted 22 countries where the organisations thought

the risks were high. Unfortunately, by October only
around $40m had been donated; some of the windows
for anticipatory intervention have already closed.
The link between El Niño and global warming, if
any, is unclear. Some simply see an age-old oscillation
imposed on the warming trend. Others think that
warming is changing the character of El Niño in
various ways. Or is El Niño shaping the warming?
Kevin Trenberth, a leading authority, sees evidence

that warming is going up not smoothly, but in steps,
with the events of 1998-99 and 2015-16 playing the role
of the risers. If he is right, this El Niño will take the
planet somewhere it has never been before. The need
for pre-emptive action has never been greater. ●

How we did
in 2026
“W
ATCH VENEZUELA ,”
we wrote in The
World Ahead 2026. We said
Donald Trump “may even
deploy special forces if he
sees a chance to capture or kill
Nicolás Maduro”. On January
3rd Mr Trump did just that,
capturing the Venezuelan
president in a daring raid on

Caracas. But one success does
not guarantee another, as Mr
Trump found—and as did we.
An emboldened Mr Trump
went on, in conjunction with
Israel, to launch a war on Iran
on February 28th. We had not
expected this. The most likely
scenario, we wrote, was that
“Israel tries to persuade Mr
Trump to strike Iran again,
hoping to topple the regime,
but he refuses.” He had, after
all, been elected on a promise
not to start any new wars in
the Middle East. But after his

triumph in Caracas, he
expected a swift capitulation
from the regime in Tehran,
which never came. In com-
mon with Mr Trump, then, the
war in Iran was our biggest
miss of 2026.
We did get some other
things right. We noted that
Ayatollah Ali Khamenei’s son,
Mojtaba, was someone to
watch as a “successor for the
top job, should it become
vacant”, which it did when
Iran’s supreme leader was
killed by an air strike on the
ﬁrst day of the war. We
warned that the global ship-

ping industry faced “treacher-
ous waters” in 2026, given the
risk of “an Iranian closure of
the Strait of Hormuz, or re-
newed Houthi attacks in the
Red Sea”. We said the fragile
ceaseﬁre in Gaza would hold.
Looking beyond the Mid-
dle East, we said ﬁghting
would grind on in Ukraine,
and that Viktor Orban would
probably lose the election in
Hungary. We anticipated that
Russia would intensify its
grey-zone provocations in
Europe. And we said the line

between war and peace would
get more blurred, which it did:
is the Iran war on or off?
On economics, we were
right to say the Supreme
Court “was likely to curtail”
Mr Trump’s power to set
tariffs at his total discretion.
We said tourists would be
wary of visiting America, even
for the World Cup. And we
asked if further progress in
artiﬁcial intelligence would
lead to a boom, a bust or a
backlash, “or some combina-
tion of all three”. In the event,

AI delivered a boom for some
that felt like a bust to many
others, causing a backlash.
There are two lessons
here. The ﬁrst is that events
can interact in unexpected
ways. The raid on Caracas,
which we expected, contrib-
uted to Mr Trump’s decision
to attack Iran, which we
didn’t. The second lesson is
that people do not always
make choices based on logic
and evidence. For decades,
America has refrained from
attacking Iran, for fear that it
would close the Strait of

Hormuz. We assumed this
would stay Mr Trump’s hand.
But world leaders often
make decisions on the basis
of emotion or ideology, or
with an eye on their legacy.
Forecasters may see the world
through the lens of logic, but
it is all too easy for them to
forget that others may not.
A look back at last
year’s predictions

I
N 2026 ARTIﬁCIAL-INTELLIGENCE models ran
amok on the public internet, solved some of the
hardest problems in mathematics and helped America
ﬁnd targets to bomb in Iran. Its rapid and seemingly
relentless advance has triggered intense debates over
how to keep AI under human control while harnessing
its creative power. In 2027 AI models will further
scramble politics, in America and abroad.
One certainty is that they will become even more

powerful and ubiquitous, serving as personal
assistants and interacting with one another on behalf
of their human masters. These trends will intensify
concerns about AI safety—that models will be misused
by bad actors, or will unintentionally do harm as they
roam the internet. In America, candidates eyeing the
2028 presidential election will ﬂesh out their positions
on the subject. Some AI insiders think recursive
self-improvement (RSI)—when models can build

better versions of themselves, sparking a virtuous (or
vicious) spiral—could arrive in 2027. It could occur
suddenly, in secrecy and with little warning.
Competition between America’s big AI labs, and
America’s fear of losing its edge to China, means a halt
in AI development is unlikely, barring a catastrophe.
The Trump administration’s refusal to consider
antitrust waivers means AI ﬁrms cannot coordinate
with one another and will not trust each other to stop.

Trust is even lower between countries.
But if RSI seems to be looming, American ﬁrms
could slow down the training of new AI models, as they
did brieﬂy in the summer of 2026 after AI agents
escaped “sandbox” environments, and could put in
place new safety measures, such as more intrusive
third-party audits. Safety worries could spur research
into new ways of verifying AI arms-control-type
agreements, such as monitoring how chips are being
used in foreign data centres. This could be costly.

AI nationalism will grow. In September, America
initially declined to make a new Anthropic model
available for testing by Britain’s world-leading AI
Security Institute, but changed its mind 17 days later.
New models could face stricter quarantine periods,
during which select American government bodies and
ﬁrms get access before wider release. The gap between
internal models and publicly available ones will widen.
Middle powers will become more concerned about

lagging behind America and China. Three-quarters of
the world’s AI computing power is in America; less
than 5% is in Europe. That disparity is unlikely to
narrow. One key test will be the European Union’s
speed in building up to seven new “gigafactories” for
AI. Successful bidders should be announced in 2027.
The Sovereign Technology Alliance, a Canadian-
German club to pool computing power and strengthen
AI co-operation, could pick up new members.

AI chips and other components will remain in short
supply. And as energy becomes a larger constraint in
America and elsewhere, places with cheaper
power—such as the Gulf, Australia, Canada and the
Nordics—could attract more investment.
The status of open-source models—some of which
can be downloaded and run on local computers rather
than distant cloud servers—will also become more
contentious. On current trends, by the end of 2027 if
not sooner, open-source models, which include most

Chinese ones, will reach the same standard as Mythos,
the Anthropic model whose prowess triggered
American export controls in June.
That could grant rogue actors access to advanced
cyber and biological know-how, unencumbered by the
guardrails baked into closed models. In 2026 Chinese
spies, Iranian officials and Houthi rebels all used
Anthropic’s Claude model for nefarious tasks, though
they were caught in the process. They might simply
turn to open-source alternatives.

As such open-source models boom, China will face
pressure to clamp down through regulation. The
Trump administration will push ﬁrms to stop China
“distilling” American models (using their outputs to
improve others) and will debate whether to ban the use
of Chinese open-source AI altogether.
Governments will also keep harnessing the
technology for their own spooks and soldiers.
Technologies used by America in Iran and by Russia
and Ukraine will spread quickly to other countries.

Humans will have to work harder to stay in control. ●
Genies and
bottles
WASHINGTON, DC
The year ahead will decide much about
the future of artiﬁcial intelligence
Shashank Joshi Washington bureau chief,

I
T IS SURELY the worst job in
international diplomacy. A new UN
secretary-general is supposed to take
office on January 1st 2027, though it may
take longer for the world’s leading powers
to pick a candidate.
The UN’s new boss will be a lamb
among wolves. Big states regard the body
with indifference, if not hostility. Its
central pillar—that all should abjure “the
threat or use of force against the territorial

integrity or political independence of any
state”—is crumbling. Russia has invaded
Ukraine. China threatens Taiwan and
harasses its neighbours. Scornful of
universal rules, America seized the leader
of Venezuela, went to war with Iran and, in
effect, threatened to annex Canada.
What order replaces the old is still
unclear. At best it is a world of surviving
but marginalised institutions; at worst, a
gangster’s paradise. President Donald
Trump claims not to need international

law, saying he will be constrained only by
“my own morality”. He has pulled America
out of dozens of international bodies,
among them UNESCO (the UN’s cultural
agency) and the World Health
Organisation. His administration wants to
destroy the International Criminal Court.
He has twice left the Paris climate accords,
and blocked efforts to levy fees on
greenhouse-gas emissions from shipping.
“There is no global government—and
while I’m president, there will be no global

taxes,” he told the UN General Assembly in
September. At least Mr Trump turned up
for the UN’s annual talkfest. Vladimir
Putin, the Russian president, stayed at
home. And China’s leader, Xi Jinping,
visited Mr Trump in Washington the same
week but skipped New York.
International rules matter most to
smaller countries. Mark Carney, Canada’s
prime minister, has cast himself as the
champion of middle powers seeking to
stand up to both America and China. The

European Union has proposed a closer
relationship with Canada; what that means
may be deﬁned in 2027. European allies
now fully arm and ﬁnance Ukraine in its
war with Russia; they also pushed Mr
Trump to give up on annexing Greenland,
a Danish territory. Faced with his tariff war,
the EU struck trade agreements with
Mercosur and India. Middle powers,
however, are largely bystanders in the
Sino-American contest for AI dominance.
Allies will not dare break with America,

hoping to weather the Trumpian storm.
Those who feel most threatened by China
and Russia know that America’s might
offers the best—if not only—means of
deterring big rapacious neighbours.
Poland and the Baltic states want to host
more, not fewer, American forces in 2027.
Japan is revamping its own armed forces in
order to integrate better with America’s.
Australia will not hear of a Plan B for
AUKUS, a programme to develop
nuclear-powered submarines based on

American and British technology.
A world of gangsters might calm things
for a while, though it would be the peace of
the graveyard for some if America forced
Ukraine to capitulate to Russia, or let
China take Taiwan. Messrs Trump, Xi and
Putin may yet strike a three-way deal that,
Yalta-style, could carve the world into
spheres of inﬂuence.
Mob bosses often respect each
other—until they don’t. In the cold war the
communist and capitalist worlds lived

under the shadow of nuclear war. Their
spheres clashed, for instance, over the
status of Berlin and the deployment of
Soviet missiles to Cuba.
Where, exactly, would the American
sphere end and the Chinese one begin? Mr
Trump may talk of dominating the western
hemisphere. But he showed in Iran that his
interests don’t end there—and that his
errors have consequences for all. The UN’s
new boss, sometimes called a “secular
pope”, will be praying for a miracle that

somehow preserves global rules. ●
Paradigm shift
Big powers tear down the old order;
smaller ones try to band together
Anton La Guardia Diplomatic editor,
The Economist
T
O BE ABLE to use the product the
vendor must be satisﬁed that the
customer is of appropriate age. It is banned
in school playgrounds. Designing the
product in such a way that it might appeal
to (and cause addiction in) children is
prohibited. Executives in the industry that
makes this product have been hauled up to

testify in front of American lawmakers. Its
biggest ﬁrms face the prospect of
enormous ﬁnes. This was all true of the
tobacco industry in the 1990s, and will be
true of the social-media industry in 2027.
Meta agreed to pay $12bn, and
potentially as much as $17bn, in damages
over the next ten years in a deal struck in
August 2026 with 47 states and the District
of Columbia. (It reached a separate
settlement with Texas for $1bn.) The
company also agreed to restrict the use of

Facebook and Instagram by younger users,
through overnight blackouts and limits on
scrolling time. In 2026 many countries,
including Brazil, Britain, Denmark,
Indonesia, Malaysia and Spain, as well as
the European Union, have joined Australia
in proposing or passing laws banning
social media for young teens.
There will only be more of this in 2027.
Included in the American settlement is, in
effect, a bounty on the rest of the
social-media industry: Meta will hand over

more money and impose even tighter
usage controls if the states reach similar
settlements with the other giants of the
industry, such as Snapchat, TikTok and
YouTube. No wonder people are calling
this social media’s “Big Tobacco” moment.
But is that accurate?
Superﬁcially, there are parallels, in the
form of huge ﬁnes, new legal restrictions
and tighter regulations. But dig a little
deeper and the analogy comes undone.
The payout is much smaller. The

settlement reached with tobacco ﬁrms in
1998 involved a monetary penalty larger
than their combined market value at the
time. For the tech ﬁrms, that would
correspond to trillions of dollars, but Meta
will hand over less than a tenth of a single
year’s revenue, spread over a decade.
To the last scroll

A
DECADE AFTER Donald Trump began
his ﬁrst term as America’s president,
and in spite of the hit he has taken in the
midterm elections, the populist wave
shows no sign of breaking. Right-wing
populists are at or near the top of the
opinion polls in Australia, Britain, France
and Germany. Left-populists are on the
rise, too. Few elections in 2027 will bring
populist politicians to high office (though
France’s presidential election might), but

their views will inﬂuence debate.
Migration is still the populist right’s
main bugbear. Those politicians used to
focus on illegal immigration. But tougher
border policies have reduced arrivals in
America and Europe, so the emphasis has
shifted to those who have already arrived.
Mr Trump has reined in the theatrical
immigration enforcement of early 2026.
But his deportation machine is running at
full throttle. And he and his supporters
have launched attacks on whole

communities of legal immigrants, notably
Somalis in Minnesota. Populists across the
West have taken to describing some
cultures as “incompatible” with Western
values. Nigel Farage’s Reform UK vows to
leave the European Convention on Human
Rights (ECHR) and to repeal domestic
human-rights laws that slow down
expulsions. In Australia, Pauline Hanson’s
One Nation party proposes withdrawing
from the Refugee Convention altogether.
Paying migrants to leave, a policy favoured

by Sweden’s hard right, may gain support.
“Remigration” will move further into
mainstream politics. Its advocates,
including the Alternative for Germany,
focus on “non-assimilated” migrants as
targets for removal. In some countries
there may be efforts to rank citizens based
on their ethnicity or linguistic ﬂuency.
Marine Le Pen, who leads the polls in
France, wants a referendum on banning
the Muslim headscarf.
As for left-populists, the economy is

more natural turf. Market-meddling is back
in vogue. Zohran Mamdani, New York’s
Democratic Socialist mayor, and Britain’s
Greens are fans of rent controls. Levies on
wealth are popular, too. Die Linke, a
far-left German party, wants one-off taxes
on the richest 0.7%, with 10% levies on
wealth over €2m ($2.3m). Jean-Luc
Mélenchon, a populist-left French
politician, says he wants to cancel a chunk
of France’s national debt.
Strikingly, fears about AI in America

have brought together populists on the
right, such as Steve Bannon, a former
Trump adviser, and those on the left, like
Bernie Sanders, a socialist senator. While
Mr Trump looks to boost AI, many local
Republicans have joined with Democrats
to campaign against data centres. In the
Senate, Josh Hawley, a Republican, and
Mark Warner, a Democrat, are pushing
legislation to force companies to report
AI-related job losses. Mr Warner has also
proposed a special tax on data centres.

Most such policies will not come to
pass. But populists do not need to be in
power to shape policy. In Britain, the
Conservatives have joined Reform in
saying they would leave the ECHR. Other
mainstream politicians, especially on the
right, are also likely to embrace populists’
ideas as their own. ●
Populists to the
right, populists
to the left
Both sides offer a mix of old and new
Fraser McIlwraith Foreign correspondent,
The Economist
Or consider what the deal means for

social-media use in future. The tobacco
moment came decades after smoking had
begun to fall out of favour as a pastime: the
share of adults who smoked had peaked in
the 1960s. The harms to smokers and to
others around them were already
indisputable. The settlement was
retribution for harm done, but it was
followed by laws designed to marginalise
smoking in everyday life: bans on
aeroplanes, in restaurants, in workplaces.
Today smoking is not permitted in public

housing and would often violate a private
lease agreement. A habit that was once
universally accepted became concentrated
among poorer, less educated people.
The restrictions on social-media use
that Meta has agreed to are draconian. The
blackouts and time limits are almost as
strict as those proposed by the Chinese
Communist Party. Squint and you can see
some of the policing of social spaces that
made smoking taboo. Phones are now
banned at some concerts and cafés. A class

divide is opening up with screen time, too:
well-off parents, including tech executives,
restrict their children’s screen time. Heavy
screen use among children is increasingly
linked to lower income.
Yet it is almost impossible to imagine a
world in which anyone would consider
scolding a child just for being on a phone.
Unlike cigarettes, smartphones are
indispensable to modern life. Curbing use
in the teenage years is unlikely to prevent
enthusiastic adoption later on. Old people

now use social media almost as much as
their grandchildren. Instead, a much better
analogy might be Americans’ relationship
with another vice: alcohol. It is out of
bounds for children and teenagers but
enjoyed, in varying degrees of moderation
and excess, by adults. ●

B
EYOND THE headlines of America’s war with Iran
and Ukraine’s battling with Russia, many other
deadly conﬂicts are rumbling on. And as new weapons
are developed and adopted, and the global order
continues to decay, these may become harder to ignore
in the coming year.
More people live close to conﬂict than you might
expect. Our analysis of data from Armed Conﬂict
Location & Event Data (ACLED), a conﬂict monitor,

ﬁnds that in the year to September 15th 2026, at least
300m people lived less than 5km (three miles) from a
clash where two or more people were killed in political
violence. And in 25 countries, there were more than 100
such deadly incidents over that period.
The total number killed in conﬂict is unknown.
Because ACLED’s records are based on media reports,
they are patchy in poor and violent places. And it is not
obvious which clashes to count. The line between

criminal and revolutionary can be hard to draw, as can
the line between government and bandit.
What is clear is that conﬂicts around the world are
changing. By mid-September 2026, drones had been
used in deadly attacks in at least 36 countries, more
than in the whole of 2025. Starlink and other satellite-
communication devices were seized or destroyed in
clashes in Iran, Myanmar, Ukraine and Venezuela.
Some gangs may now have more practical
experience with the kit dominating modern warfare

than many conventional armies do. In May Colombia’s
then president, Gustavo Petro, told his troops, among
the most seasoned in Latin America, that “narco-
traffickers have the aerial advantage.”
The global effects of this technological revolution
are not theoretical. The Houthis, who control parts of
Yemen, have ﬁred on commercial shipping in the Bab
al-Mandab strait, prompting many vessels to take
alternative routes. In normal times, ships carrying 16%

of seaborne trade by value would pass through it.
The Houthis are not the only rebel group near a
critical waterway. Sudan’s coastline remains a prize in
the country’s civil war, which is probably the world’s
deadliest conﬂict after that between Russia and
Ukraine. In southern Thailand, not far from the Strait
of Malacca, an insurgency is now in its third decade.
And across the South China Sea in the Philippines,
communist rebels are nearly 60 years into theirs.

Great powers, too, may see opportunity in the
disorder. In 2026 America toppled and captured
Venezuela’s dictator. In Myanmar, China works with
both the blood-soaked regime and a vast array of
armed rebel groups to advance its agenda. In Africa’s
Sahel, Islamists and others continue to be egged on by
Russia and its mercenary forces.
It is easy to assume that long-running, small-scale
insurgencies will remain small. But history is shaped
by exceptions. In recent years, global disorder and new

weapons have helped small groups expand into forces
able to defy great powers. That is something that
leaders in America and China, for different reasons,
would do well to remember. ●

### Page 75

75The World Ahead 2027 International
M
UCH OF THE talk about
the outlook for energy
markets in 2027 involves the
word “normalisation”. It
suggests that after a year of
turmoil in 2026, things could
return to something close to
where they were before the
start of the Iran war. I’m
sceptical. I don’t see any
credible pathway back to the
situation that existed in
February 2026. Shipping ﬂows
through the Strait of Hormuz
have been blocked once, with a

hugely disruptive effect on oil
and gas markets. It can happen
again. Conﬁdence in the strait
as a safe and reliable corridor
for energy and other goods has
been crushed.
So even as I hope that 2027
brings more stable energy
supplies and markets—and
that fuel prices come down
from their painfully high levels
as soon as possible—I think
perceptions of risk around the
strait have changed for good.
Countries around the world
will want to reduce their

exposure to disruption. This
will have consequences for
2027 and beyond.
Trade will remain very
important to the energy sector,
but countries will pay more
attention to diversiﬁcation.
They will pursue a more varied
mix of fuels and energy
technologies, of suppliers and
trade routes. Those new routes
will include pipelines out of the
Middle East that bypass
Hormuz (the United Arab
Emirates and Saudi Arabia are
already moving ahead rapidly
on these). The reality is that the

global energy system can
diversify away from the strait
over time, but the Middle East
will remain vital for energy
security and markets.
Countries will give
preference to domestically
available energy sources, where
economics and resource
endowments allow, especially
among fuel-importing
countries. The biggest
beneﬁciaries of this are likely
to be renewables, particularly
solar and wind, given how
cost-competitive they are.
The trend should also add

momentum to nuclear power’s
comeback. But rapid
expansions of new power
sources will need grids and
other key elements of
electricity systems, such as
battery storage, to avoid
bottlenecks and wasted energy
and money. Countries with
fossil-fuel resources inside
their own borders may ﬁnd it
hard not to use them,
particularly in the case of coal
in parts of Asia.
Key energy technologies
that people use in their daily
lives will also be affected.
Spikes in oil and gas prices in

2026 have made the case for
electrical products, such as
electric vehicles (EVs) and heat
pumps, more compelling. EV
sales have enjoyed triple-digit
growth in dozens of countries,
from Australia to India to
Brazil, since the Hormuz crisis
began. As well as reducing
energy bills, widespread and
sustained uptake of electrical
products could help make a
dent in global emissions.
This energy crisis has also
underscored the value of
preparedness. Member

countries of the International
Energy Agency were able to
draw on emergency oil stocks
to soften the economic impact
of supply disruptions. Other
countries around the world are
looking to build up similar
buffers, such as the Philippines
and Thailand in South-East
Asia, one of the regions hit
hardest by the energy shock.
Such moves are under-
standable in a world in which
uncertainty, volatility and
unpredictability have become
leitmotifs. Amid the concerns

about resource shortages, the
scarcest commodity of all may
be trust. And a low-trust world
is deﬁnitely not a low-cost one.
Building up buffers and adding
backup capacity need extra
spending. “Just in case”
systems tend to cost more to
operate than the “just in time”
systems that ﬂourished as
trade became more globalised
in recent decades.
It is easy to be disorientated
by all the uncertainty. Even the
picture for the next few months
is extremely hazy. What will

happen in the overlapping
conﬂicts in the Middle East,
and how will they affect energy
supplies globally? What
impact will El Niño have on
weather patterns and energy
systems around the world?
How successfully will Europe
navigate a tight natural-gas
supply this winter? How will
rising interest rates affect
ﬁnancing for new energy
projects, especially in
developing economies where
they are most sorely needed?
There are many such
questions. And more will surely

arise. Uncertainty is now the
rule, not the exception, so
energy decision-makers need
to be wary of creating new
dependencies, even as they
seek to address old ones.
Above all, they need to look
through the noise and
prioritise the fuels,
technologies and partners that
will deliver secure, affordable
and sustainable energy in the
years ahead. ●
Energy markets are not going back to the way they were
.
I don’t see any credible
pathway back to the
situation that existed in

February 2026

The age-dependency ratio
compares the number of
dependents (people under 15,
or over 64, who are deemed too
young or too old to work) with
the number of working-
age people (those aged 15-64).
This ratio tends to be lower in
wealthy countries (where low
fertility means there are fewer
dependent children) and
higher in poorer countries
(where fertility is higher).
Globally, the ratio has been
falling for decades. But it is

about to start rising as birth
rates drop and populations age,
increasing the number of
elderly dependents. This is
most dramatic in China, a
demographic echo of its now-
discontinued one-child policy.

Charting the changing share of
global GDP at purchasing-
power parity (that is, adjusting
output ﬁgures to take account
of lower costs in poorer
countries) provides a wonkish
perspective on the big picture
of world history over the past
two centuries. China’s
economy is the largest at the
start of the period shown, but
the British Empire claims the

top spot after the Opium Wars
of the 1840-50s, until Britain is
in turn pushed aside by
America in the early 20th
century; then a resurgent
China retakes the lead in the
early 21st century. In short, the
story of the past two centuries
is that China falls, then rises
again; and America rises, but
its power is now waning, at
least when measured by share
of world GDP. Britain, Japan
and Russia are no longer the
global powers they once were;
India is on the rise; France and

Germany have retained their
clout by clubbing together
within the European Union.
Looking ahead, Goldman
Sachs, an investment bank,
predicts that by 2075 the top
ﬁve economies will be China,
India, America, Indonesia and
Nigeria, followed by Pakistan,
Egypt and Brazil. There is more
to history than economics, of
course. But political and
military power necessarily rest
on economic foundations. And
this chart shows how quickly
and dramatically great powers’

fortunes can change.

2027 forecast The age of coal has ended. In 2025 its share of global
electricity generation fell
below one-third for the ﬁrst
time in history. Coal has lost
share in every region of the
world in the past two decades;
renewables (solar, wind,
hydropower, biomass and
geothermal) have collectively
overtaken it everywhere except
in Asia. Globally, renewables
overtook coal in 2025. (This was
not the ﬁrst time this had
happened: amid a rush to build
hydroelectric dams in the early

20th century, renewables
brieﬂy overtook coal as an
electricity source in 1919.) But
despite the rapid growth of
renewables, cumulative
greenhouse-gas emissions are
probably too great to keep
warming at less than 1.5°C
above the pre-industrial
average. By 2026 the remaining
carbon “budget” for a 50%
chance of meeting this target
was just 130 gigatonnes of CO2
emissions. With annual
emissions running at about 42
gigatonnes, that budget will be
gone by the end of 2028.

The sum of imports and

provides a handy barometer for
the state of globalisation. It
shot up during the 1990s and

backlash, and a series of shocks
highlighted the risks of global
supply chains. But the line

merely plateaued. The
emphasis has shifted to intra-
regional trade and diversiﬁed
supply chains. Globalisation
has not gone away; it has
merely changed shape.

Of the ten biggest cities in the
world in 1950, only three—
Tokyo, Jakarta and Kolkata—
are still on that list in 2027, with
Jakarta leaping from tenth
place to the number-one spot.
By 2100 it will have fallen to
second place, swapping places
with Dhaka, now number two.
Rapid urbanisation has been
under way in Asia for decades,
and by 2100 Asian cities will
account for eight of the top ten.
The other two cities will be

African: Cairo and Luanda.
Africa is now urbanising faster
than any other part of the
world. But it is following a
different path: its urbanisation
is sometimes summarised as
“fast, late and poor”. Of the
45% of Africans who live in
cities, according to the UN,
more than half live in shanty-
towns or slums. Cities have
historically been engines of
prosperity, expanding as they
grow richer and drawing in
more workers. But in Africa a
lack of urban planning, and

meagre spending on infra-
structure and services, make it
harder for city-dwellers to
reap the economic beneﬁts
of agglomeration.

The Great Pyramid of Giza in
Egypt was the tallest man-
made structure in the world for
nearly 4,000 years after its
construction around 2550BC. It
was ﬁnally dethroned in a spate
of cathedral-building in
medieval Europe. Strasbourg
Cathedral was the world’s
tallest building from 1647 to
1874, when (amid another burst
of cathedral-building) it was
overtaken by St Nikolai Church

in Hamburg. Then skyscrapers
started going up in American
cities. The world’s tallest
buildings became temples to
Mammon, rather than God,
with many of them named after
companies, such as Woolworth
and Chrysler. And each new
tallest building could expect
only a few years, rather than
centuries, as top dog. At 828
metres, Burj Khalifa in the
United Arab Emirates has been
the tallest since 2010. But the
Jeddah Tower in Saudi Arabia,
under construction since 2014,

aspires to be the ﬁrst building
greater than 1km in height
(scaled down from an original
goal of one mile). After
repeated delays, will it ﬁnally
overtake Burj Khalifa in 2027?

S
INCE THE start of the artiﬁcial-intelligence boom,
tech-industry luminaries have predicted that
agents—bots that can act as well as chat—would soon
spread across the economy. Sam Altman, the boss of
OpenAI, said agents may “materially change the
output of companies” in 2025. Jensen Huang, the boss
of Nvidia, predicted that agentic AI would “take off” in
2025. Others applied the same label to 2026. Yet AI
agents mostly remained the preserve of software

developers, who tend to adopt new technologies
quickly. That will change in 2027.
According to OpenRouter, a marketplace for AI
models, the share of AI prompts that trigger agentic
activity, such as the use of tools, rose from 2% to 24%
from April to September 2026. Wider use of agents will
disrupt corporate costs, job roles, security and more.
Start with costs. Unlike a back-and-forth with a
chatbot, asking an agent to perform a particular task
can burn through tokens—the units in which AI

outputs are measured and billed—astonishingly fast,
particularly if an agent spawns more agents to help it.
In 2026 ﬁrms that encouraged employees to use AI as
much as possible (so-called “tokenmaxxing”) were hit
with huge bills. Uber, for example, burned through its
entire token budget for 2026 in four months.
This has forced companies to put more focus on
cost management, cost-beneﬁt analysis and using the
right model for the job. Data from Ramp, a ﬁrm that

tracks corporate spending, suggest users were slow to
adopt powerful new AI models introduced in the ﬁrst
half of 2026, preferring to stick with cheaper, less
sophisticated ones except for the most complex tasks.
According to McKinsey, a consultancy, a ﬁfth of ﬁrms
are limiting AI use to keep a lid on operating costs.
Companies are also making greater use of
open-weight models, which can deliver impressive
results at a fraction of the cost of proprietary models.

AT&T, a telecoms giant, uses open models for 25% of its
AI usage, and has set a long-term target of 70-80%. But
ﬁrms need to get better at evaluating agents’ output, to
check that they are using the most suitable model.
Management structures and job roles will also be
affected. Agents can gather information from
Agents of
change

▸ internal systems to keep an eye on workers’ output,
which may mean fewer managers are needed.
According to Korn Ferry, an employment consultancy,
two-ﬁfths of employees say their ﬁrms have trimmed
management layers since 2025, in part because of AI.
Meanwhile, employees of all kinds will spend more
time bossing agents around. This may be easier than
managing humans: agents do not ﬁght for recognition
or promotion, do not waste time having meetings and

are happy for other agents to use their work. But agents
will also blur the lines between job roles, for example
by allowing coders to do the work of designers, and
vice versa. That may cause tension between humans.
As agents are granted access to more systems and
asked to perform more complex tasks, they will need
supervision to ensure that they do not do unexpected
things. That highlights another way agents will disrupt
companies in 2027: by posing new security threats.

There were several incidents in 2026 in which agent
swarms went rogue and broke into other systems.
These were not deliberate attacks, but unintended
consequences of AI testing. But as cyber-criminals get
their hands on more capable models, a wave of
deliberate attacks by agent swarms appears to have
begun, which will intensify in the coming months.
Finally, companies will have to decide whether and
how to make information more readily available to

external agents, to facilitate information retrieval,
commerce and so forth. Some retailers have banned
agents from their websites; others welcome them.
Organisations are already struggling with “agentic
ﬂooding”: a deluge of AI-generated job applications,
complaints and legal challenges. In all kinds of ways,
these new AI tools really will be agents of change. ●

U
NTIL RECENTLY, most users of
artiﬁcial intelligence have interacted
with the technology by typing into a
chatbot and waiting for a reply. Tech ﬁrms
think that, for consumers, the future lies
not with text interfaces but with gadgets
that weave AI into daily life. Ideas about
what form such a device might take vary—
a badge, a key fob, a smart speaker or a pair

of glasses. But all hope to dethrone the
smartphone as the main personal device.
The ﬁrst challengers are already here.
Amazon, the world’s largest online
retailer, has launched Alexa+, a
conversational AI assistant that runs on its
Echo speakers. Meta, a social-media giant,
has put its faith in spectacles. Its smart
glasses, developed with EssilorLuxottica,
a Franco-Italian eyewear ﬁrm, contain a
camera, speakers and microphones
connected to Meta’s AI models. Wearers

can summon their AI assistant by
speaking. But as new gadgets arrive,
competition will intensify in 2027.
The ﬁrst is Meta’s Muse Charm, a
key-fob device that provides voice access
to Muse, Meta’s personal AI agent, and is
expected to go on sale in December 2026.
It will go up against a similar device from
OpenAI. The AI lab has teamed up with Sir
Jony Ive, the design guru behind some of
Apple’s biggest hits. OpenAI’s device is
said to resemble a hockey puck and to ﬁt in

one hand. It will control home appliances,
play songs and answer questions, but will
not have a display. Sam Altman, OpenAI’s
boss, has contrasted the device with
smartphones and their endless demands
for attention. Using his ﬁrm’s gadget, he
says, should feel like “sitting in the most
beautiful cabin by a lake”.
For its part Apple, one of the world’s
biggest smartphone-makers, is thought to
be developing a badge-like AI device. With
a camera and microphone, it would act as

extra eyes and ears for an iPhone running
an upgraded version of Siri, its AI assistant.
Apple is also said to be exploring smart
glasses, and earbuds with built-in cameras.
Such devices aim to extend AI into the
wider world, but will still rely almost
entirely on powerful AI models running in
distant data centres. Where possible,
handling AI requests on devices close to
the user (the so-called network “edge”) can
be cheaper and quicker. Already, for

example, smartphones can perform
translation or transcription using
on-device models. And sufficiently
capable desktop and laptop computers can
run more powerful AI models that rival the
leading cloud-based ones for some tasks.
This requires a powerful machine, like a
Mac Studio or a high-end gaming PC, and
set-up is ﬁddly. But running models locally
is fast, private and free (because the
models are “open-weight” ones such as
Qwen, Llama or GLM). With new AI

devices and local AI models, the edge of
the network is worth watching in 2027. ●

small thing?

I
N 1903 THE New York Times
ran an article suggesting that
the invention of a ﬂying
machine might take “from one
million to ten million years”.
Less than ten weeks later, the
Wright brothers took off at
Kitty Hawk. New technologies
can sneak up on us, going from
impossible to inevitable
seemingly overnight. For
engineers, however, it’s not a
surprise. Powered ﬂight was
made possible by steady
progress in several ﬁelds

(aerodynamics, lightweight
engines and bicycle design, in
the Wrights’ case), until the
technology punched through.
The Industrial Revolution
was full of these “punch-
through” moments. Seven
years before Kitty Hawk,
Thomas Edison literally
punched the table when Henry
Ford sketched out his plans for
an affordable, petrol-powered
car. Edison himself had
punched through 17 years
earlier with the ﬁrst practical
light bulb, at a time when most
lamps were fueled by whale oil.

I believe we are now
entering another era of
punch-through technologies.
In the ﬁrst quarter of the 21st
century, technological progress
was dominated by advances in
computing. But computing has
changed what we can design
and manufacture in the
physical world, making
possible things we have long
dreamed of, but imagined were
still beyond our reach.
How long, for instance, has
humanity yearned for a way to
defeat cancer? Now the
combination of tumour

sequencing, computing and
mRNA technology enables
Moderna to create vaccines
tailored to recognise and attack
each patient’s tumour. The
advent of reusable rockets,
mass-produced satellites and
electronically steered antennae
allows Starlink to bring high-
speed internet to people
anywhere on Earth—from far-
ﬂung villages to ships at sea.
These companies combine
the technology stacks of
internet giants with the
scientiﬁc expertise and
manufacturing capabilities of

industrial ﬁrms. I believe that
same combination could now
transform one of the world’s
biggest industries: energy.
Fusion power—making
energy by smashing atoms
together, rather than splitting
them—has long been energy’s
holy grail. The process that
powers the stars could give us a
virtually inexhaustible supply
of clean power from tiny
quantities of fuel. Once you
ﬁgure out how to build a
machine that essentially has a
star inside it, you can make

more of them. Endless energy,
powered by the physics of the
big bang, made in a factory.
This sounds quixotic, which
perhaps explains fusion’s long
association with science
ﬁction. Soviet scientists began
developing fusion machines in
the 1950s. Yet they were like the
gliders in the era before the
Wright brothers, showing that
an idea worked, but without
making it practical. Those
systems consumed more
energy than they produced.
When I arrived at the

Massachusetts Institute of
Technology nearly two
decades ago, however,
advances in other ﬁelds had
started to change what such
machines could do. More
powerful computers could
simulate how plasma behaves;
advanced manufacturing was
changing how fusion facilities
were designed and built; new
superconducting materials in
magnets let the fuel reach
star-like conditions inside
smaller machines. When we
founded Commonwealth
Fusion Systems in 2018, we
believed we could bring these

advances together to build a
machine to produce energy at
industrial scale and reliability.
That machine now exists, 35
miles outside Boston. It’s called
SPARC. In 2027 we will turn it
on, and soon thereafter
demonstrate that its plasma
can release more fusion energy
than we supply to heat it. It will
be fusion’s Kitty Hawk, its
punch-through moment. We
will have built the working
heart of a fusion power-station.
Hooking one up to the grid will
follow in the early 2030s.

SPARC—like the Wright
ﬂyer—is a demonstration of
what’s possible. For people
born today, a fusion power-
plant could become as ordinary
as an airport. It offers an
inexhaustible, emissions-free,
inﬁnitely scalable form of
energy, improving on a trajec-
tory deﬁned by computing and
manufacturing, not resource
extraction. It can help us
overcome long-standing
problems: poverty made worse
by unreliable electricity;
emissions from burning fossil
fuels; conﬂicts over access to

energy resources.
We are often wary of what
new technologies will do to our
lives, particularly when they
cause rapid change. But an age
of punch-throughs could
deliver things we have wanted
for centuries. No doubt some
people will worry that the
future is coming too quickly.
But some of it has been a very
long time coming. ●
Fusion power’s Kitty Hawk moment
Once you ﬁgure out how
to build a machine that
has a star inside it, you
can make more of them

The technology is about to start working, says Bob Mumgaard,

T
HE GLAMOROUS side of the
entertainment industry calls it the
three Cs: consent, credit and compen-
sation. Hard-nosed media executives call it
the three Ls: litigation, legislation and
licensing. Both are talking about the same
thing: how to stop artiﬁcial intelligence
from pirating their content. In 2027 the
battle will move from the courtroom to the
bargaining table. It will start to change the
nature of the media industry.

The creative industries have a long
history of existential angst over tech-
nology. In the mid-1950s Hollywood feared
that the rise of free television would kill off
cinemas. In the late 1990s the “Napster
moment”—peer-to-peer music sharing via
the internet—threatened to upend the
record industry. Eventually, ﬁlm studios
and record labels realised that rather than
ﬁghting it out in court, they could make
more money by licensing their content to
distributors on new platforms.

The same will happen with AI. Media
and entertainment ﬁrms still have over 135
active lawsuits against AI labs in America
for training their models on allegedly
pirated material. But increasingly these
lawsuits are becoming leverage for
lucrative licensing deals. “2027 may be the
year when AI stops being something the
creative industries argue about, and starts
becoming something they account for,”
says Pete Woodbridge, author of “Dream
Machine”, a book about AI and creativity.

material. Judge Sidney Stein is expected to
issue summary judgment, or schedule a
trial, by early 2027.
But though litigation against AI ﬁrms
may lead to big payouts, licensing may
become a more sure-ﬁre route to proﬁt. It
starts, as in the Napster era, with music.
Warner Music Group (WMG) says AI
licensing deals will have a material impact
on streaming revenue growth in ﬁscal 2027.
That is because a year after it dropped a
copyright lawsuit against Suno, a maker of

AI music, Suno launched a trio of models,
called V6, trained on WMG’s licensed
music as well as that from other labels.
Suno is still being sued by Universal Music
Group (UMG) and Sony Music, which are
not part of the deal. Meanwhile Spotify, a
streaming service, has signed licensing
deals with UMG and other record labels to
use their music to train its AI models and to
offer new products to customers, such as
letting fans make their own remixes.

The ﬁlm industry is gingerly
considering licensing, too. Google, a tech
giant, is thought to be quietly courting
Walt Disney, Universal, Skydance and
other studios with a view to licensing their
characters and ﬁlm libraries for use in its AI
video models. Television actors, musicians,
sports stars, creators and inﬂuencers are
also working with technology and media
platforms such as YouTube on ways to
monetise AI-generated likenesses.
Some might fear that more AI content,

licensed or not, will only increase the
deluge of slop. Deezer, a music-streaming
service, says that at peak times as many as
half of the uploads on its platform are
AI-made. AI-generated microdramas are
also on the rise, spreading from China to
platforms like TikTok. The number of titles
on Steam, a game-distribution platform,
disclosing the inclusion of AI-generated
content has risen sharply.
But entertainment executives hope
something new will emerge from the

industry’s growing collaborations with AI
labs. In music, for instance, they hope fans
will pay to use AI tools to remix their
favourite songs, with revenues ﬂowing
back to artists and songwriters. Get ready
for the next act of the creator economy. ●

to licensing

I
N THE ARTIﬁCIAL-INTELLIGENCE gold
rush, Nvidia, an American chip designer,

has been the chief seller of shovels. But
TSMC is the company actually making
them. The Taiwanese ﬁrm manufactures
all of Nvidia’s AI chips. Its central role in
the AI supply chain has transformed its
fortunes. Since 2023 TSMC’s market value
has risen sixfold, to over $2trn, making it
the world’s seventh-most valuable ﬁrm.
TSMC accounts for almost three-
quarters of the market for foundry
chipmaking, in which ﬁrms manufacture
semiconductors designed by others. For

the most advanced chips, which power AI
models and smartphones, its share exceeds
nine-tenths. In 2027, though, its near-
monopoly in advanced chipmaking will
face competition.
The ﬁrst challenger is Intel, America’s
semiconductor champion. The company
ceded its lead in chipmaking to TSMC
around a decade ago and is racing to catch
up again. Lip-Bu Tan, Intel’s boss, calls
Intel’s foundry business a “national
treasure” and wants it to lead a revival of

advanced chipmaking on American soil. A
crucial test will come in late 2027, when
Intel starts making chips using its 14A
technology, among the industry’s most
advanced. The Trump administration is
pressing American technology ﬁrms to use
Intel’s factories; Apple, a gadget-maker, is
rumoured to be among the prospective
customers for 14A. Intel is also supplying
its technology to Terafab, Elon Musk’s
project to build vast chip factories for his
companies, SpaceX and Tesla.

Second is Samsung. The South Korean
electronics giant, which, like Intel, designs
and makes chips, has long run a distant
second to TSMC in foundry work. It has

silicon shield

85The World Ahead 2027 Business
▸ also struggled with manufacturing
problems of its own. Nonetheless, it is
pressing ahead: in 2027 its factory in
Taylor, Texas, will begin mass-producing
advanced two-nanometre (2nm) chips.
A more unlikely challenge comes from
Rapidus, a Japanese startup backed by the
government and by domestic industry
heavyweights. It plans to start mass-
producing cutting-edge 2nm chips in 2027,
and is betting that Japan’s history of

electronics manufacturing can help it jump
straight to the technological frontier.
Rapidus plans to undercut TSMC on price
and to court smaller ﬁrms that struggle to
secure capacity from the Taiwanese giant.
For now, the upstart’s ambitions are
modest. Rapidus plans to start producing
6,000 wafers—the silicon discs on which
chips are etched—per month in 2027, rising
to 25,000 by 2028. That is not many
compared with TSMC, which will be
producing around 90,000 wafers a month

on its 2nm process and over 180,000 on its
3nm process by the end of 2026. Even so,
Rapidus is the ﬁrst new entrant in frontier
chipmaking for decades.
Catching up with TSMC will be hard.
Advanced chipmaking is a lengthy and
expensive process: a leading-edge fab
costs around $20bn to build and equip, and
takes between two and three years to
become fully operational. TSMC is
spending heavily to expand its own
capacity. In 2026 it will invest around

$60bn, one and a half times as much as in
2025, and analysts expect it to spend
around $75bn in 2027. As part of that push
it has also pledged more than $260bn to
expand its operations in America. Some of
its plants in Arizona are already churning
out chips for Nvidia and Apple.
Tech bosses privately concede that
other foundries are not as easy to work
with as TSMC. The company has the tools,
support and scale to match the needs of
some of the biggest tech ﬁrms, and that

will be difficult for newcomers to replicate.
Yet booming demand for AI is straining
supply chains and forcing ﬁrms to seek
alternatives. Jensen Huang, Nvidia’s boss,
has said that manufacturing capacity
constrains his company’s growth. Even
TSMC’s rising capital spending has not
satisﬁed customers who want it to invest
more. Rivals have a chance to ﬁll the gap.
The consequences go beyond business.
TSMC’s dominance in chipmaking, with
much of its capacity in Taiwan, has made

the island’s semiconductor sector vital to
the global technology industry. Taiwan’s
cutting-edge fabs, primarily TSMC’s, are
sometimes said to form a “silicon shield”
against Chinese aggression. But in 2027, as
some of that capacity shifts overseas—
both with TSMC’s own expansion into
America and because of new suppliers
emerging elsewhere—the island’s
once-impregnable shield will start to
weaken. Taiwan may have to get used to
being less indispensable. TSMC may also

have to get used to competition. ●

C
HINA BOASTS around 140 carmakers.
Many are tiny and will never be heard
of abroad, or even in much of China. But
the country’s biggest brands are becoming
ever more frequent sights on the world’s
roads. Crossing the street in Britain often
means dodging a Jaecoo 7, a popular

hybrid SUV made by Chery; in Brazil it
could be a BYD electric vehicle (EV). These
Chinese cars are mostly exports, which
could exceed 5.8m vehicles this year. But
Chinese carmakers that want to become
global giants are now also following the
route set by American and European (and
then Japanese and South Korean)
carmakers, by setting up factories abroad.
This shift has already begun, but 2027 is
the year when the handbrake will come off.
The Chinese manufacturers’ existing

plants will get up to full production. New
ones will start churning out vehicles in
quantity. And announcements of overseas
expansion will accelerate. In today’s car
industry, going global means going local.
Localisation has many beneﬁts. Top of
the list is avoiding tariffs, such as those
imposed on Chinese EVs by the European
Union in 2024. Meeting local-content
requirements by using local suppliers, and
sidestepping the high cost of transporting

ﬁnished cars, are also good reasons to
localise. Proximity makes it easier and
quicker to adapt vehicles to cater for
changing tastes or regulations in different
regions. Setting up factories creates jobs
and curries favour with governments.
BYD, the world’s biggest EV-maker, has
led the way. Operations at its factory in
Thailand, opened in 2024, will ramp up
towards full annual production of around
150,000 vehicles. Output will also rise at its
factory in Brazil, with capacity of 300,000,

and at a new plant in Hungary, opening in
late 2026. SAIC will start construction of a
factory for its MG brand in Spain. And
Great Wall Motors (GWM) is likely to start
work on a second factory in Brazil.
Chinese ﬁrms are rushing to set up
factories before the EU tightens
restrictions on Chinese imports, or just to
beat each other to new foreign markets. It
is quicker to take over factories abandoned
by foreign competitors or even to strike
deals to use parts of their existing sites.

BYD’s Brazilian plant once belonged to
Ford, and GWM acquired its ﬁrst one from
Mercedes-Benz. In Britain, Chery has
signed a deal with Nissan to make cars at
its underused plant in Sunderland,
probably starting in 2027. It previously
acquired a Nissan plant in Spain, where
Geely recently signed a production deal
with Ford. BYD and Xpeng are both looking
for sites to acquire from foreign rivals.
Guangzhou Automobile Group is
opening a factory in Mexico and BYD and

Geely hope to make cars in the country
soon. Both, along with Chery, are also
exploring deals to manufacture in Canada.
But America has kept Chinese cars at bay
with 100% tariffs (introduced by the Biden
administration). Donald Trump has
maintained this de facto ban on imports,
but China will be ready if he softens his
stance. To the horror of America’s car
ﬁrms, which are ill prepared to compete
with cheap, tech-laden Chinese models,
Mr Trump has said he would allow the

Chinese to build cars in America to boost
employment. So far no Chinese ﬁrm has
taken him up on the offer. But perhaps
2027 is the year one will. ●

A
T SOME POINT in October 2027 an
Airbus A350 will take off from Sydney
with 238 passengers on board and spend as
many as 22 hours in the air, depending on
the route and winds. When it lands in
London almost a full day later, it will have
set a new record for the world’s longest
scheduled commercial ﬂight, currently
held by a Singapore Airlines service from
Singapore to New York (which takes a
measly 19 hours).
Qantas, Australia’s ﬂag-carrier and the

operator of the marathon ﬂight, has
ordered a ﬂeet of 12 specially customised,
“ultra-long-range” versions of the
A350-1000. It did not do that just to gain
entry into the record books. The airline
reckons there is enough demand to sustain
daily nonstop ﬂights between Sydney and
London. It will follow up with a daily
nonstop Sydney-New York service. And
unlike the Singapore Airlines ﬂight, which
uses a cabin conﬁgured with only premium
seating, Qantas offers the full range—from

economy seats at the back all the way to a
ﬁrst-class experience that it says “blurs the
lines between air travel and boutique
accommodation”. (The lucky folk in the
cheap seats will have access to a “well-
being zone” with a “hydration station”—
aka, a place to stretch and drink water.)
A plane that can ﬂy from Sydney to
London is, in effect, capable of ﬂying
between any two points on Earth. Yet the
Qantas service is unlikely to spur a spate of
new routes from Tokyo to Tulum, or

Panama to Manama. Rather than a glorious
new era for ultra-long-haul ﬂights, the
Sydney-London and Sydney-New York
services will mark their culmination.
One reason is that the longer an aircraft
ﬂies nonstop, the more fuel it needs to
carry, including fuel to carry the weight of
all the extra fuel. A 20-hour ﬂight thus
needs more of the stuff than two ten-hour
trips. That limits ultra-long routes to those
with strong demand and, more important,
lots of well-to-do travellers willing to shell

out for premium cabins.
There are only a handful of such routes.
City pairs with lots of migrants, family-
and-friends visitors, and tourists in both
directions, such as London-Sydney, are
one type. A second is made up of busy
commercial corridors such as between San
Francisco and Bangalore. And a third is
composed of connections between hubs,
such as Doha-Los Angeles or Hong
Kong-New York. Think of these not as
ﬂights between two cities but between two

regions. Most passengers will be heading
to or from another city—or both.
Yet just because the world is running
out of very distant places to put within
reach of a single hop does not mean it is
running out of new connections altogether.
In 2024 the ﬁrst Airbus A321XLR—which
stands for “Xtra Long Range”—entered
service with Iberia, Spain’s ﬂag-carrier,
which uses it to connect Boston and
Madrid. The single-aisle plane, a variant of
the type usually used for short hops, can

spend up to 11 hours in the air and is perfect
for what the aviation industry calls a “long
and thin” route: “long” referring to
distance and “thin” to demand. The appeal
of the XLR lies in its ability to ﬂy long
distances without the costs of a big plane
like a Boeing 777 or Airbus A350, thereby
opening up routes which would otherwise
have been unproﬁtable.
After years of delays and supply-chain
disruptions across the aviation industry,
deliveries of A321XLRs picked up in 2026

and will accelerate in 2027. American
Airlines will start ﬂights between
Philadelphia and Porto, Portugal’s second
city. IndiGo, India’s biggest airline, already
uses the plane to ﬂy nonstop from Mumbai
to Amsterdam. Expect many more “long
and thin” routes in 2027. Just as airlines like
Ryanair made it common to ﬂy point-
to-point within Europe, aircraft like the
A321XLR make it possible to do so across
oceans and continents. The era of ultra-
long-haul may have peaked. But the age of

“long and thin” is only just starting. ●
Faster, longer,
thinner

T
WENTY-ﬁVE YEARS ago Leonard
Lauder, then chairman of Estée
Lauder, a beauty giant, noticed what he
called the “lipstick effect”. After the
attacks of September 11th 2001, the spectre
of war hung over America and the global
economy tanked. Lauder noticed jittery
female consumers cut down on all sorts of
spending, from food to travel, but splashed

out on one small, affordable pick-me-up:
lipstick. Analysts speculated that sales of
lipstick—the “lipstick index”—might
function as a recession indicator.
In 2027 the world will, once again, look
gloomy. Wars in Ukraine and the Middle
East seem likely to grind on. The
disruption to energy markets has inﬂation
ticking higher. Meanwhile, tech nerds are
talking about artiﬁcial intelligence taking
everyday jobs. People are still looking for a
pick-me-up in difficult times. But what

they are reaching for is changing.
Over the years experts have proposed
all sorts of quirky economic indicators. In
the 1920s the “hemline index” suggested
that women went for shorter skirts when
times were good. Alan Greenspan, a
central banker, popularised the idea that
Why spas are
the new lipstick
Pick-me-ups for hard times
have changed
Avantika Chilkoti Business writer,
The Economist

### Page 87

87The World Ahead 2027 Business
▸ sales of men’s underwear drop particularly
sharply in downturns; replacing garments
that remain out of sight seems less urgent.
The lipstick index, despite being based on
anecdotal evidence and piecemeal data,
has been followed most closely of all. Over
the years economists tried to explain why
the theory might hold. Were women
dolling up for job interviews in downturns?
Or trying to woo a mate? They settled on
the idea that spending on lipstick was just

part of “little-treat culture”.
But now, amid growing concerns about
the cost of living in many rich countries,
any correlation between economic gloom
and an uptick in lipstick sales seems to
have vanished. According to estimates
from McKinsey, a consultancy, global sales
of premium lipstick grew by just 3%
year-on-year in 2025, down from double-
digit growth a few years ago. And industry
analysts do not see that ﬁgure jumping any
time soon.
The reason, says Sandra Cariglio of ReD

Associates, an advisory ﬁrm, is that cos-
metics are no longer seen as an indulgence,
but as a necessity. Thanks to high-
deﬁnition smartphone cameras and the
constant gaze of social media, spending on
cosmetics has surged since Lauder’s time.
Consumers have stocked their cabinets
with serums, glosses, fragrances and more.
The global beauty industry is expected to
be worth $590bn by 2030.
What does feel like a treat nowadays is
a trip to the spa. Roughly half of those

surveyed by McKinsey said they had
increased their spending on spa services
(such as massages or facials) in the past
year, compared with 28% for cosmetics
such as lipstick. “People used to go buy a
Chanel lipstick because they couldn’t
afford a Chanel bag,” says Jasmina Vico,
who runs a skincare clinic in London that
is doing a roaring trade. “Now they want to
spend money on experiences, rather than
having a handbag or a beauty product.”
Spa treatments are a cheaper

experience than a weekend break or a
fancy restaurant meal, and one that
consumers may strive to maintain, even in
hard times, because they consider it to be a
form of health care, not just an indulgence.
The shift reﬂects a new obsession with
“wellness”: it has become fashionable,
since the covid-19 pandemic, to show that
you care about your health.
Spending on spa treatments is not just
resilient, but might be expected to go up as
people cut back in other areas, such as

holidays, in hard times. That could make it
a useful economic indicator. And unlike
lipstick sales, which track the sentiment of
adult women, spending on facials and
massages captures a wider demographic,
including younger women and some men.
The lipstick index is out; in 2027, keep an
eye on the spa index instead. ●
W
EIGHT-LOSS DRUGS are reducing
waistlines around the world. Around
15% of Americans have tried GLP-1 drugs
such as Wegovy, Ozempic, Zepbound and

Mounjaro, up from 6% in 2024. The rest of
the world is catching up: global sales of
GLP-1s will double by 2035 to $190bn,
reckons Morgan Stanley, a bank. Their rise
has been good news for drug ﬁrms, and
bad for purveyors of junk food. But their
effects extend far beyond diets.
In 2027 retail businesses of all kinds will
rush to cater to Ozempic users, as they
respond to GLP-1s’ reshaping not just of
consumers, but of consumer behaviour.
People on the jabs constitute a distinct

customer segment, with an emphasis on
self-improvement and optimisation. Even
as a minority, they will inﬂuence the
broader world of fashion, says Cassandra
Napoli of WGSN, a trend forecaster. GLP-1s
“don’t need universal adoption to have a
mainstream effect”, she notes.
As more GLP-1 users slim down, they
will restock their wardrobes in a bout of
“revenge shopping”. Some will simply shift
sizing. Lingerie brands such as Soma are
redesigning their bras to adjust for the way

weight loss changes the shape and density
of women’s breasts. H&M, a fast-fashion
giant, is cutting down on quadruple- and
triple-extra-large clothes.
But as some consumers shed pounds,
they are buying and returning multiple
sizes of their favorite frocks, eating into
retailers’ margins. To combat this,
adjustable closures and stretchy garments
will proliferate. In-person shopping will
make a comeback; it is easier to check a
garment’s ﬁt in person than on a screen.

As they lose facial fat, shoppers will
also head to retailers such as Sephora, Ulta
and Olive Young for supplements and
creams that promise to help. Kiehl’s, a
skincare brand, has made ﬁxing GLP-1
users’ haggard faces part of its marketing.
Meanwhile, more than 80% of American
plastic surgeons received requests related
to GLP-1s in 2025, according to the
American Society of Plastic Surgeons.
Fast-growing procedures include facial
fat-grafts (up 39% over 2024) and lifts of all

kinds (up 8-29%, depending on the area).
Venturing outside, Ozempic’s effects
will reorient leisure. Lighter passengers
may allow American airlines to save 1.5% on
fuel costs. Cruise ships will swap out
all-you-can-eat buffets for lighter fare.
Fitness will become a bigger part of going
on holiday, leading to a boom in “wellness”
retreats at posh resorts. Such spas used to
promise weight loss, but will now
emphasise strength training and longevity.

But retailers should not be too worried.
GLP-1 users reallocate about a third of the
cash they save on groceries to other goods,
according to BCG, a consultancy.
Newfound conﬁdence seems to be
boosting their spending across the board,
on everything from make-up to handbags.
Marketing to GLP-1 users requires a greater
emphasis on virtue rather than vice. But
either can be the basis for proﬁt. ●

T
HE HUMANOID-ROBOT
industry has a peculiar
habit. It likes to build the future
one body part at a time. Some
companies focus on motion
control, while others devote
their attention to dexterous
hands or general-purpose
brains. Almost everyone talks
about data, often assuming
that more is a panacea. Yet if
data volume alone were the
answer, then carmakers, sitting
on oceans of real-world driving
data, would have already

delivered fully autonomous
vehicles. They have not.
Most humanoid robots
today are not much more than
tele-operated puppets or short-
lived demonstration models.
They exist to entertain con-
sumers or impress investors. To
advance from demonstration to
dependable deployment is a
huge leap. For these machines
to go from conference stages to
factories, offices and even into
people’s homes, we need the
correct architecture. It rests on
three pillars: hardware,

software and data.
Robots must be designed
with a software mindset, but
hardware must come ﬁrst. In
the car industry, hardware
precedes software, and the
boundary between the two is
comparatively clear. Humanoid
robots are different. Their
hardware and intelligence must
be conceived together, right
from the start. This is easy to
describe, but extraordinarily
difficult to engineer. The
industry is moving so fast that
both hardware and software are

difficult to source at scale.
Instead, success will require
both to be developed in-house,
with a holistic approach to
research and innovation. A
typical electric car has one or
two drive motors; Xpeng’s
humanoid robot has 76, one for
each joint actuator. Achieving
safety and reliability demands
a level of rigour far beyond that
needed in carmaking.
When it comes to the
software, many ﬁrms hope to
be the “Android” of robotics,
creating a single AI model to

orchestrate everything from
vision to movement. Today’s
models shine in intelligence
and generalisation. But like
self-driving cars in 2018, they
look good in demonstrations,
yet are far from balancing cost,
safety, precision and reliability.
Rather than one all-purpose
brain, humanoid robots will
need several speeds of thought:
ultra-fast models for motor
control and split-second
reﬂexes; fast models for
motion and real-time
interaction; slow models for

reasoning and memory;
separate models dedicated to
safety. This is the approach we
are taking at Xpeng. Humans
work in much the same way.
We do not write an essay
before pulling our hand away
from a hot surface. Reﬂection
has its place, but so do reﬂexes.
As for data, training a
typical large model requires
roughly 200 terabytes of data.
Training a “vision-language-
action” model to control an
autonomous car requires 1,000
times more, or around 200

petabytes. And training a
humanoid robot will require
even more than that. Yet scale
alone tells us very little.
Data needs variety, not
merely volume. The objective
should be to build high-quality
data loops and infrastructure
that create a self-reinforcing
ﬂywheel of data and capability.
At Xpeng, we think the systems
we built to train cars can be
applied directly to robots. The
body may be different, but the
engineering logic travels well.
These three pillars are just

the foundation. For a general-
purpose humanoid robot, there
will also have to be progress in
four other areas. Robots must
learn, just as children do, how
to move around safely. They
must learn to handle tools and
navigate a range of environ-
ments. They must be able to
learn and apply specialist
knowledge and skills. And they
will need social and emotional
intelligence to act correctly
depending on the situation.
To achieve all this,
humanoids should be deployed

in stages. Commercial uses,
such as providing guidance to
shoppers or tourists, offer a
logical starting-point. Tasks
tend to be repetitive, clearly
deﬁned and performed in
controlled surroundings.
Industrial settings introduce
greater complexity. The home
is the most challenging
environment of all: intimate,
unstructured and gloriously
unpredictable. The paradox of
robotics is that the setting with
the greatest potential human
beneﬁt is the least forgiving

engineering environment.
The challenges are
formidable. But I believe that
2027 will mark the beginning of
mass production for advanced
general-purpose humanoid
robots. At Xpeng we are
currently pursuing the
capabilities needed to meet
these goals. For the robotics
industry, our ambition must
not be simply to build capable
machines. It must be to ensure
they enrich human life and
extend our physical
capabilities—giving people
more freedom to focus on what

only humans can do. ●
Get ready for humanoid robots
The setting with the
greatest potential beneﬁt
is the least forgiving
engineering environment

Finance & economics
A
S THE WORLD heads into 2027 market-watchers
are more worried than usual. In any given year the
chances of a recession are around 15%. But Kalshi, a
prediction market, puts the chance in the coming year
of a recession in America, which could take the global
economy tumbling down with it, at nearly 25%. Is that
plausible? And what form might the next crash take?
Start by considering what probably won’t cause the

next crisis. In recent years shock upon shock has
battered the global economy. But growth has not
nosedived. That suggests the next crash won’t be from
an energy shock: the closure of the Strait of Hormuz in
2026 was one of the biggest such shocks since the
1970s, yet it barely affected global growth. Economies,
it seems, are now less oil-dependent and more ﬂexible.
A trade war probably won’t do the trick, either.
Donald Trump’s enthusiasm for tariffs has hardly

dimmed since his blitz of trade barriers on “Liberation
Day” in April 2025. At the time, forecasters put the
odds of an imminent recession in America as high as
two-thirds. But in the event, American and global GDP
stayed pretty much exactly on their earlier trend.
Whatever new levies Mr Trump imposes as his
presidency enters its lame-duck phase probably will
not shake up the global economy much more.
Even high interest rates, that perennial cause of

recessions, do not look especially threatening. Rudi
Dornbusch, an economist, once quipped that
America’s economic expansions don’t die of old age,
but are murdered by the Federal Reserve—with raised
interest rates. On that basis, the Fed’s huge rate
increases in 2022 and 2023, and similar moves around
the world, ought to have caused a crash. But they did
not, perhaps because the most interest-rate-
sensitive sectors, such as manufacturing, are no longer

so important to rich-world economies. So the more
modest rate increases on the cards for 2027 are
unlikely to cause serious problems for growth.
What does that leave? Today’s biggest economic
risks are probably government debt and artiﬁcial
intelligence. A debt crisis would be more familiar.
Emerging markets have dealt with them for years.
But rich-world governments, especially America, are
The next crash
What could bring down the
global economy in 2027?

Archie Hall Acting economics editor, The Economist

90 Finance & economics The World Ahead 2027
▸ now borrowing astonishing amounts—more, relative
to the size of their economies, than at any point
outside wartime or around recessions. The
repercussions of a debt crisis embroiling American
Treasuries, which remain the world’s safe asset, would
be vast. Rich countries’ debt levels are already giving
bond markets indigestion.
Markets mostly still expect governments to sort
themselves out and pay back their debts (and not by

inﬂating them away or imposing ﬁnancial repression).
But real problems would start if that assumption were
seriously challenged—perhaps if a particularly
irresponsible government were elected, or if debts
piled up so much that repayment would simply be too
painful. One catalyst in 2027 might be France’s
election, where the populist right is in the lead.
What about AI? Although the technology is new,
economic history offers lessons. One scenario is 1987,

when then-novel computerised trading techniques
spurred a sharp, unexpected and global stock-market
crash. As AI agents start trading at scale, in chaotic and
potentially uninterpretable ways, a reprise is possible.
Another worrying precedent is 2000. From railways
to websites, revolutionary technologies often push
markets into a tizzy. When the tech bubble burst at the
turn of the millennium, plunging stocks dragged the
wider economy down, too. In contrast to that period,

AI ﬁrms have surging earnings, not just ever-more-
optimistic valuations. But those earnings could return
to earth if competition reduces proﬁt margins, or if the
AI roll-out proves bumpier than expected.
Then there is 2008. At ﬁrst, technology giants
funded AI from their vast earnings and cash piles. Now
they have turned to debt. Especially leveraged are the
“neoclouds”, upstarts borrowing huge sums to fund
data centres. The ﬁnancing links between AI ﬁrms are

getting ever more circular and complex. That pattern
of ﬁnancial sophistication, built atop rickety debt, has
a clear precedent in the mortgage-market troubles that
helped kick off the global ﬁnancial crisis nearly 20
years ago. If anticipated AI revenues that the whole
ediﬁce is relying on fail to materialise, or show up later
than expected, some of those riskier borrowers may
struggle to pay their debts. That, in turn, could hit the
balance-sheets of other AI ﬁrms that are ﬁnancing

them. Chaos could easily snowball.
Still, the future has a way of surprising. The
chances are that the next crash—whether it comes in
2027 or later, and whatever family resemblances it
shares—will be its own creature entirely. ●
