I
N 2026 THE closure of the
Strait of Hormuz triggered
the biggest supply shock in
petroleum history. For much
of the year the global econ-
omy absorbed it remarkably
well: China slashed its oil
imports, America exported
more fuel and Europe delayed
gas purchases, keeping price
benchmarks well below the
peaks that analysts had feared
when the Iran war began. By
the ﬁnal quarter, though, the
buffers were wearing thin. In
2027 tightness will spread

across a range of commodity
markets—whether or not
Hormuz reopens for good.
Oil will show the strain
ﬁrst—though, as in 2026, the
crude price is the wrong gauge
of it. Tankers will keep slipping
through the strait, so the world
will not face a crippling deﬁcit
of raw petroleum. The scarce
commodity could remain
reﬁning capacity, if Middle
Eastern plants stay largely
offline and Ukraine keeps
pummelling Russian reﬁneries.
The longer the war drags on,

the longer diesel prices will
stay in the stratosphere, and
those for jet fuel, too. Peace in
the Gulf would push prices
down. But delays in reﬁning
and shipping would keep
markets tight for months.
Sulphur, vital to industries
from mining to chipmaking,
could stay a luxury item for
similar reasons. In late 2026 it
traded at $750-800 a tonne,
against $100-200 in normal
times. The market was already
tight before the Iran war: Rus-
sia, keen to preserve its limited

output for domestic use (sul-
phur is largely a by-product of
oil and gas reﬁning), had
banned exports. The crisis then
cut nearly half of the world’s
exported supply; restrictions in
China, India, Kazakhstan and
Turkey shrank non-Gulf ex-
ports by another 40%. Unless
Gulf tensions ease, the trade
will stay subdued. Indonesian
nickel producers, which oper-
ate on razor-thin margins, will
suffer; copper producers, with
greater pricing power amid
strong demand, can absorb the

cost more easily.
Fertiliser producers, many
of which also rely on sulphur,
have been hit hard. Factories in
Morocco, India, America and
Brazil initially cut output
before shifting towards sul-
phur-sparing variants that
deliver fewer nutrients. High
prices for natural gas—anoth-
er key input—are squeezing
the industry further.
Scarce, expensive fertilis-
er, combined with dear diesel
and high freight costs, could
hamper agricultural exports
this coming year. When the

Iran war began, many farmers
still had stocks of fertiliser
and fuel, and planting was
under way or ﬁnished; they
lack those buffers this time. A
food-price index compiled by
the UN’S Food and Agricul-
ture Organisation in October
was at its highest since No-
vember 2022. Low yields, due
to low fertiliser use, could
keep prices high in 2027.
In all three cases, only a
swift end to the Iran war can
halt the steady shrinkage of
the buffers protecting supply

from another shock.
Aftershocks from Iran will
disrupt commodity markets
Matthieu Favas Energy and
commodities editor,
The Economist

91The World Ahead 2027 Finance & economics
A
NEW ERA of expensive money is here. In
September the ten-year American Treasury yield
climbed above 5%, a threshold last crossed for any
length of time two decades ago. British, French,
German and Japanese yields also rose as investors,
spooked by stubborn inﬂation and mounting public
debt, sold bonds. Worse, the increase in borrowing
costs comes as debt burdens are already at record
highs. For much of the 2010s politicians could ignore

warnings of a debt reckoning, thanks to low interest
rates. As 2027 dawns, the outlook is much scarier.
Yet even as borrowing costs surge, politicians have
shown little appetite for belt-tightening. America is
expected to run a deﬁcit of around 6% of GDP in 2026,
and France more than 5%. To ﬁnance those deﬁcits and
reﬁnance old debt issued when rates were far lower,
governments are selling vast amounts of bonds. Across
the OECD, a club mostly of rich countries, govern-

ments will issue debt worth around a ﬁfth of GDP in
2026, close to a record. The costs of the debt binge are
getting harder to sustain. Is a reckoning coming?
Whether one arrives in 2027 will depend partly on
what governments do next. The obvious course would
be to borrow less. Yet most politicians show little sign
of doing so. In America, tax cuts, rising spending on
pensions and health care, and a swelling interest bill
mean borrowing will continue at a prodigious pace,

even with the economy near full employment.
In Japan, Takaichi Sanae, the prime minister, is
pushing state-backed investment in semiconductors
and artiﬁcial intelligence, while preparing to cut the
consumption tax on food. In France, the deﬁcit will be
near 6% of GDP in 2027, and an election in the spring
will make retrenchment harder. Hundreds of
thousands have taken to the streets to protest against
strained public services and rising living costs. Marine

Le Pen, the front-runner, has promised to cut fuel taxes
and scrap a rise in the retirement age for some workers.
Britain is the partial exception. Memories of Liz
Truss’s bond-market debacle in 2022 still loom large,
and borrowing is on a better trajectory. But Andy
Burnham, the prime minister, faces rising demands to
ﬁnd money for social care, housing and defence.
Interest payments absorb 3.3% of GDP across the
OECD, and will rise further as cheap debt is reﬁnanced

at higher rates. If investors see little prospect of ﬁscal
restraint, they will demand a larger premium to hold
long-dated debt, pushing borrowing costs higher still.
To blunt some of the rise in costs, governments will
increasingly rely on short-term debt, on which yields
are usually lower. Across the OECD, the share of new
debt with a maturity of more than ten years has fallen
to its lowest since 2009; Treasury bills (with a maturity
of one year or less) now make up almost a quarter of

America’s marketable debt, the highest share in the
rich world. But shorter maturities mean higher rates
feed through to budgets faster. And with inﬂation
stubborn, short rates may not provide much relief.
That will leave governments with uncomfortable
choices. Expect more Truss-like moments in the year
ahead, as rising borrowing costs force politicians to
delay tax cuts and water down spending promises.
One dangerous temptation is to lean on central banks.

Donald Trump has repeatedly pressed Kevin Warsh,
the Federal Reserve chairman, to cut interest rates, to
no avail (Mr Warsh has raised them). But if central
banks keep rates too low, the bond market will revolt
more ﬁercely, driving long-term yields even higher.
The reckoning can be delayed. It cannot be avoided. ●

A
LMOST EVERY week an
artiﬁcial-intelligence lab releases a
new model with ever more astonishing
capabilities. Connoisseurs claim that each
advance improves their productivity, and
look down their noses at those who have
not kept up. AI researchers worry that
progress is so rapid that, before long, the
technology will be able to take over the
world, or destroy it. Yet judging by
measurements of the real economy, AI is

having surprisingly little impact—a state of
affairs that will not change in 2027.
Many people expected AI to have
disrupted the labour market by now, with
bots putting millions of office-based
employees out of work. Yet in America, the
global centre of AI development, the share
of workers employed in white-collar
occupations is around an all-time high (see
chart). The number of software developers
is rising, even though writing code is what
AI models do best.

It is true that recent college graduates
are struggling, with an unusually large gap
between their unemployment rate and the
overall level. But that is likely to be a result
of the shift to hybrid and remote working
during the covid-19 pandemic. Across the
OECD club of mostly rich countries, the
share of working-age people in
employment is at an all-time high.
With AI displacing so few people, the
hoped-for gains to productivity have not
materialised. America shows few signs of

rising total factor productivity (growth
that cannot be explained by extra labour or
capital). The Economist examined some
small, rich, techy countries such as Estonia,
Singapore and Switzerland but found that
productivity growth there is no faster.
AI’s economic effects are muted in part
because adoption of the technology
remains surprisingly low. According to
America’s Census Bureau, only about a
quarter of companies are using AI in a
meaningful way. A survey by Jonathan

Hartley of the University of Texas at
Austin, and his colleagues, suggests that
the share of American workers using AI is
no longer growing.
Financial markets are giving mixed
signals about the future economic gains
from AI. In recent months the interest rates
on government debt have jumped. That
can be read as a sign that people expect
faster economic growth: in a world of rapid
income growth, the desire to invest would
be higher, and the desire to save lower. Yet

lots of other factors, including resurgent
worries about inﬂation, may better explain
rising interest rates. The best evidence
about AI points the other way.
Isaiah Andrews of the Massachusetts
Institute of Technology and Maryam
Farboodi of Cornell University analyse
bond-market moves around big AI model-
releases. They ﬁnd that long-term yields
fall, a surprise if better AI is expected to
juice future growth. A stock-market index
made by Goldman Sachs, a bank, follows

American ﬁrms with “the largest estimated
potential change to baseline earnings from
AI adoption via increased productivity”. In
recent months this basket has under-
performed. The markets could be wrong.
But if not, AI’s economic transformation
could be slower to arrive than expected. ●
Show me
the money
AI models are brilliant. But their
economic effects will remain hard to spot
Callum Williams Senior economics writer,
The Economist

M
ARKETS WILL be watching two big
personnel changes in Europe in 2027.
In February the new head of the European
Central Bank will take over from Christine
Lagarde, who resigned in October 2026, a
year before her term was due to end. And in
April, French voters will be asked to pick a

successor to Emmanuel Macron, their
president. The two appointments are
connected in more ways than one.
The job of running the ECB became
more interesting during Ms Lagarde’s
tenure, which began in 2019. Since then the
ECB has had to deal with a pandemic, an
inﬂation shock, tariffs, geopolitical rifts
over Ukraine and more. The bank also had
to revisit its role as lender of last resort to
governments when interest rates on
government bonds surged.

Today France ﬁnds itself in a state of
political paralysis just as economic growth
is stalling and public debt, already high,
becomes a lot more expensive to service.
The front-runner in the polls, Marine Le
Pen of the populist-right and eurosceptic
National Rally, has in principle committed
to bringing down the deﬁcit. But her
policies do not add up: she wants to cut
fuel taxes and scrap a rise in the retirement
age. So to reduce borrowing costs, she has

instead called on the ECB to cut rates.
Ms Lagarde and Ms Le Pen thus
seemed on course for an almighty clash in
2027. To complicate matters further, the
new French president would have played
an important role in choosing Ms
Lagarde’s successor as the end of her term
neared. By stepping down early, she is
letting Mr Macron and other euro-zone
leaders pick their preferred successor
ahead of the French presidential elections,
much to the opposition’s chagrin.

That successor, to be announced
sometime before the end of 2026, will in
practice be chosen by Germany behind the
scenes. It would normally be Germany’s
turn to lead the bank. The problem is that
Ursula von der Leyen, the president of the
European Commission, is German and her
term runs until 2029. It would be too much
to insist on a German ECB president
The changing
of Lagarde
The ECB prepares for a stand-off
with France

93The World Ahead 2027 Finance & economics
▸ alongside Mrs von der Leyen. So instead,
Germany will be expected to pick a
non-German candidate. Officials in Berlin
will insist that, given the situation in
France, the post cannot go to a soft-money
dove without much experience in ﬁghting
crises. The job is therefore likely to go to a
politically smart, experienced and
pragmatic candidate such as Klaas Knot,
the former long-time governor of the
Dutch central bank.

Whoever gets the job will have to be
quite tough on France—not despite the
country being a heavyweight in Europe,
but because of it. France is the second-
largest economy in the EU,aG7 member
and permanent member of the UN Security
Council. It cannot be bullied. At the same
time, the EU and the euro were in part
constructed on France’s political and
economic might. But now yields on French
debt are nearly 1.5 percentage points higher
than those on EU debt, as investors worry

about its ﬁscal incontinence.
But should that difference widen to two
percentage points or more, France would
look to the ECB to contain a panic. The new
ECB president would then have to explain
to policymakers in Paris that those yields
were of their own making, and that
substantial ﬁscal measures would be
needed ﬁrst, in order to restore trust.
The ECB can afford to let France sweat a
little. The euro-zone has put institutions in
place to make sure a crisis in France does

not spread to other countries. Banks are
better supervised and capitalised, and the
ECB has signalled to markets that it will
stand behind governments’ debt—
provided their ﬁscal policy is on the right
track. Meanwhile, debt in countries such as
Greece and Spain is coming down.
Italy also offers a salutory example for
France. It must elect a new parliament by
December 2027. But what might have
caused market jitters a few years ago
hardly seems to matter now. Giorgia

Meloni, Italy’s prime minister, is leading in
the polls and has so far managed to keep
debt in check and avoid a clash with the EU
or the ECB. One reason is that Italy’s
populists had a standoff with the ECB in
2018—and lost. In 2027, expect the ECB to
give France the same treatment. ●
T
HE AGE of foreign aid is over. When
Donald Trump and his lieutenant Elon
Musk shut down the United States Agency
for International Development in
2025—Mr Musk joked about “feeding

USAID into the wood chipper”—foreign-
assistance obligations fell by nearly 50%,
compared with 2024 (see chart).
Development folks hoped that America
might continue to fund many of USAID’s
existing activities under new federal
organisations. But those hopes were
dashed, and foreign-assistance obligations
are expected to be even lower in 2026. The
rest of the rich world has followed suit.
Germany, France and Britain all cut their
aid budgets in 2026, too.

Going into 2027, poor countries hoping
for support and investment from rich ones
are having to change their pitches. Donor
countries are no longer handing out cash
based on neediness. But Mr Trump has not
abandoned the practice of sending money
abroad altogether. Instead, he has
weaponised America’s assistance by tying
aid to the resources that lie beneath many
developing countries.
In 2025 and 2026, Mr Trump signed
mineral-related deals with at least 20

countries, from Ukraine to the Democratic
Republic of Congo. Critical minerals such
as lithium, cobalt and rare-earth elements,
the supply of which is currently dominated
by China, are particularly sought after.
The deals being struck offer
infrastructure investment from America’s
government and its companies in return
for a portion of the extracted minerals and
proﬁts. Early investments include a mining
operation in Brazil, which is experimenting

with a new way to reﬁne rare-earth
elements, and a railway in central Africa.
Yet many deals are, for now, little more
than a set of promises. But those promises,
along with new minerals projects within
America itself, are collectively worth
$40bn, according to officials.
Over the coming year, America will
reveal how much it is willing to invest, and
where. Among the projects ﬁghting for
approval are a tungsten mine in
Kazakhstan, a copper mine in Pakistan and

more infrastructure in central Africa.
The hunger for minerals is not limited
to America. Europe has done deals with 14
foreign countries, including Rwanda.
Officials also hope a free-trade deal made
in January 2026 with Mercosur, a bloc of
South American countries including
Argentina and Bolivia, will open up access
to lithium on the continent. China, which
has long invested heavily in mining
overseas, is set to increase its spending
even further. In 2025 its ﬁrms spent over

$30bn on mines overseas. In 2027,
according to an American official, they are
expected to dole out even more.
Some of these projects are backed by
friends and family of Mr Trump, as well as
the American government. The president’s
sons Donald junior and Eric are part of a
group of investors who have taken a 20%
stake in an entity related to the Kazakh
tungsten-mining venture. And Cantor
Fitzgerald, an investment ﬁrm controlled
by Howard Lutnick, the commerce

secretary, helped investors in the project
raise money for a related venture,
according to the New York Times. With
Democrats taking control of Congress in
2027, such deals may come under scrutiny.
For the chosen poor countries, the
beneﬁts can be immense, with health and
humanitarian aid ﬂowing in response to
mining deals in African countries. Between
2004 and 2012, the height of the last
commodities supercycle, foreign inﬂows
transformed the economies of some poor

countries. Investment in infrastructure can
boost growth. Revenues from mines and
oilﬁelds also ease ﬁscal constraints for
governments. For politicians in the
developing world, foreign aid may be in
retreat, but the days of courting the leaders
of the rich world are far from over. ●

of foreign aid

Science & technology
T
HE PATH to a longer human life may run through
man’s best friend. Loyal, a San Francisco-based
startup, is developing drugs to extend the lifespan of
older dogs. In 2027 the company expects to get
approval from American regulators to sell its lead
candidate, a daily pill designed to mimic some of the
age-extending effects of calorie restriction without
causing weight loss or suppressing appetite. The

coming year could be an important one for humans
hoping to live longer, too, as longevity treatments, long
the preserve of the rich and the kooky (sometimes one
and the same), start moving towards the mainstream.
There is no ﬁxed deﬁnition of a longevity drug.
Broadly, it is one that targets a biological cause of
ageing or helps prevent multiple age-related diseases
at once, extending “healthspan” (the years a person
spends in good health). The Food and Drug

Administration, America’s drug regulator, does not
classify ageing as a disease. Longevity drugs must
therefore be tested against speciﬁc age-related
conditions, not ageing in general.
In some cases these are drugs that already exist.
GLP-1 medicines, used to treat diabetes and obesity,
regulate blood sugar and insulin. But researchers have
found that they can also help patients with heart
disease, sleep apnoea, and kidney and liver problems.
Because many of these conditions become more

common with age and contribute to early death, some
researchers already regard GLP-1s as longevity drugs.
Recent studies make the case more directly. In May
2026 researchers writing in Nature Communications
reported a post-hoc analysis of a trial in which people
with HIV and lipohypertrophy, a condition in which
excess fat builds up around the abdomen, took
semaglutide (sold as Ozempic by Novo, a Danish
ﬁrm). After about seven months, those on the drug

showed signs of slower biological ageing, as measured
by “epigenetic clocks”, tests that estimate biological
rather than chronological age. The drug seems to work
by reducing persistent inﬂammation, which
accelerates ageing in people with HIV.
A second study, in mice, which was published in
September 2026, goes further. Researchers at the
Longevity drugs’
new lease of life
The idea of slowing human ageing
is attracting renewed attention
Shailesh Chitnis Business writer, The Economist

▸ University of California, Berkeley, injected older
female mice with semaglutide for the rest of their lives.
Their median lifespan was about 12% longer than that
of mice on a placebo. Both ﬁndings are preliminary,
and neither shows that GLP-1 drugs actually slow
ageing in humans. But they warrant follow-up studies.
Some are building the biology of ageing into drug
discovery from the start. Insilico Medicine, a Hong

Kong-listed startup that uses AI to develop drugs, is
testing a drug for idiopathic pulmonary ﬁbrosis, a
disease that scars the lungs. The ﬁrm says the drug
may also act on ageing. In an analysis of 42 patients
from an early-stage trial, six “proteomic clocks”, which
estimate biological age from circulating proteins, put
treated patients at a lower biological age after 12
weeks. Larger trials will take place in 2027.
Others are pursuing an approach called epigenetic

reprogramming, which aims to reverse age-related
changes to the epigenome, the chemical markers on
DNA that control which genes switch on or off. The
idea is now moving into human trials. NewLimit, a
Silicon Valley startup, plans to begin clinical trials in
2027 of an epigenetic-reprogramming therapy
intended to reverse cellular ageing in the liver. Life
Biosciences, a rival startup co-founded by David
Sinclair of Harvard University, dosed its ﬁrst patient in

June in a safety trial of a therapy that partly
reprogrammes cells in the eye. It is testing its
treatment against glaucoma and a sudden form of
optic-nerve damage.
After a ﬂurry of interest in longevity drugs came to
nothing 15 years ago, investors and drugmakers had
long shunned the ﬁeld as having little scientiﬁc
credibility. The success of GLP-1 drugs has helped
change that calculation and, somewhat aptly, given the
ﬁeld a new lease of life. NewLimit raised $435m in

funding in June. Eli Lilly, an industry giant, has
announced a partnership with Insilico Medicine worth
up to $2.75bn. Novartis, another giant, has teamed up
with BioAge Labs, a startup focusing on age-related
diseases, in a deal worth up to $550m.
Drugmakers have a further reason to pursue this
new market. Iqvia, a data provider, estimates that
branded drugs worth $230bn in annual sales will face
competition from generic or biosimilar versions by

2030 as patents expire. Treating ageing, once
dismissed as a fringe pursuit, may well be the
industry’s best shot at replacing that revenue. ●
A ﬂurry of
interest in
longevity drugs
came to nothing
15 years ago
T
HE IMMINENT arrival of psychedelic
medicines marks a profound shift in
the treatment of mental-health conditions.
A synthetic form of psilocybin is expected
to be approved soon for treatment-resis-
tant depression and to arrive in America in

early 2027. Compass Pathways, a British
biotech ﬁrm, is making the drug. Its arrival
will offer a new way to treat psychiatric
conditions in America, and encourage
other countries to loosen restrictions on
the medical use of these drugs.
Proponents hope psychedelics might
treat a range of conditions including post-
traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), opioid
and alcohol addiction, obsessive-com-
pulsive disorder and end-of-life distress.
Eli Lilly’s move into psychedelics in 2026

means trials are likely to be funded for
many disorders. A ﬁeld once dominated by
scrappy startups is now thought to have
strong commercial potential. Another ﬁrm,
Delphinium, will seek approval in 2027 to
use LSD for anxiety and depression.
These drugs are very different from
anything regulators have approved before.
But in 2026 President Donald Trump, with
the head of the Food and Drug Administra-
tion standing by his side, said getting them
onto the market was a priority.

Research into using psychedelics as
adjuncts to psychotherapy, involving
thousands of patients, ﬂourished between
the 1940s and 1960s. There was hope of
using them to treat alcoholism, addiction,
PTSD and mood disorders. It all came to an
end after a moral panic over recreational
use and 1960s counterculture. This led to
stricter laws that made research extremely
difficult. The ﬁeld stagnated for decades.
Now times have changed. Under the
new approach, approved psychedelics will

be given in closely supervised treatment
sessions, with support for patients before-
hand and afterwards, to help them through
what can be a confusing experience.
The theory is that these drugs create a
temporary form of ﬂexibility—neuroplas-
ticity—in the brain that allows new ways of
thinking to displace older, unhelpful ones.
Some argue this is equivalent to years of
therapy in one treatment, though patients
still need psychiatric support. And despite

these drugs’ great promise, they do not
work for everyone. For people who have
tried everything else, such failure can be
terribly distressing.
Other worries abound. Approval of
these drugs may encourage people to try to
get hold of them illicitly for recreational
purposes. At the very least, unsupervised
use can result in a bad trip, which can
worsen mental distress. In those at risk of
psychosis or schizophrenia, psychedelics
can trigger psychotic episodes (trials

exclude such patients). Another risk is
“ontological shock”—a destabilising frac-
turing of a person’s worldview that can
cause long-term distress.
Yet these drugs will also save lives.
Their wider availability will, however,
create philosophical problems. When does
sadness become a treatable condition?
Many people do not go to their doctor for
low mood, as antidepressants can take
weeks to work. A one-off trip might offer
an appealing ﬁx for the ups and downs of

life, but where should the line be drawn?
Should doctors, for example, prescribe
psychedelics to assuage grief over the loss
of a loved one? In 2027, the world will have
a new tool to treat the human condition.
The challenge will be to use it wisely. ●
Taking a
legal trip
The legalisation of psychedelic drugs
offers potential—and pitfalls
Natasha Loder Health editor, The Economist

96 Science & technology The World Ahead 2027
N
OWHERE HAS artiﬁcial intelligence had more
impact than in the ﬁeld of programming—and
that is now feeding back into the development of AI
itself. A report in November 2026 by Metr, a
think-tank, estimated that AI capabilities were
growing roughly twice as quickly as they were a year
earlier, due to the use of AI tools. Findings like this
explain why many insiders expect 2027 to be the year
AI systems become capable of building their own

successors. If they can do so without any human
involvement, the result could be a rapid increase in
capabilities—a phenomenon known as “recursive
self-improvement” (RSI).
AI systems are getting steadily better at coding. At
the beginning of 2026, Claude Code, Anthropic’s
coding agent, was starting to become popular, but
most users still kept it on a tight leash, approving
individual changes line by line, and tightly specifying
what it should do. By the end of the year, however, the

most forward-looking developers were running full
“software factories”, issuing high-level orders to
swarms of coding agents and leaving them to it.
Because code either works or fails, improving a
model’s coding prowess can be automated: generate
an inﬁnite number of coding challenges, score a
system well if it passes them and poorly if it fails, and
iterate. This approach is known as “reinforcement
learning with veriﬁable rewards”. But it is not the only

technique by which AI can be used to build better AI.
Training systems in this way favours tasks with
deﬁnite, testable answers, such as coding or
mathematical proofs, over other tasks with fuzzy or
subjective answers, such as creative writing or
devising legal arguments. To improve those kinds of
capabilities, too, AI researchers are pursuing an
evolution of an older technique, called “self-play”.
Self-play underpinned early AI breakthroughs such

as AlphaZero, a board-game-playing model built by
DeepMind, Google’s AI lab. It was trained by playing
myriad games of Go against itself and learning from
its victories. In today’s more elaborate form of
self-play, AI systems are set up in environments in
which they act as both participants and judges. Two AI
models tasked with creating legal arguments, for
instance, may be put on opposite sides of a mock case,
with a third AI model given the role of judge. As with

playing Go, the system improves by competing with
itself and learning from its wins and losses.
Yet another approach to RSI involves getting AI
models to modify other models directly, by tweaking
the “weights” that govern their behaviour. In late 2026
researchers at the Massachusetts Institute of
Technology found that frontier AI models could do
this without having been trained explicitly to do so.
For now, such systems can only meaningfully improve

much simpler versions of themselves, but in future this
may offer a “native” path to RSI, the researchers say.
Whatever form it takes, RSI raises pressing issues.
Experts worry that RSI could rob humanity of the
ability to supervise the training of powerful AI models,
potentially resulting in dangerous models with aims
that are “misaligned” with those of humans. In “AI
2027”, an inﬂuential forecasting exercise from early
2025, the decision to pursue RSI was held up as the key

moment in which humanity sealed its own fate. That
decision would be made, the forecasters estimated, in
the autumn of 2027. Progress is right on track. ●

W
HEN I WAS a teenager, I
read a lot about the
world, and I was afraid. Experts
warned that the 21st century
was going to be difficult—that
population growth, climate
change, resource extraction
and other crises could cause a
collapse of the human species.
It seemed that we were in
danger of consuming ourselves
into the ground.
Then, over the subsequent
years, I saw time and time
again how technology and

science could fundamentally
alter these odds: how our
growing understanding of the
human body allowed us to
invent cures for diseases that
had killed many people; how
we ﬁgured out ways to get
more energy from the same
resources; how technologies
like solar panels became much
cheaper, much faster than
expected; and how we could do
things that were previously
unimaginable, like building
rockets that take off and then
land back on this planet.
I work in artiﬁcial

intelligence because it can help
us do more of that. We can use
this technology to bring about
a world where our children live
longer and healthier lives, with
more energy, more agency,
more food and more wealth.
That world is increasingly
within our reach, but
leveraging AI for science also
brings very real risks—and we
need to act now to seize the
beneﬁts of this new world
while avoiding the dangers.
Making the beneﬁts of AI a
reality starts with investing in

scientiﬁc infrastructure. In
2027 I believe that AI will begin
to do to science what it did to
coding and mathematics in
2026. AI systems will get
radically better at a broad
range of scientiﬁc tasks, which
will let us take on far more
ambitious projects than we can
today. This has already begun.
At Anthropic, in the past 12
months, our AI systems have
identiﬁed a potentially useful
enzyme system; helped a lab
analyse glioma epidemiology
in roughly one-tenth of the

time it usually takes; and
designed new protein binders
with a success rate well above
the industry standard.
But there is a bottleneck of
scientiﬁc capacity. UNESCO
reckons there are fewer than
10m scientists in the world. We
have too few laboratories to do
materials-science analysis, too
few biological labs to develop
and test new drugs, and too few
large scientiﬁc instruments in
space or on Earth for the
experiments we want to run.
What will happen when we

have a billion virtual scientists,
directed by 10m human ones?
The scientiﬁc supply chain
may go through the same kind
of sudden growth as the
compute supply chain. If we
start building the supply chain
now—new labs, new material
inputs—we will be better able
to take advantage of the
scientiﬁc innovations from AI.
But we need to take
precautions. For many years,
researchers and science-ﬁction
authors have predicted a future
in which AI systems drastically

increase the pace of overall
scientiﬁc and technical
progress, making things move
so quickly that it is hard for
humans to keep up. We will
soon reach that point. Just
because we can sprint ahead
beyond human limits does not
mean that we always should.
Scientiﬁc research isn’t
beneﬁcial by default. A good
example of research that must
be done carefully is biological
research, which could lead to
life-saving medicines if done
well, and catastrophic risks if

not. Scientists, governments
and DNA-synthesis ﬁrms work
together to keep biological
manufacturing safe. We will
need similar co-operation and
co-ordination to ensure the
safety of AI-led science.
Governments should invest
further in their AI security
institutes to develop more
capacity to provide third-party
testing of AI systems. Firms
should invest in third-party
auditors who can inspect their
development practices, and
especially how they test and

deploy scientiﬁc advances
internally. Today the majority
of AI development is governed
by voluntary agreements, but it
is likely we will need to make
this mandatory, with common-
sense rules for the most
powerful AI systems.
Ultimately, we will need
wisdom and restraint. Carl
Sagan once wrote that the
humans who might one day
reach other star systems would
be “a species very like us, but
with more of our strengths and
fewer of our weaknesses…more

conﬁdent, farseeing, capable,
and prudent”. To harness AI for
science, these are the qualities
we will need to embody.
But where Sagan thought
mankind had thousands of
years to achieve this—to
become a better and wiser
people—we instead have a
handful of years. I believe we
can do it, but we will only
succeed by preparing today for
the world we want tomorrow,
and by constantly ﬁnding the
wisdom to differentiate
between what we can do and
what we should do. ●

98 Science & technology The World Ahead 2027
A
FTER SUCCESSFULLY sending four astronauts
around the Moon in 2026, NASA is preparing for
the next step in its lunar programme: a less far-ﬂung,
but nevertheless complex trip to low-Earth orbit. In
2027 the four-person crew on the Artemis III mission
will check that the various technologies and
components needed for a Moon landing—sourced
from a range of contractors, including SpaceX and
Blue Origin—all work together as planned. If it goes

well, that rehearsal will set the stage for Artemis IV, a
mission to land astronauts near the lunar south pole,
which is planned for 2028.
Meanwhile, the Indian Space Research
Organisation (ISRO) plans to join the human-space-
ﬂight club in 2027 with its Gaganyaan-4 mission. It
aims to put three astronauts (sometimes called
“vyomanauts”, from the Sanskrit word for “space”) into
low Earth orbit for 48 hours. But the much-delayed
launch has several hurdles ahead, namely the launches

of the ﬁrst three (uncrewed) Gaganyaan missions.
These will test the critical components of the mission
architecture (such as rockets and crew capsules)
before any humans are put in harm’s way. One of these
missions will feature a humanoid robot, Vyommitra.
ISRO also hopes to have the ﬁrst module of a space
station, called Bharatiya Antariksh, in orbit by 2028.
Elsewhere in the solar system, NASA’s Lucy
spacecraft will reach its ﬁrst target of investigation, a

Trojan asteroid near Jupiter, in August 2027, after a
six-year journey. Trojans are thought to be remnants of
the very earliest era of the solar system and have never
been studied up close. Lucy will investigate seven of
them over the course of more than a decade.
The European Space Agency plans to launch
PLATO, its ﬂagship planet-hunting probe, in the ﬁrst
half of 2027, on an Ariane 6 rocket. With 26 digital
cameras, it will look for Earth-like planets around

Sun-like stars by looking for tiny dips in their
brightness as planets transit in front of them.
Also observing the heavens in the coming year will
be China’s space telescope, known as Xuntian. With a
two-metre primary mirror, it will be smaller than
NASA’s Hubble space telescope, launched in 1990, but
Chinese officials insist it will be better in other ways: it
has a more sensitive digital camera and a bigger ﬁeld
of view. Xuntian will co-orbit Earth with China’s

Tiangong space station. The country’s Tianwen-2
probe, meanwhile, will ﬂy past Earth late in 2027 to
drop off a sample from Kamo'oalewa, a near-Earth
asteroid that it visited in the summer of 2026. The
spacecraft will then head for a rendezvous with comet
311P, in the asteroid belt, in 2035.
China’s space industry is making rapid strides on
launch hardware. LandSpace, a rocketry company, will
be aiming for a big milestone in early 2027 when it
attempts to reuse its Zhuque-3 rocket. The company

recovered the ﬁrst stage of this rocket after a test ﬂight
in August 2026, during which its second stage reached
orbit. LandSpace thus joined SpaceX and Blue Origin,
both American ﬁrms, as the only private companies to
make reusable rockets that can reach orbit. But the
Zhuque-3 is just one of several reusable rockets under
development in China. Space Pioneer, another startup,
has the Tianlong-3. And China Aerospace Science and
Technology Corporation, a state-owned company, is

working on a version of its Long March rocket with a
reusable booster.
The leader of the international rocketry pack,
SpaceX, will continue its march into space with further
tests of its gigantic Starship rocket and the ﬁrst test
launches of its AI1 satellites, a constellation of which
will form an orbital data centre known as “Starmind”.
Each AI1 satellite will carry 72 of Nvidia’s most
advanced AI chips and travel in a special orbit that
keeps it in sunlight 98% of the time. Starship will also

launch more of SpaceX’s third-generation Starlink
communications satellites.
Blue Origin aims to begin launches for its satellite
constellation, called TeraWave, in late 2027. More than
5,000 satellites will eventually be put into low- and
medium-Earth orbit to provide global internet access.
And Russia is building its own constellation, called
Rassvet. Space is about to get much more crowded. ●
Up, up and away

on in the coming year

EFENDING AGAINST a barrage of
low-cost drones, missiles and glide
bombs is possible—but it costs a fortune.
In the ﬁrst 100 hours after Operation Epic
Fury began against Iran on February 28th
2026, America ﬁred interceptors worth an
estimated $1.7bn. But shooting down every
$50,000 Shahed drone with a $5m Patriot
interceptor missile is not economically
sustainable. In Ukraine, meanwhile, such
interceptors are in short supply as the

country fends off Russian aerial attacks.
But in 2027 the economics of air
defence will become less grievous.
Cheaper systems will enter production or
service. Others will be made in greater
numbers, bringing down costs.
Accelerating test-and-redesign cycles is
one way to save money. Latvia has let
Frankenburg Technologies, an Estonian
interceptor startup, conduct around 100
test launches a year at its Adazi military
range. That helped Frankenburg get its

Mark 1 counter-drone missile “from
PowerPoint to the demonstration of a
guided catastrophic hit” in just 13 months,
says Kusti Salm, the ﬁrm’s boss. At ranges

↑ Taking aim at the drones
W
HEN A SPACEX Falcon 9 rocket
launches Haven-1 in 2027, it will be
the ﬁrst privately built and operated space
station. It is the brainchild of Jed McCaleb,

a tech entrepreneur who set up a ﬁrm
called Vast Space in 2021 to build it. The
station is an aluminium cylinder, covered
in shielding to stop it being punctured by
micrometeorites and space debris. It is 10.1
metres long and 4.4 metres in diameter,
with a pressurised volume of 80 cubic
metres, a docking port at one end for the
SpaceX taxi service that will carry people
to and from it, and an observation window
at the other. It can accommodate up to four

people. Once it is in orbit, four missions are
planned, each lasting two weeks.
The price of a stay is not yet public
information, but Vast hopes to attract
government customers (it has already
agreed to host two French astronauts) and
private ﬁrms interested in microgravity
research. The real prize, though, will be the
knowledge needed to build—starting in
2028—a multi-module, permanently
crewed version, Haven-2. This, the ﬁrm
hopes, may be chosen by NASA, America’s

space agency, as a private-sector
alternative to its lodgings on board the
ageing International Space Station (ISS),
which is due to be scrapped in the 2030s.
Vast is not alone in such hopes. Three
other space-station startups, also vying for
NASA’s custom, aim to go straight to the
permanently crewed stage. The most
advanced are those proposed by Axiom
Space (like Vast, founded for the purpose
of building such a station) and Starlab (a
collaboration between Voyager

Technologies, an American ﬁrm, and
Airbus, a European one).
Axiom’s offering, Hab-1, the habitable
part of which is due to launch in 2029, will
start as an appendage of the ISS. It will later
detach to form the nucleus of a larger
structure, Axiom Station, which will be
assembled, like Haven-2, from multiple
modules. Starlab, also pencilled in for
2029, will be a single cylinder, like Haven-1.
But it will have ﬁve times the pressurised
volume, and will require SpaceX’s next-

generation Starship to carry it into orbit.
Residents of these stations will enjoy all
mod cons. Axiom hired Philippe Starck, a
French designer, to help with the lighting
on Hab-1. Starlab has plumped for Journey,
a design agency, and Hilton, a hotel chain,
to make things nice and cosy.
Trailing the ﬁeld is Orbital Reef, a
design based on inﬂatable modules
proposed by Sierra Space, a ﬁrm in Denver
that is also working on a spaceplane, and
Blue Origin, Jeff Bezos’s space company. It

would be launched on Blue Origin’s New
Glenn heavy-lift rocket.
As for the ISS, its proposed fate is a
controlled splashdown at Point Nemo, the
spot in the Paciﬁc Ocean farthest from
human habitation. But the ISS has outlived
three previous attempts to kill it off, and
may well do so again. ●

Would you

R
ESOLUTIONS TO GET ﬁt
in the new year are noth-
ing new, but expect a new
twist in 2027. Exoskeletons—
powered rigs that attach to
the body and use electric
motors to assist movement—
are getting better and cheap-
er, and more consumers will
strap them on. Devices for the
hips and ankles went on sale
in 2026, and a $4,999 pair of
powered knee modules
branded MO/GO will begin

shipping in 2027. Such kit—
sold as “powered clothing” or
“movewear”—can also help
people get (and stay) in shape.
At ﬁrst blush, offloading
effort to a machine seems an
odd way to exercise. Consider
the trial of “robotic shorts”
reported in Nature Machine
Intelligence in 2024. For ten
older participants who
walked 400 metres on level
ground, the device cut energy
expenditure by 10% on aver-
age. For 12 younger adults on a
500-metre uphill trail, it fell by

nearly 18%. Participants adapt-
ed within a few steps, says
Lorenzo Masia, a co-author
who now runs a biorobotics lab
at the Technical University of
Munich. The best users cut
their energy burn by a quarter.
Such assistance, though,
need not mean less exercise
overall. By supporting a weak
or painful joint or muscle group
holding the rest of the body
back, exoskeletons help people
“unlock more of their own
potential”, says Conor Walsh,
head of the Harvard Biodesign

Lab. Some users will tackle
trails they would otherwise
avoid, perhaps alongside a
ﬁtter friend. Others may go
sightseeing on foot (in China,
exoskeletons can be rented for
hiking on the Great Wall) or
run an errand without the car.
Dr Walsh expects a net gain in
physical activity, judging by a
related technology: e-bikes.
In a study carried out in
Germany, 101 participants were
asked to use an ordinary bicy-
cle and an electrically assisted
one for a fortnight each, in

random order. On the e-bike,
they spent 76% more time
cycling. This more than made
up for the reduced exertion:
participants expended 48%
more energy cycling with
electrical help than without it.
Exoskeletons will probably
follow suit, says Daniel Ferris,
of the University of Florida’s
Centre for Engineering Human
Performance & Wellness.
For many older people, fear
of a fall limits walking. Re-
searchers are developing exo-
skeletons that can detect and

prevent falls by swiftly moving
a leg outwards. Researchers
will, however, need to establish
how an exoskeleton reroutes
forces, potentially overloading
unassisted joints or muscles.
One such trial, at Georgia
Tech, will begin in 2027. But
there is already evidence that
the body can adapt to, and
beneﬁt from, localised assis-
tance, as a paper in Scientiﬁc
Reports showed in 2023.
Researchers in Seoul
randomly assigned 60 older
people to six weeks of walk-

ing exercises, either with or
without a hip exoskeleton.
Three months on, those who
had worn the rig were using,
on average, 23% less energy to
walk than before. As ankle
muscles weaken with age,
work shifts to the hips, which
are less efficient. The exoskel-
eton appeared to restore some
of the workload to the ankle
muscles, strengthening them.
In many ways, then, artiﬁcial
muscles can bolster real ones.
Powered exoskeletons can
help get people moving more

correspondent, The Economist
elsewhere in Europe, one or two tests a
year can be the norm. Increased
production should bring the Mark 1’s price
below $30,000 by the end of 2027.
Comparable interceptors can cost
$250,000, or double that in wartime.
Ukraine has taken rapid iteration even
further, using feedback from the front.
Take the Sting, a quadcopter interceptor
introduced in 2025. It has been destroying
roughly 2,500 Shahed and Geran drones a

month, with a success rate higher than
90%, says an executive at Wild Hornets, its
manufacturer. The Sting sells for about
$2,000, with some conﬁgurations costing
half that. In-house production of many
parts helps, as does scale: more than 10,000
are built a month. A faster variant, the
Sting 2, will be in mass production by 2027.
Then there is cheaper propulsion.
Instead of a gas turbine, the Kestrel, a new
interceptor from Greenjets, a British
startup, uses ducted electric fans with

fewer moving parts. Built to kill Russia’s
fastest Geran drones, it can exceed
600kph. Anmol Manohar, the ﬁrm’s boss,
calls it “jet performance at electric costs”. A
Kestrel costs less than $50,000, compared
with $125,000 for a similarly capable
Coyote Block 2C, a jet-powered interceptor
made by America’s RTX. Greenjets has
been turning out 1,500 Kestrels a month.
Mr Manohar expects to quadruple output
by early 2027, knocking a third off the cost.

Cheaper still is gunﬁre, made
increasingly viable by advances in robotics.
In July, Allen Control Systems, an Austin-
based startup, began shipping Bullfrog, a
robotic machinegun mount that uses
cameras and a laser rangeﬁnder—at a tenth
of the cost of radar—to aim at drones. The
ﬁrm expects to ship more than 2,000 units
in 2027, including to Ukraine.
Weapons that ﬁre bursts of energy, not
bullets, are also becoming practical, at
least against drones. In 2027 a British navy

destroyer will get DragonFire, a laser gun.
Each shot costs about $13. But it is no use
against cruise and ballistic missiles, where
something like a Patriot is still needed.
Lockheed Martin, maker of the Patriot
system, is developing a cheaper form of its
interceptor. The aim is to deliver 90% of
the capability at half the price. Fire Point, a
Ukrainian startup, is working on an even
cheaper option, at less than $1.2m apiece. It
aims to test-ﬁre it in mid-2027. ●

I
N OCTOBER,OPENAI
released more than 700
AI-generated papers solving
major open problems in
mathematics, many of which
are so technical and densely
written that no human claims
to understand them. To verify
such solutions, we use
specialised software tools, such
as Lean, which can certify that
a complex mathematical
argument is valid. But in
August, an AI model found a
security ﬂaw in Lean, which
could be exploited to certify

that any mathematical
statement was proven, whether
or not it really was. The ﬂaw
was swiftly patched. But it
shows that we are in a new
reality: even the most trusted
veriﬁcation tools might be
gamed in future by a
sufficiently capable AI.
In mathematics, this has
limited real-world impact, as
many of the puzzles we study
do not have immediate
practical implications. But if
this situation is replicated in
engineering or in the sciences,
we could lose expert human

understanding of complex
real-world systems, and the
mathematics used to model
them, just as we are turning
over the operation of such
systems to autonomous, and
potentially misaligned, AI
agents. This is a dangerous
combination of risks.
The world runs on complex
mathematical models.
Computational ﬂuid-dynamics
models forecast weather and
climate; power-grid models
balance supply and demand;
actuarial models set insurance
rates But models can fail to

track reality accurately, due to
omissions or errors in the data
fed into them, or limitations in
their assumptions. These risks
are magniﬁed when AI agents
are incentivised to push models
beyond safe usage parameters.
The advent of powerful AI
models, and their incipient
deployment in critical real-
world infrastructure, magniﬁes
these risks still further. Modern
AI is infamous for “reward
hacking”—ﬁnding loopholes or
shortcuts to achieve a given

goal—as well as other types of
“misalignment”, when the
goals it pursues do not fully
match the intention of the
human prompting it. The
safety protocols we have
traditionally relied upon to
protect against catastrophe
could meet their match in a
powerful AI focused on using
whatever means it can to fulﬁl
its objectives. Even such gold
standards as a phase III clinical
trial might not be immune to
AI-powered reward hacking.
The emerging ﬁeld of AI

safety can provide ways to
reduce or mitigate potential
harms: by isolating AI agents in
“sandboxes”, say, or requiring
independent audits of their
outputs. But one of the
strongest defences against
misalignment is robust
supervision of AI agents by
human experts with a deep
understanding of the agent’s
strengths and weaknesses, the
mathematical models it is
using, or the domain in which
the task resides—and who look
for signs of misalignment
before approving any outputs.

It is therefore highly
concerning that, when faced
with the trade-off between
speed and performance on one
hand, and safety and human
understanding on the other,
many individuals and ﬁrms
now promoting the use of AI in
real-world applications and in
the sciences seem willing to
sacriﬁce the latter in the hopes
of making further gains on the
former. As recently as 2025, AI
labs often collaborated with
external academic experts to
explore how their tools could

augment human-directed
research. But now many of the
results generated are proudly
touted as being nearly fully
autonomous, with no human
expert involved in supervising
the AI or in reviewing its
outputs. Indeed, there is often
no researcher available from
these labs who can present and
explain the results, even to
other experts in the ﬁeld.
Fortunately, AI can be used
to bolster safety and under-
standing, rather than under-
mine it. Experts can use AI

tools to stress-test models for
vulnerabilities, or perform
analyses of competing models.
AI-assisted decision-making
can be performed in
collaboration between human
experts and AI agents, with the
human understanding of the
conclusions reached keeping
pace with the speed of output.
Such a human-centred
approach offers the best of
both worlds: a responsible path
to realising the remarkable
potential of these tools, while
keeping them aligned with

genuinely beneﬁcial societal
goals. As we enter an era where
AI misalignment could affect
the internet and the physical
world in increasingly negative
ways, the role and value of
human understanding—in
academia and education, in AI
labs, in regulation and in the
deployment of AI tools—is
more important than ever. And
the evaluation and mitigation
of misalignment risks urgently
needs to become a larger part
of the AI conversation. ●
When AI’s performance outpaces understanding and safety

The role and value of
human understanding is
more important than ever
By Invitation

risks, says Terence Tao, professor of mathematics at UCLA

ISNEY IS ﬁRING up its marketing hyperdrive for
one of its biggest movie-making gambles in years.
Half a century after the ﬁrst Star Wars ﬁlm, the studio
is attempting to reboot one of its most prized
franchises in 2027. That original hit from 1977, now
known as “A New Hope”, is getting a 50th-anniversary
re-release in February, letting fans marvel at the Death
Star on IMAX screens. Then in May will come
“Starﬁghter”, a new Star Wars ﬁlm with new characters

and, in Ryan Gosling, a new leading man.
Star Wars is not the only mega-franchise getting a
makeover. In 2026 several venerable movie properties
returned, including the 31-year-old Toy Story (with its
ﬁfth feature ﬁlm) and 30-year-old Scream (with its
seventh). More are on the way. In 2027 there will be
new cinematic instalments of franchises including
Spider-Man, the Avengers and the Lord of the Rings.
Amazon, meanwhile, has started work on a 26th

outing for James Bond, a 65-year-old ﬁlm series that it
acquired with its takeover of Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer in
2022. (Who will play 007 is due to be announced by the
end of 2026.) Batman will return—again—probably in
2028. A sixth Pirates of the Caribbean movie is in
development. And, more than 40 years after Arnold
Schwarzenegger ﬁrst vowed that he would be back,
James Cameron, a star director, has made it known that
he is toying with a seventh outing for the Terminator.

Despite—or perhaps because of—the mania for
sequels and reboots, audiences seem to be tiring of
them. Several misses in 2026 have rung alarm bells.
“Minions and Monsters” earned half as much as most
of the previous ﬁlms in the Despicable Me series. A
limp live-action remake of “Moana” lost Disney
millions. “Supergirl” ﬂopped. “Spider-Man: Brand
New Day” was a big hit of the year, taking $2.5bn. But
even that fell well short of Marvel’s glory days:

“Avengers: Endgame”, the high point in 2019, took
more than $3.6bn in today’s terms. Franchise
instalments are being turned out faster than ever, but
with diminishing returns (see chart on next page).
If audiences are cooling, why do the reboots keep
coming? One reason is that a well-timed revival can
still work. “The Devil Wears Prada 2” took advantage

▸ of millennial nostalgia to earn even more than the ﬁrst
ﬁlm did in 2006. In “Toy Story 5” the villain was a tablet
computer, tapping into parents’ contemporary
concerns about screen time.
Streaming and video games have made it easier to
keep franchises alive between movie instalments.
James Bond fans, frustrated at the wait for a new ﬁlm,
have been able to watch “007: Road to a Million”, a
gameshow launched by Amazon in 2023, and play “007

First Light”, a game released in 2026. And artiﬁcial
intelligence has made it easier to keep characters alive.
Disney de-aged an octogenarian Harrison Ford for its
latest Indiana Jones ﬁlm and cloned the voice of Darth
Vader, played by James Earl Jones, who died in 2024.
(Yoda, at a mere 900 years old, may soon be one of the
studio’s younger stars.)
The main reason for the glut of franchises, however,
is risk aversion. Cinema-going is in long-term decline,

with global box-office receipts in 2026 forecast to be
more than 40% below pre-pandemic levels, in
inﬂation-adjusted terms. Staying at home has never
been more tempting, with vast streaming libraries on
services like Netﬂix, and an inﬁnity of user-generated
video on platforms like YouTube and TikTok. The
average American now goes to the cinema only about
twice a year. Studios calculate that people are more
likely to turn out for a big name than a novel idea.

Producers seem to recognise that the diet of
reboots is getting monotonous. Sony called “The
Social Reckoning” a “companion piece” rather than a
sequel to “The Social Network” from 2010. Disney
labels its re-dos of old ﬁlms “re-imaginings”. Some
ﬁlm-makers are knowingly making fun of the franchise
phenomenon: the recent “Scary Movie”, released 26
years after the original, was billed as a “rebooquel”; the
forthcoming sequel to the 40-year-old “Spaceballs” is

jokingly subtitled: “The New One”.
Will the high-stakes reboot of Star Wars be a
success? Fans reliably show up for new chapters in the
beloved space saga. Yet cinema-going trends suggest
that its best days at the box office are behind it. Each
new franchise instalment seems to generate less
excitement than the last. Disney executives, weighing
up the chances of a smash hit, may feel like quoting
Han Solo: “Never tell me the odds!” ●

I
T WAS UNDENIABLY one of
the Hollywood triumphs of
2026—and it did not obey any
of the usual rules for success.

“Backrooms” was the work
not of a legendary director
with a string of hits to their
name, but the feature-ﬁlm
debut of 20-year-old Kane
Parsons. It was made with a
small budget (around $10m)
and did not feature any huge
stars. And its source material
was not a comic book or
cherished classic novel, but a
horror series, based on a bit of
internet lore, and it was re-
leased on YouTube. Yet “Back-
rooms” went on to pull in
$400m worldwide, making it

one of the highest-grossing
independent ﬁlms of all time.
According to Parrot Ana-
lytics, a data ﬁrm, ﬁlms and
TV shows based on YouTube
and social-media content
made up less than 1% of the
adaptations that reached
screens in 2025. But it is a
growing trend. There were
over four times as many shows
and ﬁlms based on this kind
of intellectual property in
2025 than there were in 2017.
More such projects will go
into production in 2027 as
Hollywood turns to YouTube

for new ideas and talent.
Horror will continue to be
a focal point. Mr Parsons and
others have proved that you
can turn a creepy, low-budget
web series into a creepy,
low-budget movie. Curry
Barker, the ﬁlm-maker behind
“Obsession”, another mega-
smash of 2026, also started his
career putting sketches and
videos online.
Expect a raft of spooky
stories based on viral internet
content to start ﬁlming in
2027. Dylan Clark’s short
chiller ﬂick, “Portrait of God”,

has racked up 10m views on
YouTube since its release in
2022. He is in the process of
developing it for the big
screen. Other titles, including
“Siren Head” (about a mon-
ster that looks like a tele-
phone pole), “Cartoon Cat”
(about a massive, aggressive
feline) and “The Mandela
Catalogue” (about supernat-
ural beings that psycholog-
ically torture their victims),
have recently prompted
bidding wars among studios.
Reality TV will be another
area of real interest. Big-name

YouTubers such as MrBeast
and Sidemen have already
successfully adapted their
digital brands into popular
unscripted shows. The Stokes
Twins—who have almost
200m followers—are next.
They will host a new series for
Netﬂix in 2027 based on their
“signature dynamic hijinks”,
such as “Eating 100 Years of
School Lunches!”.
YouTube is an appealing
source of intellectual proper-
ty because it highlights ideas
and talent that already reson-
ate with audiences. Yet it is

YouTube executives who are
least happy about the trend.
Having watched franchises
like “Cocomelon” and stars
such as Ms Rachel (an educa-
tional YouTuber) expand onto
Netﬂix, the video giant is
scrambling to keep its biggest
players onside. It is reportedly
offering creators up to $10m
not to post elsewhere. The
ﬁght for the next generation
of Hollywood hitmakers will
be worth watching.

I
T IS NOT surprising that the main
repositories of countries’ culture would
come under attack in the culture wars, and
from both sides. From the left, protesters
have accused museums of proﬁting from
colonialism. Several institutions have, as a
result, returned artefacts to the countries
from which they were taken. From the right
have come complaints of museums “going
woke”. In 2025 the Trump administration
released a report that accused the

Smithsonian’s American-history museum
of “thinly veiled anti-Americanism”. All
this has added to the existing ﬁnancial
troubles of museums in the West, which
face government-funding cuts and
pressure to become self-sufficient.
Institutions reopening in 2027 have
responded in different ways.
The year’s biggest reopening will be the
Pergamon Museum complex on Berlin’s
Museum Island, which has been closed
since 2023. The opening will be partial: the

section of its classical-antiquities
collection containing Roman items and
parts of its Ancient Near-East museum will
not open for another decade. But
highlights from both will once again
welcome visitors from June 4th.
So will its sizeable Islamic-art museum.
There are around 7m Muslims now living in
Germany, 8.5% of the population. “We
think these cultures connect,” explains
Stefan Weber, the museum’s director.
“They follow each other. They are not

separate satellites.” He has invited
immigrants to work at the museum. “We
can’t train them to be historians, but we
can train them how to do tours.”
Its bigger innovation may lie in design.
The museum used to be organised by
dynasties and geographies. “If you know
them, ﬁne,” says Mr Weber. “But if you
don’t know who the Abbasids and other
dynasties are, you’re lost.” In the new
layout, four “orientation rooms” with
wall-sized globes and maps will show how

European and Muslim civilisations met
and inﬂuenced each other.
Interconnectedness also guided the
redesign of the Gilcrease Museum in Tulsa,
Revamping
the galleries
New and reopening museums reckon
with the past decade’s cultural shifts
Jon Fasman Senior culture correspondent,
The Economist
Oklahoma, which features one of the
world’s largest collections of art from the
American West, and is due to reopen in
early 2027. The museum “had been very
segregated”, explains Brian Whisenhunt,

its head. Documents, rare books,
indigenous art and Western American art
all had their own sections. In the
renovation, “the team pulled out the most
compelling stories…regardless of where
they are within the disciplines.”
This brought the museum closer to its
original mission. Thomas Gilcrease, the
Native American oilman who founded the
museum, “wanted to show the art, history
and culture of the Americas”, says Mr
Whisenhunt. “He was trying to tell a

bigger story and history.”
A similar drive to recontextualise a
familiar story spurred a renowned
Norwegian museum in Oslo to change its
name from the Viking Ship Museum to the
Museum of the Viking Age. The old
museum used to focus “on the graves and
the ships”, explains its director, Aud
Valborg Tonnessen. The new one, opening
in November 2027, tells “the whole story of
the Viking age, not just the people in those
graves”, she says. It will explore social roles,

gender and ethnicity as well as the
introduction of a monetary economy and
the building of a nation.
One thing all these renovations have in
common is a hefty price tag. The
Pergamon revamp will cost $1.7bn. Around
one-third of the $141m cost of the
Gilcrease’s new building came from private
funds. And Norwegian foundations and
private donors gave generously to the
Viking museum. American private donors
have long given to museums; Europeans
less so. “To seek [funding] for a museum in

the amount we’ve done is new for us,” says
Ms Tonnessen. “We need to raise our
competence in this ﬁeld.” ●

HE EYES of the Lord are in every
place,” declares the biblical book of
Proverbs. And, in every place, people’s eyes
are on the Lord. Bible sales have been
climbing heavenwards since 2019 in
America and Britain. On social media,
believers across the world share videos of
themselves praying, studying scripture or
describing how they came to faith. (More

than 20m posts are tagged #Christian on
TikTok.) Films and TV shows, such as “The
King of Kings”, “Mary”, “David” and
“House of David”, have dramatised stories
from the holy book.
Christian content will continue its
ascension in 2027. Mel Gibson proved that
religious ﬁlms could still be blockbusters:
his blood-spattered cruciﬁxion ﬁlm, “The
Passion of the Christ”, was the ﬁfth-
highest-grossing title in 2004. In 2027 Mr
Gibson returns with “The Resurrection of

the Christ: Part One”. (The second
instalment will arrive in 2028, proving that
Jesus may have conquered death, but even
he cannot avoid sequels.)
Those who prefer their adaptations of
scripture to be faithful to the source
material may ﬁnd themselves in disbelief.
Mr Gibson has said that the ﬁlm will ﬁll
some of the gaps in the Gospels; audiences
should expect some “crazy stuff”.
Mr Gibson is not the only one telling
the story of the cross. “The Chosen”, a hit

TV series dramatising Jesus’s life, will
spawn a feature ﬁlm in 2027 focused on the
cruciﬁxion, exploring “how Jesus’s ﬁnal
hours changed the world for ever”. The
movie will be released in cinemas
worldwide and will no doubt draw a large
ﬂock—to date, more than 300m people in
175 countries have watched the show.

The eight-episode series will explore how

brothers and sold into slavery, before rising
to great power in Egypt.
There will be factual content, too. A
forthcoming four-part documentary
promises to “expand and sharpen our
understanding of one the most inﬂuential
people in history”. The series, which was

commissioned by the BBC but will be
distributed globally, combines “cinematic
storytelling” with “the latest historical
research, archaeological discoveries,
technological innovation and expert
testimony” to examine Jesus Christ’s life
and times, and how his following grew.
“Unbound”, another documentary
series, will tell the story of how the word of
God reached believers across the world.
The transmission of the Bible, the
documentary’s makers have said, is a story

of “intrigue, courage, power, suppression,
battles, greed, mercenaries, merchants,
entrepreneurs, smugglers and much more”.
The demand for this kind of content
has probably been a revelation to
commissioners. Google searches for
“Christian ﬁlm” and “Christian TV show”
rose by 30% and 40% in the past ﬁve years
compared with the preceding half-decade.
Religious media brought in $700m for
streaming platforms from 2020 to 2025
according to Parrot Analytics, a data ﬁrm,

which says it “has transitioned from a
niche segment to a major revenue driver”.
Demand is increasing globally from Brazil
to South Africa, suggesting that, in 2027
and beyond, studios and streamers are
likely to keep the faith. ●
Africa rising
“C
HILDREN OF BLOOD
ANDBONE” is one of
the most anticipated ﬁlms of
2027. It is an adaptation of a
fantasy bestseller of the same
name by Tomi Adeyemi, a
Nigerian-American author,
set in a ﬁctional Yoruba king-

dom. The ﬁlm was shot across
Africa and features an all-
black cast with South African
and Nigerian leads and a
soundtrack put together by
Tems, a Grammy-winning
Nigerian singer. When re-
leased in January, it is likely to
be a blockbuster.
After years of trying, ﬁlms
and TV series from and about
Africa are ﬁnally attracting the
world’s attention. Recent
successes such as “My Father’s
Shadow”, a BAFTA-winning
ﬁlm, and “The Polygamist”, a
hit Netﬂix series, have proved

that stories with an African
focus and starring local talent
can resonate with international
audiences. Subtitles and unfa-
miliar accents are not the barri-
ers they once were. Producers
and distributors are making
bigger bets on African talent,
spurring impressive interconti-
nental collaborations. Major
releases planned for 2027 will
maintain the momentum, as
will more fundamental changes
behind the scenes.
The perception of African
ﬁlm has long been dictated by

the outsize inﬂuence of Nolly-
wood—Nigeria’s proliﬁc,
low-budget, ﬁlm industry. The
new landscape is more diverse.
Film-makers in lesser-known
markets, from Ivory Coast to
Rwanda, are partnering with
Western distributors and
generating buzz at internation-
al ﬁlm festivals. South Africa is
emerging as a hub. Years of
investment by the state-owned
broadcaster, SABC, and Mul-
tiChoice, a regional media
giant with headquarters there,
are paying off.

As Hollywood remains
saturated with remakes and
sequels, Western production
houses are looking for fresh
ideas. So are distributors.
NEON, the ﬁrm that introduced
the world to “Parasite”, a South
Korean movie that went on to
win four Oscars, will push
“Clarissa”—a star-studded,
low-budget retelling of Virgin-
ia Woolf’s “Mrs Dalloway”, set
in Nigeria—to global theatres
in 2027, after an exceptional
run at foreign festivals.
Making ﬁlms and TV
shows in Africa can also be

cheap. By combining world-
class post-production studios
with picturesque scenery,
South Africa has shown it can
do everything, at a lower cost
than Hollywood. The $3bn
acquisition of MultiChoice by
Canal+, a French giant, will
provide more funding. Such
competitiveness is one reason
why South Africa is now the
only African country where
Netﬂix commissions original
series and ﬁlms.
African diasporas will
continue to be an audience for
the ﬁnished product. With

Africa’s cinemas producing
only 3% of global box-office
revenues, ﬁlms made on the
continent recoup most of
their money elsewhere. Pro-
ducers will not mind that. In
ﬁlm, as in music, fashion and
other art forms, says Katleho
Ramaphakela, a South
African ﬁlm-maker, “the
world’s eyes are on Africa as a
pop-cultural leader.”

106 The World Ahead 2027
H
ER NAME was glamorous; her
appearance, less so. Parked on the
runway, the U-2 Dragon Lady looked like a
ﬂoundering albatross with the nose of a
camel. Coming in to land, she would ﬂop
on her belly, sometimes with a thud, while
a chase car raced to steady her gigantic
wings. In the air, though, she was a beauty,
and she ascended dramatically, taking off
at an angle of 35 degrees and reaching
5,000 feet (1,500 metres) within 20

seconds. That was merely the beginning,
for her ceiling was 70,000 feet, in the lower
stratosphere, at the edge of space. She
glided, for she was a glider at heart, along
the wide curvature of the Earth.
From there, she spied. With the
panoramic camera in her belly and the
antennae that poked out of her she could
record in 1958 the number of bombers
parked at Soviet airﬁelds and activity
around rocket sites. In 1961 she could
count the number of Soviet missiles

massing in Cuba. Over the world’s
persistent trouble spots, the Middle East
and the Koreas, she ﬂew repeatedly, taking
note of anything untoward that America’s
enemies were doing. Satellites could
record these things too, but only when
their orbits allowed. She could spy day and
night and on demand, as required, and for
decades she glided too high for any other
aircraft or missile to touch her.
Her birth, at Lockheed’s Skunk Works
in California in 1955, had been secret. At

ﬁrst she was simply an “article”, not a
plane. Her creator, Kelly Johnson, wanted
something like a single-seater sailing boat,
with no landing gear and a bicycle
conﬁguration, but with a jet engine for
propulsion. She could be light because she
carried no weapons. The CIA gave her the
letter U for “utility”; deﬁnitely not R for
reconnaissance. There was already a U-1
aircraft, so she was named U-2. Her
nickname came from a mix of a 1930s
comic strip and a ﬁlm about the daughter

of a famous Chinese villain, Dr Fu
Manchu. It suggested an unpredictable
woman of mysterious allure and cunning:
just what you might expect if you crossed
an internal combustion engine with what
was, in aeronautical terms, a feather.
Her alibi was weather-monitoring. In
1987, ﬂying over Antarctica, she brought
back conclusive proof of the damage
being done to the ozone layer. But climate
research began as a cover story—one that
was blown in 1960 when a U-2 crashed in

Russia and her pilot, Gary Powers,
admitted under pressure that he had
indeed been spying. After various
upgrades the Lady’s unmistakeable
silhouette, which had not changed,
appeared in the 1990s over Iraq and even
in 2026, above Iran.
Nonetheless, as aircraft went, she was
very old indeed. Her death-date was set
for 2018, then 2023, then 2025. In 2020 the
air force tried to replace her with the
uncrewed RQ-4 Global Hawk, but she was
so much cheaper per ﬂight-hour ($2,380

against $6,710) that the Hawks were
scrapped. Instead, the Lady had a $50m
full-body reﬁt of new electro-optical
reconnaissance systems, cameras and
communications gear. She had seen her
rivals off, and in style.
Yet the air force has again requested no
funding for her in ﬁscal 2027. The thought
of such an oldster in their shiny ﬂeet
continued to annoy them. She was no
longer untrackable on radar, and other
arguments against her were growing.
First, satellites could now orbit in

chains to provide continuous surveillance.
Second, from the 1980s Lockheed had
supplied NASA with ER-2 Earth Resources
aircraft which, though they could not ﬂy
quite as high as the Lady, could still do
good science in the lower stratosphere.
And third, in the era of drones and AI, and
with human eyes not doing the spying
anyway, why should men (and a few
women) still do a job for which, in the
1960s, they were given suicide pills?
The Dragon Lady, beﬁtting her name,

was a handful to ﬂy. Because she was
taking her pilot almost out of this world he
had to wear a full-pressure suit, with all
the attendant fuss of a urinary collection
device and long-date tubes of baby food
sucked through his face mask. To avoid
the “bends” caused by nitrogen dissolved
in his blood, he had to breathe 100%
oxygen all the time. In the freezing
conditions above 50,000 feet the
windshield often froze over and the
navigation system could seize up. And if

the Lady came under ﬁre, her pilot had
nothing but a helmet to defend himself.
Merely cruising, even on autopilot,
could be hair-raising, too. Her do-not-
exceed speed, above which she would
break up, was a mere ﬁve knots above her
stall speed, at which she would drop out
of the sky. Pilots called this “Coffin
Corner”. Coming in to land, she had to
stall two or three feet above the runway,
so that she could then drop a minimal
distance to Earth; ground-crew then

had to seize the ends of her wings and
attach stabilisers.
The reward for the pilot was some of
the best views of Earth any human, bar an
astronaut, was likely to see. Air-force
personnel operated the spy cameras, and
NASA monitored the scientiﬁc equipment,
so he could simply gaze in awe, with no
sound but his own breathing.
One day, perhaps even in 2027, the
Pentagon’s efforts will ﬁnally ground her.
But do not be surprised if the elusive Lady
who has escaped so many obituaries

glides away once again. ●
The U-2 Dragon Lady
Obituary
Will America’s legendary spy plane ﬁnally be grounded in 2027?
Ann Wroe, Obituaries editor, The Economist
